Chapter 41

Algorithmic Services And Simulations

Orientation

A tic-tac-toe class scans the whole board after every move, and a test with a 3 by 3 board passes. A grader then sends a board of 100 000 by 100 000 cells and the same class runs out of time. The moves are all legal and the answers are all right. The cost per call is the only defect.

This chapter teaches small services whose public calls must stay fast and stay consistent. Each lesson names the authoritative state, the indexes derived from it, and the order in which one call may change them.

Bounded In-Memory Contract

Every problem here runs in one process with one caller at a time. All data lives in memory, sizes have stated limits, and a call either finishes or reports a failure value. Persistence, networking and several threads are outside the contract.

Prerequisites

You should know hash maps, tree sets, heaps, tries from earlier chapters and the indexes of chapter 40. An authoritative state is the one record that decides what is true. A derived index is a structure rebuilt from that record to answer one query fast. A precondition is a fact that must hold before a call may change anything.

What Each Lesson Protects

  • Move Pieces On A Board keeps the position, the occupancy and the counters in agreement after every accepted move.
  • Resolve Paths In A Tree gives every reachable node one parent and one normalized path.
  • Update Values That Depend On Others keeps cached values equal to a fresh evaluation.
  • Merge Feeds From Many Sources applies visibility before the output limit.
  • Apply Transactions Without Partial Changes leaves the object unchanged when any check fails.
  • Answer Prefix Queries With A Trie keeps a number on every prefix of every stored key.
  • Track Status And Pick The Next Task lets the status table overrule every queue entry.
  • Rank Words By Prefix keeps a short ranked list at every prefix node.
  • Keep Several Indexes In Step sends every change through a single writer.

Reading Order

Each lesson opens with a class that passes small tests and breaks under a real workload, and you predict the break before the explanation. Two traces follow, then Java code and a section on false friends. A false friend is a design that resembles the pattern and violates one of its conditions. Four exercises follow in the order build, vary, edge case and recognition. The last two lessons combine the earlier ones.

What You Can Do After This Chapter

You can state which structure is authoritative before you write a method. You can order the checks and writes of a call so that a failure changes nothing. You can pick a trie, a heap or a status table by the query that the class must answer.

Move Pieces On A Board

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Win Check That Rereads The Board

A server hosts tic-tac-toe on a 1000 by 1000 board, and a player wins by filling one row, column or diagonal with their marks. After every move the server rereads the board to decide whether the game ended. Each move makes it read about four million cells, and almost none of them changed since the last move.

A snake game has the same shape. The code must reject a move into the snake's own body, so it searches a list of body cells for the new head. That search takes time proportional to the snake's length, and the game slows down as the player scores.

Both games ask the same question. How can a call decide whether a move wins or fails by looking only at what the move changed? This lesson answers it for a board of marks, a snake on a grid and a robot that walks along the edge of a rectangle.

Simple approach

Scanning Every Line After Each Move

The direct design stores the owner of each cell in a grid. After placing a mark it scans every row, every column and both diagonals for each player.

final class ScanBoard {
    private final int n;
    private final int[][] grid;                               // 0 is empty, otherwise the player id

    ScanBoard(int n) { this.n = n; this.grid = new int[n][n]; }

    int move(int row, int col, int player) {
        if (grid[row][col] != 0) return -1;                   // a taken cell is illegal
        grid[row][col] = player;
        for (int p = 1; p <= 2; p++) {                        // test both players
            boolean diag = true, anti = true;
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                boolean rowFull = true, colFull = true;
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {                 // two reads for every inner step
                    if (grid[i][j] != p) rowFull = false;
                    if (grid[j][i] != p) colFull = false;
                }
                if (rowFull || colFull) return p;
                if (grid[i][i] != p) diag = false;
                if (grid[i][n - 1 - i] != p) anti = false;
            }
            if (diag || anti) return p;
        }
        return 0;                                             // no line is full yet
    }
}

The answers are correct. The cost is the problem.

Predict first: The board is 1000 by 1000 and a player places one mark. About how many cells does the scan read, and how many of those cells could change the answer?

The two nested loops read two cells per inner step for two players, which is about 4 * 1000 * 1000 = four million reads. Only the marked cell's row, its column and the two diagonals can change the answer, which is at most 4000 cells. A line that does not contain the new mark has exactly the same cells as before the move.

Bottleneck

Rereading Cells That Did Not Change

The scan costs O(n^2) per move on an n by n board, while a single move changes one cell. Every line that misses the new cell holds the same marks it held one call earlier, so the scan repeats work whose answer is already known. A million moves on the 1000 by 1000 board read four trillion cells in total.

The snake search has the same defect. It costs O(L) per move for a snake of length L, because it compares the new head against every body cell. The body changes by one cell at each end per move, and the search ignores that.

A third cost is correctness. A move changes several things at once, such as the head, the tail, the score and the collision answer. If the code writes some of them and then discovers an illegal move, the board is left half changed. The order of the checks and the writes matters as much as their speed.

Key idea

Count Per Line And Track Taken Cells

Keep one authoritative representation of the position, and derive every other view from it inside the same call. A derived view answers the question that the naive scan answered by rereading, and it changes by a small amount per move.

Counting Marks Per Line

A counter is an integer kept for one line of the board. An n by n board has n rows, n columns and 2 diagonals, so it has 2n + 2 counters. Player 1 adds 1 to a counter and player 2 adds -1. Each cell contributes exactly 1 or -1, so a line holds n marks of one player exactly when its counter has absolute value n.

A move at (row, col) changes at most four counters: the row, the column, the main diagonal when row equals col, and the anti-diagonal when row plus col equals n - 1. The win check reads those four values and costs O(1).

Tracking Taken Cells In Order

The snake needs two views. The body in order, from head to tail, is authoritative because order decides which cell is released next. A deque holds it. The occupancy set is a set of the body cells, and it is derived from the deque. It answers "is this cell taken" in O(1).

The order of updates carries the rule. A move that does not eat releases the tail before the head enters, so the head may enter the cell that the tail leaves. A move that eats keeps the tail. The call therefore checks first, using the occupancy set and the tail cell, and it writes nothing until the move is known to be legal.

Reducing Steps Around A Loop

The robot visits only the cells on the edge of a rectangle, and those cells form a loop. The cycle length is the number of cells on that loop, which is 2 * (width + height) - 4. After that many steps the robot stands on the same cell again.

One position index along the loop is therefore enough, and a call with num steps adds num modulo the cycle length to it. The cell follows from the index. The direction follows from the index except at index 0, where it depends on whether the robot has ever moved.

The invariant is that the authoritative position and every derived view agree after each accepted move, and a rejected move changes nothing. The target is O(1) per call for the board, the snake and the robot.

State

State Behind Each Move

The contract is bounded and held in memory. One object owns one board, calls arrive one at a time, and a rejected call leaves every field unchanged.

  • Owner grid is the authoritative record of which player marked each cell.
  • Line counters are the derived signed sums for rows, columns and the two diagonals.
  • Body deque is the authoritative snake, stored from head to tail.
  • Occupancy set is the derived set of cells covered by the body deque.
  • Loop index is the authoritative position of the robot along the edge, counted from the start corner.
  • Moved flag records whether the robot has taken at least one step, because direction at index 0 depends on it.
Walkthrough

Following Moves On A Board

Counting Marks In A Small Game

The first trace plays tic-tac-toe on a 3 by 3 board. The pointer call sits on the latest move, and each step shows the sums of the three rows, the three columns and the two diagonals. Player 1 adds 1 and player 2 adds -1.

The second call targets the cell that player 1 already holds, so it returns -1 and every sum stays as it was. The fifth call leaves the main diagonal sum at -1, because player 2 owns two corners and player 1 owns the center. The sixth call completes column 1 and wins, and the seventh call returns -1 because the game is over.

A Snake That Follows Its Tail

The second trace moves a snake on a 3 by 3 grid with three pieces of food. Each step lists the body from tail to head, where a cell is written as its row digit and then its column digit, and it shows the score.

The fourth call moves the head onto the cell that the tail holds. The move does not eat, so the tail leaves first and the move is legal. The fifth call aims at a cell held by a middle body segment, so it ends the game and writes nothing. The sixth call returns -1 because the game is over.

Code

Moves That Check Before Writing

The first class keeps one signed counter per line and decides the winner from at most four of them.

final class LineCounts {
    private final int n;
    private final int[] line;                                 // rows, then columns, then main and anti diagonal
    private final boolean[] used;                             // authoritative: which cells hold a mark
    private boolean over;

    LineCounts(int n) { this.n = n; this.line = new int[2 * n + 2]; this.used = new boolean[n * n]; }

    int move(int r, int c, int player) {
        if (over || r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= n || c >= n || used[r * n + c]) return -1;   // validate before any write
        used[r * n + c] = true;
        int d = player == 1 ? 1 : -1;                         // the sign of this player
        line[r] += d;
        line[n + c] += d;
        if (r == c) line[2 * n] += d;
        if (r + c == n - 1) line[2 * n + 1] += d;
        if (Math.abs(line[r]) == n || Math.abs(line[n + c]) == n
                || Math.abs(line[2 * n]) == n || Math.abs(line[2 * n + 1]) == n) {
            over = true;                                      // a full line ends the game
            return player;
        }
        return 0;
    }
}

The second class moves a snake. It reads the tail cell to judge the move, and it changes the deque and the occupancy set only after the judgment.

final class SnakeMove {
    private final int rows, cols;
    private final Deque<Integer> body = new ArrayDeque<>();   // head first, tail last
    private final Set<Integer> occupancySet = new HashSet<>();
    private boolean over;

    SnakeMove(int rows, int cols) {
        this.rows = rows; this.cols = cols;
        body.addFirst(0); occupancySet.add(0);
    }

    /** Returns false when the move ends the game; eats says whether the new cell holds food. */
    boolean advance(int dr, int dc, boolean eats) {
        if (over) return false;
        int head = body.peekFirst();
        int r = head / cols + dr, c = head % cols + dc;
        if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= rows || c >= cols) { over = true; return false; }
        int cell = r * cols + c;
        int tail = body.peekLast();
        boolean blocked = occupancySet.contains(cell) && (eats || cell != tail);   // the tail cell is free unless the snake grows
        if (blocked) { over = true; return false; }
        if (!eats) { body.pollLast(); occupancySet.remove(tail); }                 // release the tail first
        body.addFirst(cell);
        occupancySet.add(cell);                                                    // the head enters last
        return true;
    }
}

Each call costs O(1) expected time. The snake class stores the body twice, so its space is O(L) for a snake of length L.

When to use it

Commands That Change Positions

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is a stream of commands that change a position or the set of taken cells, where each command passes a fixed sequence of legality checks. Write down which structure is authoritative and which structures are derived from it. The invariant is that the two agree after every accepted command and that a rejected command writes nothing.

Ordering The Checks And Writes

Run every check that can fail first, using only reads. Then apply the writes in an order that matches the rules of the game, such as releasing the tail before placing the head. A check that depends on a pending write, like the tail cell, reads the old value directly and never edits the structure to find out.

False Friends

Removing the tail from the occupancy set before checking the head looks natural. It is a false friend, because a failed move then leaves the tail missing from the set. Taking the remainder of the step count by the board size is another false friend for the robot, because the robot walks the edge loop and not a line of that length. A sorted index over layer counts needs care too. Remove the old key before the count changes, because a key that changes while it sits in a TreeSet breaks the ordering. Skip the derived views when the board is tiny and called rarely, since a direct scan is simpler and fast enough.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Design Tic-Tac-Toe

LeetCode 348

Problem Specification. Design a class Board for an n by n grid that starts empty and is played by players 1 and 2. The call move(row, col, player) places a mark for the player. A move is illegal when the cell is outside the grid, the cell already holds a mark, or an earlier move already completed a line. An illegal move returns -1 and changes nothing. A legal move that completes a full row, column or main diagonal or anti-diagonal of one player's marks returns that player. Any other legal move returns 0. Turn order is not enforced. The call filled() returns the number of marks placed.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Size satisfies 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Players are the integers 1 and 2.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and coordinates may lie outside the grid.
  • Return is an int.

Example 1. Input n = 3, calls move(0,0,1), move(0,0,2), move(1,1,1), move(2,2,1), move(0,1,2), filled(), output 0, -1, 0, 1, -1, 3.

Example 2. Input n = 2, calls move(0,0,1), move(2,0,1), move(1,1,2), move(0,1,2), filled(), output 0, -1, 0, 2, 3.

Prerequisites. Two-dimensional indexing and signed sums.

What This Exercise Changes. The call validates the cell and the game state before it touches any sum, so a rejected move cannot leave a partial update.

Hint

Which four sums can one mark change, and when is the grid itself needed?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. An owner array of bytes is the authoritative record of the marks. Four derived values summarize it: a signed sum for each row, a signed sum for each column, and one sum for each diagonal. Player 1 adds 1 and player 2 adds -1, so a line holds n marks of one player exactly when its sum has absolute value n.

A move first validates the game state, the coordinates and the cell, and it returns -1 without a write when any check fails. Only then does it store the owner, raise the mark count and update the at most four sums that contain the cell. The winner field is set once and blocks every later move.

The invariant is that each sum equals the signed number of marks on its line, and a rejected move writes none of the fields.

Complexity.

  • Time per call is O(1), since a move touches at most four sums.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the owner array plus O(n) for the sums.
import java.util.*;

public final class TicTacToeSolution {
    static final class Board {
        private final int n;
        private final byte[] owner;                                // authoritative marks
        private final int[] rowSum, colSum;                         // derived sums per row and column
        private int diagSum, antiSum, marks, winner;

        Board(int n) {
            this.n = n;
            this.owner = new byte[n * n];
            this.rowSum = new int[n];
            this.colSum = new int[n];
        }

        /**
         * Places a mark or rejects the move.
         * Time: O(1).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: every check runs before the first write.
         */
        int move(int row, int col, int player) {
            // A finished game rejects every move.
            if (winner != 0) return -1;
            // Coordinates outside the grid are illegal.
            if (row < 0 || col < 0 || row >= n || col >= n) return -1;
            // A taken cell is illegal.
            if (owner[row * n + col] != 0) return -1;
            int d = player == 1 ? 1 : -1;
            // All checks passed, so the writes below cannot be partial.
            owner[row * n + col] = (byte) player;
            marks++;
            rowSum[row] += d;
            colSum[col] += d;
            if (row == col) diagSum += d;
            if (row + col == n - 1) antiSum += d;
            // Only the four sums that contain the cell can have reached n in absolute value.
            if (Math.abs(rowSum[row]) == n || Math.abs(colSum[col]) == n
                    || (row == col && Math.abs(diagSum) == n)
                    || (row + col == n - 1 && Math.abs(antiSum) == n)) winner = player;
            return winner;
        }

        int filled() { return marks; }
    }

    /** Brute force: stores the grid and scans every line after each legal move. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final int n; final int[][] g; int marks; boolean over;
        Oracle(int n) { this.n = n; g = new int[n][n]; }
        int move(int r, int c, int p) {
            if (over || r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= n || c >= n || g[r][c] != 0) return -1;
            g[r][c] = p; marks++;
            for (int q = 1; q <= 2; q++) {
                boolean d = true, a = true;
                for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                    boolean rf = true, cf = true;
                    for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) { if (g[i][j] != q) rf = false; if (g[j][i] != q) cf = false; }
                    if (rf || cf) { over = true; return q; }
                    if (g[i][i] != q) d = false;
                    if (g[i][n - 1 - i] != q) a = false;
                }
                if (d || a) { over = true; return q; }
            }
            return 0;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Board b = new Board(3);
        int[][] e1 = {{0, 0, 1, 0}, {0, 0, 2, -1}, {1, 1, 1, 0}, {2, 2, 1, 1}, {0, 1, 2, -1}};
        for (int[] m : e1) if (b.move(m[0], m[1], m[2]) != m[3]) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (b.filled() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1 filled");
        // Example 2 includes a coordinate outside the grid.
        b = new Board(2);
        int[][] e2 = {{0, 0, 1, 0}, {2, 0, 1, -1}, {1, 1, 2, 0}, {0, 1, 2, 2}};
        for (int[] m : e2) if (b.move(m[0], m[1], m[2]) != m[3]) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        if (b.filled() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 2 filled");
        // Random games, including out-of-range and repeated cells, against the scanning oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(348);
        for (int round = 0; round < 2000; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            Board s = new Board(n);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(n);
            for (int op = 0; op < 30; op++) {
                int r = rnd.nextInt(n + 2) - 1, c = rnd.nextInt(n + 2) - 1, p = 1 + rnd.nextInt(2);
                if (s.move(r, c, p) != o.move(r, c, p)) throw new AssertionError("move mismatch");
                if (s.filled() != o.marks) throw new AssertionError("filled mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Design Snake Game

LeetCode 353

Problem Specification. Design a class Snake for a grid of height rows and width columns. The snake starts with length 1 at row 0, column 0. The array food lists cells, and only the first uneaten cell is visible at any time. The call move(direction) takes U, D, L or R and moves the head one cell. It returns the number of food cells eaten so far, or -1 if the game is over. The game ends when the head leaves the grid or enters a cell held by the body after the move. A move onto the visible food grows the snake by one cell, and the tail stays. Any other move releases the tail before the head enters. After the game ends, every call returns -1 and changes nothing.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Grid satisfies 1 <= height, width <= 100.
  • Food has at most 50 distinct cells inside the grid.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.
  • Return is an int.

Example 1. Input width 3, height 3, food [[0,1],[1,1],[1,0]], calls R, D, L, U, D, output 1, 2, 3, 3, -1.

Example 2. Input width 2, height 2, food [[0,1],[1,1],[1,0]], calls R, D, L, U, R, output 1, 2, 3, 3, 3.

Prerequisites. The tic-tac-toe exercise above, deques and hash sets.

What This Exercise Changes. The occupancy set must drop the tail before the head enters, so the call judges the move first and then updates the deque and the set in that order.

Hint

Which cell is free for a move that does not eat, and what must stay unchanged if the move is illegal?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The body deque holds the cells from head to tail and is the authoritative state. A bit set indexed by row * width + col is derived from it and answers whether a cell is taken. The call also keeps the number of eaten food cells, which is both the score and the index of the visible food.

A move computes the new head cell and rejects it when it leaves the grid. It then decides whether the cell holds the visible food. A cell in the occupancy set blocks the move, except that the tail cell does not block a move that does not eat, because that move releases the tail before the head enters. When the move is blocked the game is over and the deque and the set stay as they were. Otherwise a non-eating move removes the tail from both structures, and every legal move adds the head to both.

The invariant is that the occupancy set holds exactly the cells of the body deque after each accepted move, and a rejected move writes nothing except the game-over flag.

Complexity.

  • Time per call is O(1), since a move runs a fixed number of deque and bit set operations.
  • Space is O(width * height + F) for the bit set and the F food cells, plus O(L) for a body of length L.
import java.util.*;

public final class SnakeSolution {
    static final class Snake {
        private final int width, height;
        private final int[][] food;
        private final ArrayDeque<Integer> body = new ArrayDeque<>();   // head first, tail last
        private final BitSet occupancySet = new BitSet();              // derived from the deque
        private int eaten;
        private boolean over;

        Snake(int width, int height, int[][] food) {
            this.width = width; this.height = height; this.food = food;
            body.addFirst(0);
            occupancySet.set(0);
        }

        /**
         * Moves the head one cell and returns the score, or -1 when the game is over.
         * Time: O(1).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: occupancySet equals the cells of body after each accepted move.
         */
        int move(char direction) {
            // A finished game rejects every move.
            if (over) return -1;
            int head = body.peekFirst();
            int r = head / width, c = head % width;
            switch (direction) {
                case 'U' -> r--;
                case 'D' -> r++;
                case 'L' -> c--;
                default -> c++;
            }
            // Leaving the grid ends the game before any write.
            if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= height || c >= width) { over = true; return -1; }
            int cell = r * width + c;
            boolean eats = eaten < food.length && food[eaten][0] == r && food[eaten][1] == c;
            int tail = body.peekLast();
            // The tail cell is free for a move that does not eat, so only other body cells block.
            if (occupancySet.get(cell) && (eats || cell != tail)) { over = true; return -1; }
            if (eats) {
                // Growth keeps the tail and reveals the next food.
                eaten++;
            } else {
                // The tail leaves both structures before the head enters.
                body.pollLast();
                occupancySet.clear(tail);
            }
            body.addFirst(cell);
            occupancySet.set(cell);
            return eaten;
        }

        boolean consistent() {
            BitSet check = new BitSet();
            for (int cell : body) check.set(cell);
            return check.equals(occupancySet);
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: a list of cells, tail removed first, then a linear search for the head. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final int w, h; final int[][] food; final ArrayList<int[]> body = new ArrayList<>(); int eaten; boolean over;
        Oracle(int w, int h, int[][] food) { this.w = w; this.h = h; this.food = food; body.add(new int[] {0, 0}); }
        int move(char d) {
            if (over) return -1;
            int[] hd = body.get(body.size() - 1);
            int r = hd[0] + (d == 'U' ? -1 : d == 'D' ? 1 : 0), c = hd[1] + (d == 'L' ? -1 : d == 'R' ? 1 : 0);
            if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= h || c >= w) { over = true; return -1; }
            boolean eat = eaten < food.length && food[eaten][0] == r && food[eaten][1] == c;
            if (!eat) body.remove(0);
            for (int[] b : body) if (b[0] == r && b[1] == c) { over = true; return -1; }
            body.add(new int[] {r, c});
            if (eat) eaten++;
            return eaten;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the last move hits a middle segment.
        Snake s = new Snake(3, 3, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 1}, {1, 0}});
        int[] want1 = {1, 2, 3, 3, -1};
        String m1 = "RDLUD";
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) if (s.move(m1.charAt(i)) != want1[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 at " + i);
        // Example 2: the head keeps entering the cell the tail just left.
        s = new Snake(2, 2, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 1}, {1, 0}});
        int[] want2 = {1, 2, 3, 3, 3};
        String m2 = "RDLUR";
        for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) if (s.move(m2.charAt(i)) != want2[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 2 at " + i);
        // Random grids, food lists and moves against the list-based oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(353);
        String dirs = "UDLR";
        for (int round = 0; round < 3000; round++) {
            int w = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5), h = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            List<Integer> cells = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < w * h; i++) cells.add(i);
            Collections.shuffle(cells, rnd);
            int fc = rnd.nextInt(Math.min(8, w * h) + 1);
            int[][] food = new int[fc][];
            for (int i = 0; i < fc; i++) food[i] = new int[] {cells.get(i) / w, cells.get(i) % w};
            Snake a = new Snake(w, h, food);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(w, h, food);
            for (int op = 0; op < 40; op++) {
                char d = dirs.charAt(rnd.nextInt(4));
                if (a.move(d) != o.move(d)) throw new AssertionError("move mismatch");
                if (!a.consistent()) throw new AssertionError("occupancy set drift");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Walking Robot Simulation II

LeetCode 2069

Problem Specification. Design a class Robot for a grid of width columns and height rows. The robot starts at (0, 0) facing East. The call step(num) repeats num times: if the cell ahead lies outside the grid, the robot first turns 90 degrees counterclockwise, from East to North to West to South to East, and then it moves one cell forward. The call getPos() returns [x, y]. The call getDir() returns East, North, West or South.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Grid satisfies 2 <= width, height <= 100.
  • Steps satisfy 0 <= num <= 10^9, and step(0) changes nothing.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.
  • Return of getPos is an int array of length 2.

Example 1. Input width 6, height 3, calls step(2), step(2), getPos(), getDir(), step(2), step(1), step(4), getPos(), getDir(), output [4,0], East, [1,2], West.

Example 2. Input width 2, height 2, calls getDir(), step(0), getDir(), step(4), getPos(), getDir(), output East, East, [0,0], South.

Prerequisites. Modular arithmetic and the two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The step count is reduced by the cycle length, and the start corner needs a flag, because the same cell has two directions.

Hint

What is the direction at the start corner before any step and after one full loop?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The robot visits only the cells on the edge of the rectangle, and those cells form a loop of 2 * (width + height) - 4 cells. The solution stores one loop index, numbered from the start corner along the bottom edge, and one flag that records whether any step happened. A call to step(num) adds num modulo the cycle length to the index, and it sets the flag only when num is positive.

The position follows from the index by four ranges, one for each side. The direction follows from the same ranges: East on the bottom side, North on the right side, West on the top side and South on the left side. Index 0 is special. The robot faces East there before any step, and South once it has taken a step, because it arrives at the start corner by walking down the left side.

The invariant is that the loop index equals the total number of steps modulo the cycle length, so the position and the direction match a step-by-step simulation.

Complexity.

  • Time per call is O(1), since the update is one addition and one remainder.
  • Space is O(1), because the state is the index, the flag and the grid size.
import java.util.*;

public final class RobotSolution {
    static final class Robot {
        private final int w, h, cycle;
        private int index;
        private boolean moved;

        Robot(int width, int height) {
            this.w = width; this.h = height;
            this.cycle = 2 * (width + height) - 4;
        }

        /**
         * Advances the robot along the edge loop.
         * Time: O(1).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: index equals the step total modulo the cycle length.
         */
        void step(int num) {
            // Zero steps change nothing, including the moved flag.
            if (num == 0) return;
            moved = true;
            // The long sum prevents overflow for num near 10^9.
            index = (int) (((long) index + num) % cycle);
        }

        int[] getPos() {
            // Bottom side, indices 0 to w - 1.
            if (index < w) return new int[] {index, 0};
            // Right side, indices w to w + h - 2.
            if (index < w + h - 1) return new int[] {w - 1, index - (w - 1)};
            // Top side, indices w + h - 1 to 2w + h - 3.
            if (index < 2 * w + h - 2) return new int[] {w - 1 - (index - (w + h - 2)), h - 1};
            // Left side, the remaining indices.
            return new int[] {0, h - 1 - (index - (2 * w + h - 3))};
        }

        String getDir() {
            // The start corner has two directions, chosen by the moved flag.
            if (index == 0) return moved ? "South" : "East";
            if (index < w) return "East";
            if (index < w + h - 1) return "North";
            if (index < 2 * w + h - 2) return "West";
            return "South";
        }
    }

    /** Brute force: one cell at a time with the turn rule from the problem text. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final int w, h; int x, y, d;
        static final int[] DX = {1, 0, -1, 0}, DY = {0, 1, 0, -1};
        static final String[] NAME = {"East", "North", "West", "South"};
        Oracle(int w, int h) { this.w = w; this.h = h; }
        void step(int num) {
            for (int i = 0; i < num; i++) {
                int nx = x + DX[d], ny = y + DY[d];
                if (nx < 0 || ny < 0 || nx >= w || ny >= h) { d = (d + 1) % 4; nx = x + DX[d]; ny = y + DY[d]; }
                x = nx; y = ny;
            }
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Robot r = new Robot(6, 3);
        r.step(2); r.step(2);
        if (!Arrays.equals(r.getPos(), new int[] {4, 0}) || !r.getDir().equals("East")) throw new AssertionError("example 1a");
        r.step(2); r.step(1); r.step(4);
        if (!Arrays.equals(r.getPos(), new int[] {1, 2}) || !r.getDir().equals("West")) throw new AssertionError("example 1b");
        // Example 2: the start corner before any step, after step(0) and after one full loop.
        r = new Robot(2, 2);
        if (!r.getDir().equals("East")) throw new AssertionError("example 2a");
        r.step(0);
        if (!r.getDir().equals("East")) throw new AssertionError("example 2b");
        r.step(4);
        if (!Arrays.equals(r.getPos(), new int[] {0, 0}) || !r.getDir().equals("South")) throw new AssertionError("example 2c");
        // A very large step count must match the loop arithmetic, checked by reducing to the cycle by hand.
        Robot big = new Robot(5, 4), small = new Robot(5, 4);
        big.step(1_000_000_000);
        small.step((int) (1_000_000_000L % 14));
        if (!Arrays.equals(big.getPos(), small.getPos()) || !big.getDir().equals(small.getDir())) throw new AssertionError("large step");
        // Random sizes and step sequences against the step-by-step oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2069);
        for (int round = 0; round < 3000; round++) {
            int w = 2 + rnd.nextInt(6), h = 2 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            Robot a = new Robot(w, h);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(w, h);
            for (int op = 0; op < 20; op++) {
                int num = rnd.nextInt(4) == 0 ? 0 : rnd.nextInt(3 * (w + h));
                a.step(num); o.step(num);
                int[] p = a.getPos();
                if (p[0] != o.x || p[1] != o.y) throw new AssertionError("position mismatch");
                if (!a.getDir().equals(Oracle.NAME[o.d])) throw new AssertionError("direction mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Layered Counts In A Cube

LeetCode 3391

Problem Specification. The rules below are written from the problem title, and the LeetCode statement may differ in detail. Design a class Cube for an n by n by n binary matrix that starts with every cell 0. The layer x is the set of n * n cells with first coordinate x. The call setCell(x, y, z) sets the cell to 1 and returns true if the value changed, or false otherwise. The call unsetCell(x, y, z) sets the cell to 0 and returns true if the value changed, or false otherwise. The call largestLayer() returns the layer with the most ones, and it returns the largest x among tied layers.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Size satisfies 1 <= n <= 100.
  • Coordinates are integers from 0 to n - 1.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Return types are boolean for the two updates and int for largestLayer.

Example 1. Input n = 3, calls largestLayer(), setCell(0,0,0), largestLayer(), setCell(0,0,0), setCell(2,1,1), largestLayer(), unsetCell(2,1,1), largestLayer(), unsetCell(0,1,1), output 2, true, 0, false, true, 2, true, 0, false.

Example 2. Input n = 2, calls setCell(1,0,0), setCell(1,1,1), setCell(0,0,0), largestLayer(), unsetCell(1,0,0), unsetCell(1,1,1), largestLayer(), unsetCell(0,0,0), largestLayer(), output true, true, true, 1, true, true, 0, true, 1.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above and tree sets.

What This Exercise Changes. Each layer has an ordered key made from its count, so a repeated set or unset call returns before touching the index, and a real change removes the old key first.

Hint

What must happen to a layer's key in the ordered set before its count changes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A flat boolean array of n^3 cells is the authoritative matrix. A count array holds the number of ones in each layer, and it is derived from the matrix. An ordered set holds one key for each layer, built from the count and the layer index, so the last key names the layer with the most ones and, among equal counts, the largest index.

An update first reads the cell. If the value already matches the request, the call returns false and writes nothing. Otherwise it removes the layer's current key from the ordered set, changes the cell and the count, and inserts the new key. The remove must come first, because the key is derived from the count and a key that changes while it sits in the set breaks the ordering.

The invariant is that the ordered set holds exactly one key for each layer and that key matches the layer's current count.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per update, because it does one removal and one insertion in a tree set of n keys, and largestLayer is O(log n) for reading the last key.
  • Space is O(n^3) for the matrix plus O(n) for the counts and keys.
import java.util.*;

public final class LayersSolution {
    static final class Cube {
        private final int n;
        private final boolean[] cell;                              // authoritative matrix
        private final int[] ones;                                  // derived count per layer
        private final TreeSet<Long> ranking = new TreeSet<>();     // derived keys: count and layer index

        Cube(int n) {
            this.n = n;
            this.cell = new boolean[n * n * n];
            this.ones = new int[n];
            for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) ranking.add(key(0, x));
        }

        private static long key(int count, int x) { return ((long) count << 8) | x; }

        // Moves one layer to a new count; the old key leaves the set before the count changes.
        private void shift(int x, int delta) {
            ranking.remove(key(ones[x], x));
            ones[x] += delta;
            ranking.add(key(ones[x], x));
        }

        /**
         * Sets one cell to 1.
         * Time: O(log n).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a repeated call returns before the index is touched.
         */
        boolean setCell(int x, int y, int z) {
            int i = (x * n + y) * n + z;
            // The value already matches, so nothing changes.
            if (cell[i]) return false;
            cell[i] = true;
            shift(x, 1);
            return true;
        }

        /** Sets one cell to 0 with the same checks; O(log n) time and O(1) space. */
        boolean unsetCell(int x, int y, int z) {
            int i = (x * n + y) * n + z;
            if (!cell[i]) return false;
            cell[i] = false;
            shift(x, -1);
            return true;
        }

        /** Reads the last key; O(log n) time; the largest key has the most ones and then the largest index. */
        int largestLayer() { return (int) (ranking.last() & 255); }

        boolean consistent() {
            int[] c = new int[n];
            for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) for (int k = 0; k < n * n; k++) if (cell[x * n * n + k]) c[x]++;
            TreeSet<Long> want = new TreeSet<>();
            for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) want.add(key(c[x], x));
            return Arrays.equals(c, ones) && want.equals(ranking);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Cube c = new Cube(3);
        if (c.largestLayer() != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 0");
        if (!c.setCell(0, 0, 0) || c.largestLayer() != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 1");
        if (c.setCell(0, 0, 0)) throw new AssertionError("e1 repeated set");
        if (!c.setCell(2, 1, 1) || c.largestLayer() != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 tie");
        if (!c.unsetCell(2, 1, 1) || c.largestLayer() != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 unset");
        if (c.unsetCell(0, 1, 1)) throw new AssertionError("e1 unset zero");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        c = new Cube(2);
        if (!c.setCell(1, 0, 0) || !c.setCell(1, 1, 1) || !c.setCell(0, 0, 0)) throw new AssertionError("e2 set");
        if (c.largestLayer() != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 a");
        if (!c.unsetCell(1, 0, 0) || !c.unsetCell(1, 1, 1) || c.largestLayer() != 0) throw new AssertionError("e2 b");
        if (!c.unsetCell(0, 0, 0) || c.largestLayer() != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 c");
        // Random updates against a scan of a plain 3D array.
        Random rnd = new Random(3391);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            Cube s = new Cube(n);
            boolean[][][] g = new boolean[n][n][n];
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                int x = rnd.nextInt(n), y = rnd.nextInt(n), z = rnd.nextInt(n);
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (kind == 0) { boolean ch = !g[x][y][z]; g[x][y][z] = true; if (s.setCell(x, y, z) != ch) throw new AssertionError("set result"); }
                else if (kind == 1) { boolean ch = g[x][y][z]; g[x][y][z] = false; if (s.unsetCell(x, y, z) != ch) throw new AssertionError("unset result"); }
                int best = 0, bestCount = -1;
                for (int a = 0; a < n; a++) {
                    int cnt = 0;
                    for (int b = 0; b < n; b++) for (int d = 0; d < n; d++) if (g[a][b][d]) cnt++;
                    if (cnt >= bestCount) { bestCount = cnt; best = a; }
                }
                if (s.largestLayer() != best) throw new AssertionError("largest mismatch");
                if (!s.consistent()) throw new AssertionError("index drift");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Resolve Paths In A Tree

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Setting Stored Under A Missing Folder

A configuration service stores values under paths such as /app/db/host. A client writes /app/db/host before anyone has created /app/db. The store accepts the write, because it only checks that the full path is new. Now one value sits under a parent that does not exist.

Listing /app shows nothing, since no entry named db exists. Deleting /app later leaves the value behind, because nothing links it to its parent. A search finds it, and a walk from the top never reaches it.

This lesson asks how a service resolves a path so that every stored value has exactly one parent that exists, and how it keeps that true across thousands of calls. The same question appears in file systems, in routing tables and in database catalogs.

Simple approach

Storing Every Path As One String

The direct design keeps one hash map from the full path string to its value. To create a path, it cuts the string at the last slash and asks whether that prefix is in the map. To list a directory, it scans every key and keeps those that start with the directory string.

import java.util.*;

final class NaivePaths {
    private final Map<String, Integer> value = new HashMap<>();

    boolean createPath(String path, int v) {
        int cut = path.lastIndexOf('/');
        String parent = path.substring(0, cut);               // "" stands for the top
        if (!parent.isEmpty() && !value.containsKey(parent)) return false;
        return value.putIfAbsent(path, v) == null;
    }

    List<String> list(String dir) {
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (String key : value.keySet()) {                   // scans every stored path
            if (key.startsWith(dir)) out.add(key);
        }
        Collections.sort(out);
        return out;
    }
}

Creation works for clean input. The listing is wrong, and it is also slow.

Predict first: The map holds the paths /a, /ab and /a/x. What does list("/a") return, and what should it return as the children of /a?

The call returns /a, /ab and /a/x, because each string starts with the characters of "/a". Only /a/x is a child of /a. The path /ab is a sibling, and /a is the directory itself. A string prefix is not a parent relation, and the scan also touches every stored path.

Bottleneck

Strings Do Not Know Their Parents

The listing call reads all n stored paths, so one call costs O(n) even when the directory has two children. A service with 10^5 paths and 10^5 list calls does 10^10 string comparisons. The prefix test is also wrong, which makes the cost the smaller problem.

The map has no structure for the parent relation. It rediscovers the relation from string text on every call, and the text rules are easy to get wrong. A path such as //a or /a/ produces a different key than /a, so the same directory can exist twice under two spellings.

Deleting or moving a directory has the same weakness. The code would have to find every key that belongs below it by scanning, and one missed key is an orphan. The structure must store the parent relation itself, and it must read each path by one fixed rule.

Key idea

Split The Path, Then Walk A Tree

Cut each path string into components, which are the names between the separators. The path /a/b has the components a and b. The top of the tree is the root, and it has zero components.

Normalize Once At The Entry

A service must normalize each incoming string before it does anything else. Normalizing applies a stated policy that turns many spellings of one location into one list of components. A policy says whether repeated separators collapse, whether a trailing separator counts and which names are illegal. After this step no other code reads raw path text.

Each Node Owns Its Children

Store a node for every location. A node holds its value and a map from a child name to the child node. A node is reachable only through its parent, so a node cannot exist without a parent that exists. Resolving a path means starting at the root and following one map lookup per component. The cost is O(d) lookups for a path of depth d.

Ownership Gives Subtree Operations

The children of one node form its subtree together with everything below them. Listing a directory reads one map, and a sorted map yields the names in lexicographic order. Counting or removing a subtree starts at one node and never scans unrelated paths. Extra data such as a count per node is a derived value. It changes in the same call that changes the tree.

The invariant is that every stored node is reachable from the root by its list of components, and each node has exactly one parent.

State

State That A Call Reads And Writes

A path service keeps four kinds of state, and only one of them is authoritative.

  • Node is the authoritative entity, holding a value or content and a map from child name to child node.
  • Components is the list of names that the entry step produces from the raw string, and it exists only during one call.
  • Cursor is the node that the walk has reached so far, starting at the root.
  • Derived count is an optional number per node, such as the nodes below it, and it changes only in the same call as the tree.

A call first normalizes the string and then walks with the cursor. It validates every precondition during the walk and writes only after the walk has succeeded.

Walkthrough

Following Creates And Spellings

Creating Under A Parent That Must Exist

The first trace runs six calls on a small tree. The pointer call marks the latest call, and the tree column lists the stored paths with their values.

The third call names /c/d. The walk stops at the root because c is missing, so the call returns false and writes nothing. The fourth call fails for a different reason: the full path exists already.

Many Spellings, One Location

The second trace feeds raw strings through the entry step. Each step shows the list of components the policy produces, or the reason the string is rejected. The policy collapses repeated separators, ignores a trailing separator and rejects the names . and ... It also rejects a string that does not start with a separator.

The first two strings denote the same location, so they resolve to the same node. The empty list of components is the root itself, and a name with a trailing dot such as b. stays legal.

Code

Creating And Reading With A Tree

The class below stores the tree and resolves clean paths of the form /a/b. The walk to the parent is read-only, and the new node is added only after it succeeds.

import java.util.*;

final class TreeFs {
    private static final class Node {
        final Map<String, Node> kids = new HashMap<>();
        int value = -1;
    }

    private final Node root = new Node();

    boolean createPath(String path, int v) {
        String[] part = path.substring(1).split("/");          // names between separators
        Node cur = root;
        for (int i = 0; i < part.length - 1; i++) {            // O(d) lookups to reach the parent
            cur = cur.kids.get(part[i]);
            if (cur == null) return false;                     // missing parent: nothing is written
        }
        String last = part[part.length - 1];
        if (cur.kids.containsKey(last)) return false;          // the path exists already
        Node fresh = new Node();                               // the only write of the call
        fresh.value = v;
        cur.kids.put(last, fresh);
        return true;
    }

    int get(String path) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (String name : path.substring(1).split("/")) {     // one lookup per name
            cur = cur.kids.get(name);
            if (cur == null) return -1;
        }
        return cur.value;
    }
}

Each call costs O(d) map lookups plus O(L) to split a string of length L. The space is O(n) for n stored nodes.

When to use it

Names That Resolve Through Parents

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is a string made of names that identify an object, together with a rule that a parent must exist first. File paths, URL routes, configuration keys and database table names all fit. Ask what the root is, what separates the names and which spelling counts as the same location. The invariant is that each stored node is reachable from the root and has one parent.

Choosing The Node Contents

A plain directory tree needs a map per node. Use a sorted map when a call lists children in order, and a hash map when it only looks names up. A database catalog is the same tree with fixed depth: the table name is the first name, and the row identifier is the second.

False Friends

A flat map from full path to value is a false friend. It passes the creation tests and fails on listings, on subtree counts and on alternate spellings of one path. A trie over characters is another false friend, because the units here are whole names and not letters. Skip the tree when the service never lists, deletes or counts by parent, because then a flat map is enough.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Design File System

LeetCode 1166

Problem Specification. Design a class FileSystem that stores integer values at paths. A path is the character / followed by one or more names, with exactly one / between neighbouring names. The call createPath(path, value) stores the value and returns true only when the path does not exist and its parent path exists. The parent of a one-name path is the root, which always exists. Otherwise the call returns false and stores nothing. The call get(path) returns the stored value, or -1 if the path does not exist.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Path has length from 2 to 100 and contains lowercase letters and / in the form above.
  • Value is an integer from 1 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.

Example 1. Input calls createPath(/a,1), createPath(/a/b,2), get(/a/b), createPath(/c/d,3), get(/c/d), output true, true, 2, false, -1.

Example 2. Input calls createPath(/a,1), createPath(/a,5), get(/a), createPath(/a/b/c,4), get(/a/b), output true, false, 1, false, -1.

Prerequisites. Hash maps and string splitting.

What This Exercise Changes. The call walks to the parent first and adds a node only after that walk succeeds, so a failed call leaves no partial path behind.

Hint

Which node must the walk reach before any node is added?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Each node holds a value and a hash map from a child name to a child node. The root is an empty node that always exists. A create call splits the path into names and walks from the root through all names except the last. If a lookup returns null, the parent is missing and the call returns false before it writes. When the walk succeeds, the call checks that the last name is free and then adds one node.

The invariant is that every stored node is reachable from the root through its names, so no node exists without its parent. The only write of a create call comes after all checks.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L) per call for a path of length L, because splitting reads each character once and the walk does one lookup per name.
  • Space is O(N) for N stored nodes, plus O(L) for the split of one call.
import java.util.*;

public final class FileSystemSolution {
    static final class FileSystem {
        private static final class Node {
            final Map<String, Node> kids = new HashMap<>();
            int value = -1;
        }

        private final Node root = new Node();

        /**
         * Creates a path whose parent exists and whose own name is free.
         * Time: O(L) for a path of length L.
         * Space: O(L) for the split names.
         * Invariant: the node is added only after every ancestor has been found.
         */
        boolean createPath(String path, int value) {
            // Split into the names between separators.
            String[] part = path.substring(1).split("/");
            Node cur = root;
            // Walk to the parent: one lookup per name.
            for (int i = 0; i < part.length - 1; i++) {
                cur = cur.kids.get(part[i]);
                // A missing ancestor means the parent does not exist, so nothing is written.
                if (cur == null) return false;
            }
            String last = part[part.length - 1];
            // An existing name keeps its old value.
            if (cur.kids.containsKey(last)) return false;
            // The single write of the call.
            Node fresh = new Node();
            fresh.value = value;
            cur.kids.put(last, fresh);
            return true;
        }

        /** Returns the stored value or -1; O(L) time, O(L) space; invariant: read-only walk. */
        int get(String path) {
            Node cur = root;
            // One lookup per name.
            for (String name : path.substring(1).split("/")) {
                cur = cur.kids.get(name);
                if (cur == null) return -1;
            }
            return cur.value;
        }
    }

    /** Flat oracle: a map from the full path to the value, with the parent cut from the string. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final Map<String, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
        boolean createPath(String p, int v) {
            String parent = p.substring(0, p.lastIndexOf('/'));
            if (!parent.isEmpty() && !m.containsKey(parent)) return false;
            if (m.containsKey(p)) return false;
            m.put(p, v);
            return true;
        }
        int get(String p) { return m.getOrDefault(p, -1); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1.
        FileSystem f = new FileSystem();
        check(f.createPath("/a", 1), "e1 a");
        check(f.createPath("/a/b", 2), "e1 b");
        if (f.get("/a/b") != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 get");
        check(!f.createPath("/c/d", 3), "e1 missing parent");
        if (f.get("/c/d") != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 no partial path");
        if (f.get("/c") != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 no intermediate node");
        // Example 2.
        FileSystem g = new FileSystem();
        check(g.createPath("/a", 1), "e2 a");
        check(!g.createPath("/a", 5), "e2 duplicate");
        if (g.get("/a") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 old value kept");
        check(!g.createPath("/a/b/c", 4), "e2 deep");
        if (g.get("/a/b") != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2 no partial path");
        // Random calls against the flat oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(1166);
        String[] names = {"a", "b", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            FileSystem s = new FileSystem();
            Oracle o = new Oracle();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
                int depth = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
                for (int i = 0; i < depth; i++) sb.append('/').append(names[rnd.nextInt(3)]);
                String p = sb.toString();
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    int v = 1 + rnd.nextInt(100);
                    if (s.createPath(p, v) != o.createPath(p, v)) throw new AssertionError("create " + p);
                }
                if (s.get(p) != o.get(p)) throw new AssertionError("get " + p);
            }
        }
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }
}verified
Variation

Design In-Memory File System

LeetCode 588

Problem Specification. The rules below are this chapter's own wording and may differ in detail from LeetCode. Design a class Fs whose tree holds directories and files. A path is / for the root, or / followed by names with one / between neighbours. The call mkdir(path) creates the directory and every missing directory above it, and it returns true. It returns false and changes nothing if the path or any directory above it is a file. The call addContentToFile(path, text) appends the text to the file at the path, creating the file and its missing directories first. It returns false and changes nothing if the path is a directory, the root, or has a file above it. The call readContentFromFile(path) returns the content of the file, or the empty string if the path is not a file. The call ls(path) returns the names of the children of a directory in lexicographic order. For a file it returns a list holding only the file's own name, and for a missing path it returns an empty list.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Names use lowercase letters and digits, with length from 1 to 10.
  • Text has length from 1 to 20, and a file never grows beyond 10^5 characters.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.
  • Return of ls is a list of strings.

Example 1. Input calls mkdir(/a/b), addContentToFile(/a/b/f,hi), addContentToFile(/a/b/f,yo), readContentFromFile(/a/b/f), ls(/a/b), ls(/a/b/f), output true, true, true, hiyo, the list f, the list f.

Example 2. Input calls mkdir(/m), mkdir(/b), addContentToFile(/z,q), mkdir(/z/y), ls(/), ls(/z/y), output true, true, true, false, the list b, m, z, the empty list.

Prerequisites. The file system exercise above and sorted maps.

What This Exercise Changes. A node is now a directory or a file, and directories keep children in a sorted map. A read-only walk checks the kinds first, and the second pass creates missing directories.

Hint

What can the walk find at the first missing name that it cannot find before it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A node is a directory with a sorted map of children, or a file with a text buffer. The root is a directory. A helper splits a path into names, where the root path gives an empty list.

Every writing call runs two passes. The first pass only reads. It follows existing nodes and stops at the first missing name, where it accepts, or at a file in the middle of the path, where it rejects. It also rejects when the last name exists with the wrong kind. The second pass walks again and creates the missing directories, and it creates the final file for an append. Because every name below a missing node is also missing, the second pass never meets a file, so a rejected call has written nothing.

The invariant is that every node is reachable from the root, and only directories have children. Listing a directory reads the keys of one sorted map, so the order is lexicographic without sorting.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L) per path of length L for walking and creating, plus O(k) to list k children and O(T) to append T characters.
  • Space is O(N + C) for N nodes and C characters of file content.
import java.util.*;

public final class InMemoryFsSolution {
    static final class Fs {
        private static final class Node {
            final TreeMap<String, Node> kids; // null for a file
            final StringBuilder text;         // null for a directory
            Node(boolean file) {
                kids = file ? null : new TreeMap<>();
                text = file ? new StringBuilder() : null;
            }
            boolean isFile() { return text != null; }
        }

        private final Node root = new Node(false);

        private static String[] split(String path) {
            return path.equals("/") ? new String[0] : path.substring(1).split("/");
        }

        /**
         * Read-only check that the path can be created as a file or a directory.
         * Time: O(L).
         * Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: no node changes during the check.
         */
        private boolean canCreate(String[] part, boolean wantFile) {
            // The root is a directory, so it can be a mkdir target and never a file.
            if (part.length == 0) return !wantFile;
            Node cur = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < part.length; i++) {
                Node child = cur.kids.get(part[i]);
                // The first missing name ends the walk; everything below it will be new.
                if (child == null) return true;
                boolean lastName = i == part.length - 1;
                // An existing file blocks the path unless it is the final name of an append.
                if (child.isFile()) return lastName && wantFile;
                // An existing directory at the final name is fine only for mkdir.
                if (lastName) return !wantFile;
                cur = child;
            }
            return true;
        }

        /** Creates missing directories for the first count names; the walk never meets a file after canCreate. */
        private Node ensureDirs(String[] part, int count) {
            Node cur = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) {
                Node child = cur.kids.get(part[i]);
                if (child == null) { child = new Node(false); cur.kids.put(part[i], child); }
                cur = child;
            }
            return cur;
        }

        boolean mkdir(String path) {
            String[] part = split(path);
            // Validate before any write.
            if (!canCreate(part, false)) return false;
            ensureDirs(part, part.length);
            return true;
        }

        boolean addContentToFile(String path, String text) {
            String[] part = split(path);
            // Validate before any write.
            if (!canCreate(part, true)) return false;
            Node dir = ensureDirs(part, part.length - 1);
            String name = part[part.length - 1];
            Node file = dir.kids.get(name);
            if (file == null) { file = new Node(true); dir.kids.put(name, file); }
            file.text.append(text);
            return true;
        }

        String readContentFromFile(String path) {
            Node cur = root;
            for (String name : split(path)) {
                if (cur.isFile()) return "";
                cur = cur.kids.get(name);
                if (cur == null) return "";
            }
            return cur.isFile() ? cur.text.toString() : "";
        }

        List<String> ls(String path) {
            String[] part = split(path);
            Node cur = root;
            for (String name : part) {
                if (cur.isFile()) return List.of();
                cur = cur.kids.get(name);
                if (cur == null) return List.of();
            }
            // A file lists only its own name; a directory lists its sorted keys.
            if (cur.isFile()) return List.of(part[part.length - 1]);
            return new ArrayList<>(cur.kids.keySet());
        }
    }

    /** Flat oracle: sets of directory paths and a map of file paths, scanned linearly. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final Set<String> dirs = new HashSet<>(Set.of("/"));
        final Map<String, StringBuilder> files = new HashMap<>();
        static List<String> prefixes(String p, boolean includeSelf) {
            List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
            String[] part = p.equals("/") ? new String[0] : p.substring(1).split("/");
            String cur = "";
            for (int i = 0; i < part.length; i++) {
                cur += "/" + part[i];
                if (includeSelf || i < part.length - 1) out.add(cur);
            }
            return out;
        }
        boolean mkdir(String p) {
            for (String q : prefixes(p, true)) if (files.containsKey(q)) return false;
            dirs.addAll(prefixes(p, true));
            return true;
        }
        boolean add(String p, String t) {
            if (p.equals("/") || dirs.contains(p)) return false;
            for (String q : prefixes(p, false)) if (files.containsKey(q)) return false;
            dirs.addAll(prefixes(p, false));
            files.computeIfAbsent(p, k -> new StringBuilder()).append(t);
            return true;
        }
        String read(String p) { return files.containsKey(p) ? files.get(p).toString() : ""; }
        List<String> ls(String p) {
            if (files.containsKey(p)) return List.of(p.substring(p.lastIndexOf('/') + 1));
            if (!dirs.contains(p)) return List.of();
            TreeSet<String> out = new TreeSet<>();
            List<String> all = new ArrayList<>(dirs);
            all.addAll(files.keySet());
            for (String q : all) {
                if (q.equals("/")) continue;
                String parent = q.substring(0, q.lastIndexOf('/'));
                if (parent.isEmpty()) parent = "/";
                if (parent.equals(p)) out.add(q.substring(q.lastIndexOf('/') + 1));
            }
            return new ArrayList<>(out);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1.
        Fs f = new Fs();
        check(f.mkdir("/a/b"), "e1 mkdir");
        check(f.addContentToFile("/a/b/f", "hi"), "e1 add");
        check(f.addContentToFile("/a/b/f", "yo"), "e1 append");
        if (!f.readContentFromFile("/a/b/f").equals("hiyo")) throw new AssertionError("e1 read");
        if (!f.ls("/a/b").equals(List.of("f"))) throw new AssertionError("e1 ls dir");
        if (!f.ls("/a/b/f").equals(List.of("f"))) throw new AssertionError("e1 ls file");
        // Example 2.
        Fs g = new Fs();
        check(g.mkdir("/m"), "e2 m");
        check(g.mkdir("/b"), "e2 b");
        check(g.addContentToFile("/z", "q"), "e2 file");
        check(!g.mkdir("/z/y"), "e2 blocked by a file");
        if (!g.ls("/").equals(List.of("b", "m", "z"))) throw new AssertionError("e2 ls root");
        if (!g.ls("/z/y").isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("e2 missing");
        // A rejected call creates nothing, and a directory is not a file.
        check(!g.addContentToFile("/m", "x"), "directory as file");
        check(!g.addContentToFile("/z/y/w", "x"), "file above");
        check(!g.addContentToFile("/", "x"), "root as file");
        if (!g.ls("/z/y").isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("no partial dirs");
        // Random calls against the flat oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(588);
        String[] names = {"a", "b", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            Fs s = new Fs();
            Oracle o = new Oracle();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
                int depth = rnd.nextInt(4);
                for (int i = 0; i < depth; i++) sb.append('/').append(names[rnd.nextInt(3)]);
                String p = depth == 0 ? "/" : sb.toString();
                switch (rnd.nextInt(3)) {
                    case 0 -> { if (s.mkdir(p) != o.mkdir(p)) throw new AssertionError("mkdir " + p); }
                    case 1 -> {
                        String t = "t" + rnd.nextInt(5);
                        if (s.addContentToFile(p, t) != o.add(p, t)) throw new AssertionError("add " + p);
                    }
                    default -> { }
                }
                if (!s.ls(p).equals(o.ls(p))) throw new AssertionError("ls " + p + " " + s.ls(p) + " " + o.ls(p));
                if (!s.readContentFromFile(p).equals(o.read(p))) throw new AssertionError("read " + p);
            }
            if (!s.ls("/").equals(o.ls("/"))) throw new AssertionError("ls root");
        }
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }
}verified
Edge Cases

Root And Repeated Separators

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class Registry that accepts messy path strings. The normalization policy has four rules. A path must start with /. Empty names from repeated or trailing separators are dropped. The names . and .. make the path invalid. A path with no names left is the root. The call add(path, value) stores the value and returns true only if the path is valid, is not the root, does not exist, and its parent exists. The root never holds a value, and every other call that fails changes nothing. The call get(path) returns the value, or -1 for an invalid, root or missing path. The call below(path) returns the number of stored paths strictly under the location, and 0 for an invalid or missing path.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Path has length from 1 to 200 and contains lowercase letters, . and /.
  • Value is an integer from 1 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^4, and below must cost O(depth), not O(stored paths).
  • Names such as ... and a. are valid, because only . and .. alone are rejected.

Example 1. Input calls add(//a//,1), add(/a/b/,2), get(/a///b), add(/,5), below(/), below(a), output true, true, 2, false, 2, 0.

Example 2. Input calls add(/a/../b,1), add(/x,4), add(/x/..y,6), add(/x/.,7), get(/x//..y/), below(//x), output false, true, true, false, 6, 1.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Every call normalizes first, and the count of stored paths below each node rises only after the whole add has been validated.

Hint

Which spellings must reach the same node, and which must be rejected before the walk?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. One function normalizes every incoming string into a list of names, or reports it as invalid. It rejects a string without a leading separator and any name equal to . or .., and it drops empty names. Every public call starts with this function, so no other code reads raw text.

A node holds a value, a hash map of children and a count of stored nodes below it. An add call first checks that the list is not empty. It then walks from the root and records the nodes it passes. A missing ancestor or an existing last name returns false. Only then does the call create the node and raise the count of every recorded ancestor by one. The count of the root is therefore the total number of stored paths.

The invariant is that each node's count equals the number of stored nodes in its subtree, excluding itself. The count changes only in the add call, after all checks pass.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L) per call for a string of length L, because normalizing reads each character and the walk and the count update touch at most one node per name.
  • Space is O(N + L) for N stored nodes and the names of one call.
import java.util.*;

public final class RootSeparatorsSolution {
    static final class Registry {
        private static final class Node {
            final Map<String, Node> kids = new HashMap<>();
            int value = -1;
            int below = 0;
        }

        private final Node root = new Node();

        /**
         * Turns a raw string into names, or returns null for an invalid string.
         * Time: O(L).
         * Space: O(L).
         * Invariant: the result has no empty name and no "." or ".." name.
         */
        static List<String> normalize(String path) {
            // The policy needs a leading separator.
            if (path.isEmpty() || path.charAt(0) != '/') return null;
            List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
            int i = 0;
            while (i < path.length()) {
                // Skip every separator, which drops empty names.
                if (path.charAt(i) == '/') { i++; continue; }
                int j = i;
                while (j < path.length() && path.charAt(j) != '/') j++;
                String name = path.substring(i, j);
                // Only the exact names "." and ".." are illegal.
                if (name.equals(".") || name.equals("..")) return null;
                out.add(name);
                i = j;
            }
            return out;
        }

        boolean add(String path, int value) {
            List<String> part = normalize(path);
            // An invalid string or the root cannot hold a value.
            if (part == null || part.isEmpty()) return false;
            Node cur = root;
            List<Node> seen = new ArrayList<>();
            seen.add(root);
            // Walk to the parent and remember each ancestor.
            for (int i = 0; i < part.size() - 1; i++) {
                cur = cur.kids.get(part.get(i));
                if (cur == null) return false;
                seen.add(cur);
            }
            String last = part.get(part.size() - 1);
            if (cur.kids.containsKey(last)) return false;
            // All checks passed: write the node and then the counts of its ancestors.
            Node fresh = new Node();
            fresh.value = value;
            cur.kids.put(last, fresh);
            for (Node n : seen) n.below++;
            return true;
        }

        private Node find(String path) {
            List<String> part = normalize(path);
            if (part == null) return null;
            Node cur = root;
            for (String name : part) {
                cur = cur.kids.get(name);
                if (cur == null) return null;
            }
            return cur;
        }

        int get(String path) {
            Node n = find(path);
            // The root holds no value, so its stored -1 is the answer too.
            return n == null ? -1 : n.value;
        }

        int below(String path) {
            Node n = find(path);
            return n == null ? 0 : n.below;
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: canonical strings built with split, counted by scanning every key. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final Map<String, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
        static String canon(String p) {
            if (!p.startsWith("/")) return null;
            StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
            for (String s : p.split("/", -1)) {
                if (s.isEmpty()) continue;
                if (s.equals(".") || s.equals("..")) return null;
                sb.append('/').append(s);
            }
            return sb.length() == 0 ? "/" : sb.toString();
        }
        boolean add(String p, int v) {
            String c = canon(p);
            if (c == null || c.equals("/") || m.containsKey(c)) return false;
            String parent = c.substring(0, c.lastIndexOf('/'));
            if (!parent.isEmpty() && !m.containsKey(parent)) return false;
            m.put(c, v);
            return true;
        }
        int get(String p) {
            String c = canon(p);
            return c == null ? -1 : m.getOrDefault(c, -1);
        }
        int below(String p) {
            String c = canon(p);
            if (c == null) return 0;
            if (!c.equals("/") && !m.containsKey(c)) return 0;
            int n = 0;
            for (String k : m.keySet()) if (c.equals("/") || k.startsWith(c + "/")) n++;
            return n;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1.
        Registry r = new Registry();
        check(r.add("//a//", 1), "e1 a");
        check(r.add("/a/b/", 2), "e1 b");
        if (r.get("/a///b") != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 get");
        check(!r.add("/", 5), "e1 root");
        if (r.below("/") != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 below root");
        if (r.below("a") != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 invalid");
        // Example 2.
        Registry q = new Registry();
        check(!q.add("/a/../b", 1), "e2 dotdot");
        check(q.add("/x", 4), "e2 x");
        check(q.add("/x/..y", 6), "e2 dotted name");
        check(!q.add("/x/.", 7), "e2 dot");
        if (q.get("/x//..y/") != 6) throw new AssertionError("e2 get");
        if (q.below("//x") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 below");
        // A failed add leaves the counts unchanged.
        check(!q.add("/p/q", 3), "missing parent");
        if (q.below("/") != 2) throw new AssertionError("failed add changed a count");
        // Random messy strings against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(7702);
        String[] tok = {"a", "b", ".", "..", "x.", "..y"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Registry s = new Registry();
            Oracle o = new Oracle();
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                String p = messy(rnd, tok);
                if (rnd.nextInt(3) != 0) {
                    int v = 1 + rnd.nextInt(50);
                    if (s.add(p, v) != o.add(p, v)) throw new AssertionError("add " + p);
                }
                String t = messy(rnd, tok);
                if (s.get(t) != o.get(t)) throw new AssertionError("get " + t);
                if (s.below(t) != o.below(t)) throw new AssertionError("below " + t);
            }
            if (s.below("/") != o.below("/")) throw new AssertionError("total");
        }
    }

    static String messy(Random rnd, String[] tok) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
