        if (rnd.nextInt(8) != 0) sb.append("/".repeat(1 + rnd.nextInt(3)));
        int n = rnd.nextInt(4);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            // Mostly valid names so that creates succeed often.
            sb.append(rnd.nextInt(5) == 0 ? tok[rnd.nextInt(tok.length)] : tok[rnd.nextInt(2)]);
            sb.append("/".repeat(1 + rnd.nextInt(2)));
        }
        return sb.toString();
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Design SQL

LeetCode 2408

Problem Specification. This contract is written for the chapter, because LeetCode 2408 is a premium problem and its statement may differ. Design a class Sql built from distinct table names and the number of columns of each table. The call insertRow(name, row) appends a row of strings to the table and returns its row id. Ids of one table start at 1, grow by one for each successful insert, and are never reused after a delete. It returns -1 and changes nothing if the table is unknown or the row length differs from the column count. The call deleteRow(name, id) removes a live row and returns true, or returns false otherwise. The call selectCell(name, id, column) returns the string in a 1-based column of a live row, or the empty string for any invalid argument. The call count(name) returns the number of live rows, or -1 for an unknown table.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Tables number from 1 to 100, and columns are integers from 1 to 10.
  • Cells are strings of length from 1 to 10.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Ids are int values assigned per table.

Example 1. Tables a with 2 columns and b with 1 column. Input calls insertRow(a,[x,y]), insertRow(a,[p]), insertRow(a,[p,q]), selectCell(a,2,1), deleteRow(a,1), insertRow(a,[m,n]), count(a), output 1, -1, 2, p, true, 3, 2.

Example 2. The same tables. Input calls insertRow(c,[z]), insertRow(b,[k]), deleteRow(b,1), deleteRow(b,1), selectCell(b,1,1), insertRow(b,[w]), output -1, 1, true, false, the empty string, 2.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The table name is the first level of the tree, and the row id is the second level. The id counter advances only after the table and the row length have been validated.

Hint

Which name resolves the container, and which counter must a failed insert leave alone?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The table name is the first name of a two-level tree. A hash map from table name to a table record resolves it. A table record holds the column count, the next row id and a hash map from row id to the row array. The row id is the second name.

An insert validates the table name and the row length before it touches anything. Only after both checks does it read the counter, store the row and advance the counter by one. A failed insert therefore consumes no id. A delete removes the row from the map and never lowers the counter, so ids are not reused.

The invariant is that the counter of a table is larger than every id ever assigned, and the row map holds exactly the live rows.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(c) per insert for c columns, because the row is copied, and O(1) expected for every other call.
  • Space is O(R * c) for R live rows, plus O(T) for T tables.
import java.util.*;

public final class SqlSolution {
    static final class Sql {
        private static final class Table {
            final int columns;
            int nextId = 1;
            final Map<Integer, String[]> rows = new HashMap<>();
            Table(int columns) { this.columns = columns; }
        }

        private final Map<String, Table> tables = new HashMap<>();

        Sql(String[] names, int[] columns) {
            for (int i = 0; i < names.length; i++) tables.put(names[i], new Table(columns[i]));
        }

        /**
         * Appends a row and returns its id, or -1 with no change.
         * Time: O(c) for c columns.
         * Space: O(c) for the copied row.
         * Invariant: nextId advances only when a row is stored.
         */
        int insertRow(String name, String[] row) {
            Table t = tables.get(name);
            // Validate the table and the row length before any write.
            if (t == null || row.length != t.columns) return -1;
            int id = t.nextId++;
            t.rows.put(id, row.clone());
            return id;
        }

        boolean deleteRow(String name, int id) {
            Table t = tables.get(name);
            // The counter stays, so a deleted id is never handed out again.
            return t != null && t.rows.remove(id) != null;
        }

        String selectCell(String name, int id, int column) {
            Table t = tables.get(name);
            if (t == null || column < 1 || column > t.columns) return "";
            String[] row = t.rows.get(id);
            return row == null ? "" : row[column - 1];
        }

        int count(String name) {
            Table t = tables.get(name);
            return t == null ? -1 : t.rows.size();
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: a log of every successful insert per table, scanned linearly. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final List<String> names;
        final int[] cols;
        final List<List<Object[]>> log = new ArrayList<>(); // each entry: {id, row, live}
        Oracle(String[] n, int[] c) {
            names = Arrays.asList(n);
            cols = c;
            for (int i = 0; i < n.length; i++) log.add(new ArrayList<>());
        }
        int insert(String n, String[] row) {
            int t = names.indexOf(n);
            if (t < 0 || row.length != cols[t]) return -1;
            int id = log.get(t).size() + 1;
            log.get(t).add(new Object[] {id, row, true});
            return id;
        }
        boolean delete(String n, int id) {
            int t = names.indexOf(n);
            if (t < 0) return false;
            for (Object[] e : log.get(t)) if ((int) e[0] == id && (boolean) e[2]) { e[2] = false; return true; }
            return false;
        }
        String select(String n, int id, int c) {
            int t = names.indexOf(n);
            if (t < 0 || c < 1 || c > cols[t]) return "";
            for (Object[] e : log.get(t)) if ((int) e[0] == id && (boolean) e[2]) return ((String[]) e[1])[c - 1];
            return "";
        }
        int count(String n) {
            int t = names.indexOf(n);
            if (t < 0) return -1;
            int k = 0;
            for (Object[] e : log.get(t)) if ((boolean) e[2]) k++;
            return k;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String[] names = {"a", "b"};
        int[] cols = {2, 1};
        // Example 1.
        Sql s = new Sql(names, cols);
        if (s.insertRow("a", new String[] {"x", "y"}) != 1) throw new AssertionError("e1 first id");
        if (s.insertRow("a", new String[] {"p"}) != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 bad length");
        if (s.insertRow("a", new String[] {"p", "q"}) != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 failed insert kept its id");
        if (!s.selectCell("a", 2, 1).equals("p")) throw new AssertionError("e1 select");
        if (!s.deleteRow("a", 1)) throw new AssertionError("e1 delete");
        if (s.insertRow("a", new String[] {"m", "n"}) != 3) throw new AssertionError("e1 id reuse");
        if (s.count("a") != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 count");
        // Example 2.
        Sql t = new Sql(names, cols);
        if (t.insertRow("c", new String[] {"z"}) != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2 unknown table");
        if (t.insertRow("b", new String[] {"k"}) != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 first");
        if (!t.deleteRow("b", 1)) throw new AssertionError("e2 delete");
        if (t.deleteRow("b", 1)) throw new AssertionError("e2 second delete");
        if (!t.selectCell("b", 1, 1).isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("e2 deleted cell");
        if (t.insertRow("b", new String[] {"w"}) != 2) throw new AssertionError("e2 next id");
        // Random calls against the log oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2408);
        String[] pool = {"a", "b", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            Sql x = new Sql(names, cols);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(names, cols);
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                String n = pool[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                switch (rnd.nextInt(4)) {
                    case 0 -> {
                        String[] row = new String[1 + rnd.nextInt(3)];
                        for (int i = 0; i < row.length; i++) row[i] = "v" + rnd.nextInt(9);
                        if (x.insertRow(n, row) != o.insert(n, row)) throw new AssertionError("insert");
                    }
                    case 1 -> {
                        int id = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
                        if (x.deleteRow(n, id) != o.delete(n, id)) throw new AssertionError("delete");
                    }
                    case 2 -> {
                        int id = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6), c = rnd.nextInt(4);
                        if (!x.selectCell(n, id, c).equals(o.select(n, id, c))) throw new AssertionError("select");
                    }
                    default -> { if (x.count(n) != o.count(n)) throw new AssertionError("count"); }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Update Values That Depend On Others

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Total That Ignores The Edit

A budget sheet has a cell rent that holds 900, a cell food that holds 800, and a cell total that holds the formula rent + food. The sheet shows 1700 in total. The user raises rent to 1000, and total still shows 1700.

The sheet answered the first read correctly and the second read wrongly. Nothing crashed, so the error stays invisible until someone adds the numbers by hand. Another cell may read total, and then that cell is wrong too.

This lesson asks how a sheet, an expression tree or a rule engine can keep every computed value correct after any edit, without recomputing the whole sheet on each read.

Simple approach

Storing The Sum When It Is Set

The direct design computes a formula once, at the moment the user enters it. It stores the resulting number in the cell and treats it like any other number.

final class NaiveSheet {
    private final Map<String, Long> value = new HashMap<>();

    void setLiteral(String cell, long v) { value.put(cell, v); }

    void setSum(String cell, String... refs) {
        long sum = 0;
        for (String r : refs) sum += value.getOrDefault(r, 0L);   // reads the other cells once
        value.put(cell, sum);                                      // the result is saved as a plain number
    }

    long get(String cell) { return value.getOrDefault(cell, 0L); }
}

Every read costs one hash lookup, which is fast. The saved result stays the same after a later edit of a cell that the formula read.

Predict first: The sheet runs setLiteral("a", 3), setLiteral("b", 4), setSum("c", "a", "b") and setLiteral("a", 10). What does get("c") return, and what should it return?

The call returns 7, because the sum was saved when c was set and nothing touched c afterward. It should return 14, because c is defined as a plus b and a is now 10. The saved number lost the link to the cells it came from.

Bottleneck

Stale Results Or Full Recomputation

The design gives a wrong answer, so correctness is the first problem. Two quick repairs exist, and each has a cost. The first repair stores only the formulas and evaluates the whole chain on every read. A read then costs O(n) for a chain of n formula cells. With multiplicities, such as a cell that sums the same neighbor twice, a read can even visit the same cell many times.

The second repair recomputes every formula cell after each edit. An edit then costs O(n) even when it touches one cell that nothing reads. A sheet with thousands of formulas and mostly local edits wastes nearly all of that work.

Both repairs miss the key fact. An edit affects only the cells that read the edited cell, directly or through other formulas. The sheet has no record of who reads whom, so it cannot find those cells without scanning everything.

Key idea

Recompute Only Cells Affected By Edits

Keep the formula of every cell as authoritative data, and keep two derived structures beside it. The first is a cached value for each formula cell. A read returns it in O(1) time. The second is a map from each cell to its dependents, which are the cells whose formulas name that cell.

Propagating An Edit

When a cell changes, the sheet visits its dependents, then their dependents, and so on. It recomputes each visited cell once, and it does so in an order where every cell comes after all the cells it reads. A depth-first search over the dependents map gives that order when the search records each cell after its dependents and the sheet reverses the list. The cost of an edit is the size of the affected region, not the size of the sheet.

Rejecting A Bad Formula First

A new formula can name a cell that already reads the edited cell. Then the two cells would wait for each other, and no evaluation order exists. The cycle check runs before any write. It searches from the proposed references along the existing formulas, and it rejects the formula if the search reaches the edited cell. A rejected call changes nothing.

Replacing A Formula

Before an edited cell gets a new definition, the sheet removes the cell from the dependents of each cell its old formula read. Skipping that step leaves stale entries that later trigger recomputation of a cell that no longer reads them.

The invariant is that every cached value equals its formula applied to the current values of the cells it reads, and the dependents map lists exactly the cells that name each cell.

State

State Kept For Each Cell

The service lives in one object in memory. It has no files, no threads and no limit other than memory. The tests keep it under a few thousand cells. Four kinds of state describe each cell.

  • Literal is the number a user typed, and it is authoritative until the cell gets a formula.
  • Formula is the list of cells that the cell sums, with repeats, and it is authoritative for a formula cell.
  • Value is the current number of a cell, and for a formula cell it is derived from the formula.
  • Dependents is the derived set of cells that name this cell in a formula.

An edit rewrites the literal or the formula first. It then repairs the value and the dependents in the same call.

Walkthrough

Following Edits Through Formulas

A Chain That Updates

The first trace edits a sheet with the formula cells c and d. The pointer call marks the latest call, and the variables show every value after it.

The fifth call changes a, which both c and d read. The sheet recomputes c first and d second, so d reads the fresh value of c.

Replacing And Rejecting Formulas

The second trace shows a repeated reference, a rejected formula and a replaced formula. The variables show the dependents of cell a after each call.

The fourth call asks a to read e, which already reads a. The cycle check finds that loop and the call returns false with no change. The fifth call replaces the formula of e, so e leaves the dependents of a. The sixth call then edits a and recomputes nothing.

Code

A Sheet That Refreshes Dependents

The class stores formulas, cached values and dependents, and it checks for a cycle before it writes anything.

final class LiveSheet {
    private final Map<String, List<String>> formula = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<String, Set<String>> dependents = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<String, Long> value = new HashMap<>();

    long get(String cell) { return value.getOrDefault(cell, 0L); }

    void setLiteral(String cell, long v) {
        detach(cell);                                  // a literal has no formula and reads nothing
        value.put(cell, v);
        refresh(cell);                                 // dependents see the new number
    }

    boolean setFormula(String cell, List<String> refs) {
        if (reaches(refs, cell)) return false;         // reject before any write
        detach(cell);
        formula.put(cell, new ArrayList<>(refs));
        for (String r : refs) dependents.computeIfAbsent(r, k -> new HashSet<>()).add(cell);
        refresh(cell);
        return true;
    }

    private void detach(String cell) {
        List<String> old = formula.remove(cell);       // forget the old formula
        if (old != null) for (String r : old) dependents.get(r).remove(cell);
    }

    private boolean reaches(List<String> from, String target) {
        Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(from);
        Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            String c = stack.pop();
            if (c.equals(target)) return true;         // the target would read itself
            if (seen.add(c)) stack.addAll(formula.getOrDefault(c, List.of()));
        }
        return false;
    }

    private void refresh(String start) {
        List<String> post = new ArrayList<>();
        collect(start, new HashSet<>(), post);
        Collections.reverse(post);                     // each cell now follows every cell it reads
        for (String c : post) {
            List<String> f = formula.get(c);
            if (f == null) continue;                   // literal cells keep their number
            long sum = 0;
            for (String r : f) sum += get(r);
            value.put(c, sum);
        }
    }

    private void collect(String c, Set<String> seen, List<String> post) {
        if (!seen.add(c)) return;
        for (String d : dependents.getOrDefault(c, Set.of())) collect(d, seen, post);
        post.add(c);                                   // recorded after all of its dependents
    }
}

A read costs O(1). An edit costs O(k + A) for k named cells and for A affected cells and their formula entries. The space is O(n + e) for n cells and e formula entries.

When to use it

Values Computed From Other Values

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is a stored value that the API defines in terms of other stored values, such as a spreadsheet cell, a tree of operators or a score built from sub-scores. Ask whether a read must show the result of the latest edit. If yes, list which values each formula reads, because those edges decide what an edit must touch.

Choosing Where To Compute

Compute on read when edits are frequent and reads are rare, or when the dependency chain is short. Cache and propagate when reads dominate. A query that is never stored, such as a formula passed to a single call, needs no edges at all.

False Friends

A cache without dependents looks fast and returns stale values, so it is a false friend of this lesson. A reverse map without the removal step in detach looks complete and keeps edges to formulas that were replaced. A recursive evaluation that skips the cycle check loops forever on a cyclic formula. The invariant of this lesson is what exposes each of these: after every call, each cached value must match a fresh evaluation of its formula.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Expression Tree Evaluation

LeetCode 1628

Problem Specification. This chapter states its own contract, which may differ in detail from LeetCode. Implement build(postfix), which takes an array of string tokens in postfix order and returns an expression object. A token is either a nonnegative integer or one of the operators +, -, * and /. The object has the method evaluate(). It returns the value of the expression, where an operator applies to its left and right subexpressions and / truncates toward zero. Calling evaluate() again returns the same value.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Tokens number from 1 to 100, and the array is a valid postfix expression.
  • Operands are integers from 0 to 100.
  • Values at every step fit in an int, and no division has a zero divisor.
  • Return is an int.

Example 1. Input ["3","4","+","2","*"], output 14.

Example 2. Input ["0","7","-","2","/"], output -3.

Prerequisites. Stacks and recursion.

What This Exercise Changes. An operand node returns its own number and an operator node asks its two children, so evaluation follows the child edges and no node keeps a result.

Hint

What does an operand return, and what does an operator need from its two children?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. An abstract node class declares evaluate(). An operand node stores one integer and returns it. An operator node stores its symbol and two child nodes, evaluates the left child, then the right child, and applies the symbol. The builder reads the tokens left to right with a stack. An operand token pushes a new operand node. An operator token pops the right child first and the left child second, then pushes the new operator node.

The invariant is that after each token the stack holds the complete subtrees built so far, in token order. A valid postfix expression leaves exactly one node, which is the root. No node stores a result, so repeated calls give the same value.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) to build and O(n) per evaluate() call, because every node is created once and visited once.
  • Space is O(n) for the nodes, plus recursion depth up to the tree height.
import java.util.*;

public final class ExpressionTreeSolution {
    /** Common type for both node kinds; invariant: evaluate has no side effects. */
    static abstract class Node {
        abstract int evaluate();
    }

    static final class Operand extends Node {
        private final int number;
        Operand(int number) { this.number = number; }
        // O(1) time and space; returns the stored number.
        @Override int evaluate() { return number; }
    }

    static final class Operator extends Node {
        private final char symbol;
        private final Node left, right;
        Operator(char symbol, Node left, Node right) { this.symbol = symbol; this.left = left; this.right = right; }

        /**
         * Applies the symbol to the two child values.
         * Time: O(size of this subtree).
         * Space: O(height) for recursion.
         * Invariant: the left child is evaluated before the right child.
         */
        @Override int evaluate() {
            // Both children return before the symbol is applied.
            int a = left.evaluate(), b = right.evaluate();
            return switch (symbol) {
                case '+' -> a + b;
                case '-' -> a - b;
                case '*' -> a * b;
                default -> a / b;      // Java integer division truncates toward zero
            };
        }
    }

    /**
     * Builds the tree from a valid postfix token array.
     * Time: O(n).
     * Space: O(n).
     * Invariant: the stack holds complete subtrees in token order.
     */
    static Node build(String[] postfix) {
        Deque<Node> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        // One pass over the tokens, one push or two pops per token.
        for (String t : postfix) {
            if (t.length() == 1 && "+-*/".indexOf(t.charAt(0)) >= 0) {
                Node right = stack.pop();           // the right child was pushed last
                Node left = stack.pop();
                stack.push(new Operator(t.charAt(0), left, right));
            } else {
                stack.push(new Operand(Integer.parseInt(t)));
            }
        }
        // A valid expression leaves its root alone on the stack.
        return stack.pop();
    }

    // Oracle: evaluates the postfix tokens directly on a long stack.
    static long oracle(String[] postfix) {
        long[] st = new long[postfix.length];
        int top = 0;
        for (String t : postfix) {
            if (t.length() == 1 && "+-*/".indexOf(t.charAt(0)) >= 0) {
                long b = st[--top], a = st[--top];
                st[top++] = switch (t.charAt(0)) { case '+' -> a + b; case '-' -> a - b; case '*' -> a * b; default -> a / b; };
            } else st[top++] = Long.parseLong(t);
        }
        return st[0];
    }

    // Generates a random postfix expression, or returns null when a step leaves the int range or divides by zero.
    static String[] randomPostfix(Random rnd, int leaves) {
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Long> vals = new ArrayList<>();
        int pushed = 0;
        while (pushed < leaves || vals.size() > 1) {
            boolean canOp = vals.size() >= 2;
            if (pushed < leaves && (!canOp || rnd.nextBoolean())) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(101);
                out.add(Integer.toString(v)); vals.add((long) v); pushed++;
            } else {
                long b = vals.remove(vals.size() - 1), a = vals.remove(vals.size() - 1);
                char op = "+-*/".charAt(rnd.nextInt(4));
                if (op == '/' && b == 0) return null;
                long r = switch (op) { case '+' -> a + b; case '-' -> a - b; case '*' -> a * b; default -> a / b; };
                if (r > Integer.MAX_VALUE || r < Integer.MIN_VALUE) return null;
                out.add(String.valueOf(op)); vals.add(r);
            }
        }
        return out.toArray(new String[0]);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text, including a repeated evaluate call.
        Node e1 = build(new String[] {"3", "4", "+", "2", "*"});
        if (e1.evaluate() != 14 || e1.evaluate() != 14) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        if (build(new String[] {"0", "7", "-", "2", "/"}).evaluate() != -3) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // A single operand is already a complete expression.
        if (build(new String[] {"42"}).evaluate() != 42) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // Random valid expressions checked against the direct stack evaluation.
        Random rnd = new Random(1628);
        int checked = 0;
        for (int round = 0; round < 3000; round++) {
            String[] p = randomPostfix(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(12));
            if (p == null) continue;
            Node root = build(p);
            if (root.evaluate() != oracle(p)) throw new AssertionError("value " + Arrays.toString(p));
            if (root.evaluate() != oracle(p)) throw new AssertionError("repeat");
            checked++;
        }
        if (checked < 500) throw new AssertionError("too few valid cases");
    }
}verified
Variation

Excel Sum Formula

LeetCode 631

Problem Specification. Design a class Excel for a grid with height rows numbered from 1 and width columns named from A. All cells start at 0. The call set(row, col, v) stores the number v and removes any formula from the cell. The call get(row, col) returns the current value. The call sum(row, col, refs) gives the cell a formula and returns its value. Each string in refs is a single cell such as A1 or a rectangle such as A1:B2, and the formula adds the cell once for every time it is named. No formula reads its own cell, directly or through other cells.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Grid has at most 26 rows and the columns A to Z.
  • Values passed to set are integers from 0 to 100.
  • Values held by any cell fit in an int.
  • Return of get and sum is an int.

Example 1. Input Excel(3,"C"), set(1,"A",2), sum(3,"C",["A1","A1:B2"]), output 4.

Example 2. Input the same calls, then set(2,"B",5), get(3,"C"), sum(1,"A",["B2"]), get(3,"C"), output 9, 5 and 15.

Prerequisites. The expression tree exercise above and hash sets.

What This Exercise Changes. A formula stores a count per cell and expands each rectangle into those counts, so a cell named twice adds twice and an edit of it recomputes each formula once.

Hint

Which counts does a formula store when two references name the same cell?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Each cell has a literal number or a formula, which is a map from cell index to the number of times the formula names it. The cached value of each cell is derived from that definition. Each cell also owns a set of dependents, the cells whose formulas name it. A sum call expands every reference string into cell indexes and counts them, so a cell named by two rectangles gets the count 2.

An edit follows the same steps for set and sum. First the cell leaves the dependents of every cell in its old formula. Then it stores the new definition and joins the dependents of every cell in the new formula. Then a depth-first search over dependents starts at the edited cell and records each cell after its dependents. Reversing that list puts every cell after the cells it reads, and the sheet recomputes the formula cells in that order.

The invariant is that every cached value equals the sum of count times value over its formula, and a cell appears in the dependents of exactly the cells its formula names. The acyclic guarantee of the contract makes the search finish.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(R + A + E) per edit, where R is the number of cells in the named rectangles, A is the number of affected cells and E is their formula entries. get is O(1).
  • Space is O(H * W + F), where F is the total number of formula entries.
import java.util.*;

public final class ExcelSumSolution {
    static final class Excel {
        private final int width;
        private final int[] value;
        private final Map<Integer, Integer>[] formula;     // null for a literal cell; cell -> times named
        private final Set<Integer>[] dependents;           // cells whose formulas name this cell

        @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
        Excel(int height, char lastColumn) {
            width = lastColumn - 'A' + 1;
            int n = height * width;
            value = new int[n];
            formula = new Map[n];
            dependents = new Set[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dependents[i] = new HashSet<>();
        }

        private int id(int row, char col) { return (row - 1) * width + (col - 'A'); }

        /** Stores a literal and refreshes dependents; time O(A + E), space O(A). */
        void set(int row, char col, int v) {
            int c = id(row, col);
            detach(c);                 // the cell stops reading its old formula cells
            value[c] = v;
            refresh(c);                // dependents see the new literal
        }

        int get(int row, char col) { return value[id(row, col)]; }

        /**
         * Gives the cell a formula and returns its value.
         * Time: O(R + A + E).
         * Space: O(R + A).
         * Invariant: the cell joins dependents only after it left the old ones.
         */
        int sum(int row, char col, String[] refs) {
            int c = id(row, col);
            Map<Integer, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
            // Each named cell, inside or outside a rectangle, adds one to its count.
            for (String ref : refs) {
                String[] part = ref.split(":");
                int r1 = Integer.parseInt(part[0].substring(1)), c1 = part[0].charAt(0) - 'A';
                int r2 = r1, c2 = c1;
                if (part.length == 2) { r2 = Integer.parseInt(part[1].substring(1)); c2 = part[1].charAt(0) - 'A'; }
                for (int r = r1; r <= r2; r++) for (int k = c1; k <= c2; k++) counts.merge((r - 1) * width + k, 1, Integer::sum);
            }
            detach(c);
            formula[c] = counts;
            for (int d : counts.keySet()) dependents[d].add(c);
            refresh(c);
            return value[c];
        }

        // Removes the cell from the dependents of everything its old formula named; time O(entries).
        private void detach(int c) {
            if (formula[c] == null) return;
            for (int d : formula[c].keySet()) dependents[d].remove(c);
            formula[c] = null;
        }

        // Recomputes the edited cell and everything that reads it, in an order where readers come last.
        private void refresh(int start) {
            List<Integer> post = new ArrayList<>();
            collect(start, new boolean[value.length], post);
            Collections.reverse(post);
            for (int c : post) {
                if (formula[c] == null) continue;      // a literal keeps its number
                long s = 0;
                for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : formula[c].entrySet()) s += (long) e.getValue() * value[e.getKey()];
                value[c] = (int) s;
            }
        }

        // Depth-first search over dependents; a cell is recorded after all of its dependents.
        private void collect(int c, boolean[] seen, List<Integer> post) {
            if (seen[c]) return;
            seen[c] = true;
            for (int d : dependents[c]) collect(d, seen, post);
            post.add(c);
        }
    }

    // Oracle state: a literal or a count map per cell, evaluated from scratch on every read.
    static final class Brute {
        final int w, n;
        final long[] lit;
        final Map<Integer, Integer>[] def;
        @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
        Brute(int h, int w) { this.w = w; n = h * w; lit = new long[n]; def = new Map[n]; }

        long eval(int c, long[] memo, boolean[] done) {
            if (def[c] == null) return lit[c];
            if (done[c]) return memo[c];
            long s = 0;
            for (Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> e : def[c].entrySet()) s += e.getValue() * eval(e.getKey(), memo, done);
            done[c] = true; memo[c] = s;
            return s;
        }

        long read(int c) { return eval(c, new long[n], new boolean[n]); }

        boolean reaches(int from, int target, boolean[] seen) {
            if (from == target) return true;
            if (seen[from] || def[from] == null) return false;
            seen[from] = true;
            for (int d : def[from].keySet()) if (reaches(d, target, seen)) return true;
            return false;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        Excel x = new Excel(3, 'C');
        x.set(1, 'A', 2);
        if (x.sum(3, 'C', new String[] {"A1", "A1:B2"}) != 4) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        x.set(2, 'B', 5);
        if (x.get(3, 'C') != 9) throw new AssertionError("e2 first");
        if (x.sum(1, 'A', new String[] {"B2"}) != 5) throw new AssertionError("e2 sum");
        if (x.get(3, 'C') != 15) throw new AssertionError("e2 last");
        // Replacing a formula by a literal must stop later edits of the old inputs from reaching it.
        Excel y = new Excel(2, 'B');
        y.set(1, 'A', 1);
        y.sum(2, 'B', new String[] {"A1"});
        y.set(2, 'B', 7);
        y.set(1, 'A', 9);
        if (y.get(2, 'B') != 7) throw new AssertionError("stale dependents");
        // Random edits on a 4 by 4 grid checked against evaluation from the definitions.
        Random rnd = new Random(631);
        int accepted = 0;
        for (int round = 0; round < 200; round++) {
            Excel s = new Excel(4, 'D');
            Brute b = new Brute(4, 4);
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                int r = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4), col = rnd.nextInt(4), c = (r - 1) * 4 + col;
                if (rnd.nextInt(3) == 0) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(10);
                    s.set(r, (char) ('A' + col), v);
                    b.def[c] = null; b.lit[c] = v;
                } else {
                    int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
                    String[] refs = new String[k];
                    Map<Integer, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
                    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) {
                        int r1 = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4), c1 = rnd.nextInt(4);
                        boolean rect = rnd.nextBoolean();
                        int r2 = rect ? r1 + rnd.nextInt(5 - r1) : r1, c2 = rect ? c1 + rnd.nextInt(4 - c1) : c1;
                        refs[i] = "" + (char) ('A' + c1) + r1 + (rect ? ":" + (char) ('A' + c2) + r2 : "");
                        for (int rr = r1; rr <= r2; rr++) for (int cc = c1; cc <= c2; cc++) counts.merge((rr - 1) * 4 + cc, 1, Integer::sum);
                    }
                    // Skip a formula that would read its own cell, because the contract excludes it.
                    boolean cyc = false;
                    for (int d : counts.keySet()) if (b.reaches(d, c, new boolean[16])) cyc = true;
                    if (cyc) continue;
                    Map<Integer, Integer> oldDef = b.def[c];
                    b.def[c] = counts;
                    // Skip a formula whose values would leave the int range anywhere in the grid.
                    boolean big = false;
                    for (int i = 0; i < 16; i++) if (Math.abs(b.read(i)) > 1_000_000_000L) big = true;
                    if (big) { b.def[c] = oldDef; continue; }
                    int got = s.sum(r, (char) ('A' + col), refs);
                    if (got != b.read(c)) throw new AssertionError("sum result");
                    accepted++;
                }
                // Every cell must match a fresh evaluation after each call.
                for (int i = 0; i < 16; i++) if (s.get(1 + i / 4, (char) ('A' + i % 4)) != b.read(i)) throw new AssertionError("cell " + i);
            }
        }
        if (accepted < 500) throw new AssertionError("too few formulas");
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Dependency Cycle Policy

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class Sheet for cells named by lowercase strings. The call setLiteral(cell, v) stores the number and removes any formula. The call setFormula(cell, refs) makes the cell the sum of the cells in refs, with repeats counted, and returns true. It returns false and changes nothing when the new formula would make the cell depend on itself, directly or through other cells. The call get(cell) returns the current value. A cell that was never set has the value 0.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Names are strings of 1 to 8 lowercase letters.
  • Literals are integers from -1000 to 1000.
  • Refs holds 1 to 5 names, and it may repeat a name or name an unset cell.
  • Values at every cell fit in a long.

Example 1. Input setLiteral(a,3), setLiteral(b,4), setFormula(c,[a,b]), get(c), setFormula(a,[c]), get(a), output true for the formula, 7, false and 3.

Example 2. Input setFormula(x,[x]), setFormula(x,[y,y]), setLiteral(y,5), get(x), setFormula(y,[x]), output false, true, 10 and false.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The call rejects a cycle by searching before any edge changes, so a rejected formula leaves the old formula, the cached values and the dependents untouched.

Hint

Which cells does a search follow to find out whether the target is already a reader of the new references?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The sheet stores each formula as a list of names, a value for every cell and a dependents set for every name that some formula reads. A setFormula call runs a depth-first search that starts at the proposed references and follows the existing formulas. If the search reaches the target cell, including by a direct self-reference, the call returns false before it writes anything. The search needs no knowledge of the target's old formula, because a path ends the moment it reaches the target.

When the check passes, the call detaches the cell from its old inputs, stores the new list, registers the cell as a dependent of each new input and refreshes the cell and its dependents in reverse post-order. A name that no call has defined still holds a dependents set, so a later setLiteral on it reaches its readers.

The invariant is that the formula graph is acyclic after every call, and every cached value equals its formula applied to current values. A rejected call changes neither.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(k + V + E) per setFormula call for k refs and for the V cells and E entries the search and refresh visit. get is O(1).
  • Space is O(n + e) for n named cells and e formula entries.
import java.util.*;

public final class CyclePolicySolution {
    static final class Sheet {
        private final Map<String, List<String>> formula = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<String, Set<String>> dependents = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<String, Long> value = new HashMap<>();

        /** Reads a value in O(1); an unset cell is 0. */
        long get(String cell) { return value.getOrDefault(cell, 0L); }

        /** Stores a literal; a literal cannot create a cycle because it reads nothing. Time O(A + E). */
        void setLiteral(String cell, long v) {
            detach(cell);
            value.put(cell, v);
            refresh(cell);
        }

        /**
         * Replaces the formula unless it would create a cycle.
         * Time: O(k + V + E).
         * Space: O(V).
         * Invariant: the search runs before the first write.
         */
        boolean setFormula(String cell, List<String> refs) {
            // Reaching the target from any reference means the target would read itself.
            if (reaches(refs, cell)) return false;
            detach(cell);
            formula.put(cell, new ArrayList<>(refs));
            // Each reference learns that the cell reads it.
            for (String r : refs) dependents.computeIfAbsent(r, k -> new HashSet<>()).add(cell);
            refresh(cell);
            return true;
        }

        // Drops the old formula edges so no stale dependents remain; time O(entries).
        private void detach(String cell) {
            List<String> old = formula.remove(cell);
            if (old == null) return;
            for (String r : old) dependents.get(r).remove(cell);
        }

        // Iterative search along formula edges; each cell expands once, so time is O(V + E).
        private boolean reaches(List<String> from, String target) {
            Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(from);
            Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
            while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
                String c = stack.pop();
                if (c.equals(target)) return true;
                if (seen.add(c)) stack.addAll(formula.getOrDefault(c, List.of()));
            }
            return false;
        }

        // Recomputes the start cell and its readers in reverse post-order of the dependents graph.
        private void refresh(String start) {
            List<String> post = new ArrayList<>();
            collect(start, new HashSet<>(), post);
            Collections.reverse(post);
            for (String c : post) {
                List<String> f = formula.get(c);
                if (f == null) continue;
                long sum = 0;
                for (String r : f) sum += get(r);
                value.put(c, sum);
            }
        }

        private void collect(String c, Set<String> seen, List<String> post) {
            if (!seen.add(c)) return;
            for (String d : dependents.getOrDefault(c, Set.of())) collect(d, seen, post);
            post.add(c);
        }
    }

    // Oracle: keeps definitions only, detects cycles with Kahn's algorithm and evaluates with memoization.
    static final class Brute {
        final Map<String, Long> lit = new HashMap<>();
        final Map<String, List<String>> def = new HashMap<>();

        boolean acyclic(Map<String, List<String>> d) {
            Map<String, Integer> indeg = new HashMap<>();
            Map<String, List<String>> out = new HashMap<>();
            for (Map.Entry<String, List<String>> e : d.entrySet()) {
                indeg.putIfAbsent(e.getKey(), 0);
                for (String r : e.getValue()) {
                    indeg.putIfAbsent(r, 0);
                    indeg.merge(e.getKey(), 1, Integer::sum);
                    out.computeIfAbsent(r, k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(e.getKey());
                }
            }
            Deque<String> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
            for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : indeg.entrySet()) if (e.getValue() == 0) q.add(e.getKey());
            int removed = 0;
            while (!q.isEmpty()) {
                String c = q.poll();
                removed++;
                for (String u : out.getOrDefault(c, List.of())) if (indeg.merge(u, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) q.add(u);
            }
            return removed == indeg.size();
        }

        long read(String c, Map<String, Long> memo) {
            if (!def.containsKey(c)) return lit.getOrDefault(c, 0L);
            Long m = memo.get(c);
            if (m != null) return m;
            long s = 0;
            for (String r : def.get(c)) s += read(r, memo);
            memo.put(c, s);
            return s;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Sheet s = new Sheet();
        s.setLiteral("a", 3);
        s.setLiteral("b", 4);
        if (!s.setFormula("c", List.of("a", "b")) || s.get("c") != 7) throw new AssertionError("e1 formula");
        if (s.setFormula("a", List.of("c")) || s.get("a") != 3) throw new AssertionError("e1 reject");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Sheet t = new Sheet();
        if (t.setFormula("x", List.of("x"))) throw new AssertionError("self reference");
        if (!t.setFormula("x", List.of("y", "y"))) throw new AssertionError("e2 formula");
        t.setLiteral("y", 5);
        if (t.get("x") != 10) throw new AssertionError("e2 value");
        if (t.setFormula("y", List.of("x"))) throw new AssertionError("e2 reject");
        // Random calls over six names checked against a cycle test on the whole graph.
        Random rnd = new Random(3003);
        String[] names = {"a", "b", "c", "d", "e", "f"};
        int rejected = 0, accepted = 0;
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Sheet sh = new Sheet();
            Brute b = new Brute();
            for (int op = 0; op < 40; op++) {
                String cell = names[rnd.nextInt(6)];
                if (rnd.nextInt(3) == 0) {
                    long v = rnd.nextInt(11) - 5;
                    sh.setLiteral(cell, v);
                    b.def.remove(cell); b.lit.put(cell, v);
                } else {
                    int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
                    List<String> refs = new ArrayList<>();
                    for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) refs.add(names[rnd.nextInt(6)]);
                    Map<String, List<String>> cand = new HashMap<>(b.def);
                    cand.put(cell, refs);
                    boolean want = b.acyclic(cand);
                    if (sh.setFormula(cell, refs) != want) throw new AssertionError("verdict " + cell + refs);
                    if (want) { b.def.put(cell, refs); accepted++; } else rejected++;
                }
                // Every name, accepted call or not, must match a fresh evaluation.
                Map<String, Long> memo = new HashMap<>();
                for (String n : names) if (sh.get(n) != b.read(n, memo)) throw new AssertionError("value " + n);
            }
        }
        if (rejected < 100 || accepted < 100) throw new AssertionError("weak coverage");
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Design Spreadsheet

LeetCode 3484

Problem Specification. This contract is written from the title, so the LeetCode statement may differ. Design a class Spreadsheet built from rows, with columns A to Z. All cells start at 0. The call setCell(cell, v) stores the number. The call resetCell(cell) sets the cell to 0 and returns the value it held. The call getValue(formula) takes a string =X+Y where each of X and Y is a nonnegative integer or a cell name, and returns the sum of the current values. The formula is a question and the sheet does not store it.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Rows are integers from 1 to 1000, and cell names such as B17 are valid.
  • Values stored by setCell are integers from 0 to 100000.
  • Formula has exactly one +, and each integer in it is at most 100000.
  • Return is an int.

Example 1. Input Spreadsheet(3), setCell(A1,2), setCell(B2,5), getValue(=A1+B2), getValue(=A1+10), output 7 and 12.

Example 2. Input the same calls, then getValue(=C3+C3), resetCell(A1), getValue(=A1+B2), getValue(=7+8), output 0, 2, 5 and 15.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. No formula is stored, so there is no cached value and no dependents map to repair, and the call parses its argument and reads the current cell values.

Hint

Does the sheet keep anything that has to change when a cell changes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The only stored state is a grid of integers, one per cell, so the grid is authoritative and nothing is derived. A formula passed to getValue is a question about the grid at the moment of the call. The method removes the leading =, splits the text at the +, and turns each side into a number. A side that starts with a letter is a cell name, so the method reads its column from the letter and its row from the digits and returns the stored number. Any other side is an integer literal.

No cached value exists, so a later setCell cannot leave a stale one, and the sheet needs no dependents map and no cycle check. The invariant is that the grid holds the latest number for each cell and getValue reads it at call time.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because a formula has two terms and each term parses in constant time.
  • Space is O(rows) for the grid of 26 columns.
import java.util.*;

public final class SpreadsheetQuerySolution {
    static final class Spreadsheet {
        private final int[][] grid;

        Spreadsheet(int rows) { grid = new int[rows + 1][26]; }

        /** Stores a number in O(1) time and space; the grid is the only state. */
        void setCell(String cell, int v) { grid[row(cell)][cell.charAt(0) - 'A'] = v; }

        /** Clears a cell and returns the number it held; O(1) time and space. */
        int resetCell(String cell) {
            int old = grid[row(cell)][cell.charAt(0) - 'A'];
            grid[row(cell)][cell.charAt(0) - 'A'] = 0;
            return old;
        }

        /**
         * Evaluates "=X+Y" against the current grid without storing anything.
         * Time: O(1).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the result uses the numbers present at call time.
         */
        int getValue(String formula) {
            // Drop the equals sign, then split the two terms at the plus sign.
            String[] terms = formula.substring(1).split("\\+");
            return term(terms[0]) + term(terms[1]);
        }

        private int row(String cell) { return Integer.parseInt(cell.substring(1)); }

        // A term starting with a letter is a cell name; any other term is a number.
        private int term(String t) {
            if (Character.isLetter(t.charAt(0))) return grid[row(t)][t.charAt(0) - 'A'];
            return Integer.parseInt(t);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        Spreadsheet s = new Spreadsheet(3);
        s.setCell("A1", 2);
        s.setCell("B2", 5);
        if (s.getValue("=A1+B2") != 7 || s.getValue("=A1+10") != 12) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        if (s.getValue("=C3+C3") != 0) throw new AssertionError("e2 unset");
        if (s.resetCell("A1") != 2) throw new AssertionError("e2 reset");
        if (s.getValue("=A1+B2") != 5 || s.getValue("=7+8") != 15) throw new AssertionError("e2 last");
        // A query must not leave anything behind: a later edit changes the next answer.
        s.setCell("A1", 100);
        if (s.getValue("=A1+B2") != 105) throw new AssertionError("fresh read");
        // Random calls checked against a map keyed by cell name and parsed with a pattern.
        Random rnd = new Random(3484);
        java.util.regex.Pattern p = java.util.regex.Pattern.compile("=([A-Z0-9]+)\\+([A-Z0-9]+)");
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            Spreadsheet sp = new Spreadsheet(20);
            Map<String, Integer> ref = new HashMap<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                String cell = "" + (char) ('A' + rnd.nextInt(26)) + (1 + rnd.nextInt(20));
                int t = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (t == 0) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(100001);
                    sp.setCell(cell, v); ref.put(cell, v);
                } else if (t == 1) {
                    if (sp.resetCell(cell) != ref.getOrDefault(cell, 0)) throw new AssertionError("reset");
                    ref.remove(cell);
                } else {
                    String a = rnd.nextBoolean() ? cell : String.valueOf(rnd.nextInt(100001));
                    String b = "" + (char) ('A' + rnd.nextInt(26)) + (1 + rnd.nextInt(20));
                    String f = "=" + a + "+" + b;
                    java.util.regex.Matcher m = p.matcher(f);
                    if (!m.matches()) throw new AssertionError("pattern");
                    int want = 0;
                    for (int g = 1; g <= 2; g++) {
                        String x = m.group(g);
                        want += Character.isLetter(x.charAt(0)) ? ref.getOrDefault(x, 0) : Integer.parseInt(x);
                    }
                    if (sp.getValue(f) != want) throw new AssertionError("value " + f);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Merge Feeds From Many Sources

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Timeline That Slows With Every Follow

A small messaging service shows each user the ten newest posts from the accounts that user follows. The first version works well in testing, where every account has a few dozen posts. After a month, one user follows 300 accounts that have posted 40,000 times in total. Opening the timeline now takes noticeable time, even though the screen shows only ten posts.

A second report arrives from support. A user deleted an account, and the posts of that account still appear in the timelines of its followers. A third report says the timeline of one user shows that user's own posts twice.

This lesson asks how to answer a timeline query by looking at a few posts, how to keep follow state and account lifecycle rules in step with the answer, and where a rule such as "skip deleted accounts" must run so the limit of ten still counts the right posts.

Simple approach

Collect Everything And Sort

The direct design gathers every post of every followed account into one list. It sorts that list by time from newest to oldest and returns the first k entries.

final class NaiveFeed {
    private final Map<Integer, List<int[]>> posts = new HashMap<>();   // author -> {time, id}
    private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> follows = new HashMap<>();
    private int clock = 0;

    void post(int user, int id) {
        posts.computeIfAbsent(user, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(new int[] {clock++, id});
    }

    void follow(int a, int b) { follows.computeIfAbsent(a, x -> new HashSet<>()).add(b); }

    List<Integer> feed(int user, int k) {
        List<int[]> all = new ArrayList<>(posts.getOrDefault(user, List.of()));
        for (int b : follows.getOrDefault(user, Set.of())) all.addAll(posts.getOrDefault(b, List.of()));
        all.sort((p, q) -> q[0] - p[0]);                 // sorts every post, however few are needed
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < Math.min(k, all.size()); i++) out.add(all.get(i)[1]);
        return out;
    }
}

The answers are correct while the follow rules stay trivial. The cost grows with the total number of posts, and the code has no place to say that a followed account was deleted or that a user follows themselves.

Predict first: A user follows 300 accounts with 40,000 posts in total and asks for the ten newest posts. How much work does the query above do, and how many of those posts does the answer actually need?

The query copies all 40,000 posts into one list and sorts them, which costs about 40,000 times the logarithm of 40,000 comparisons. The answer needs ten posts. Almost all of the copying and sorting is wasted, and the waste grows every time any followed account posts.

Bottleneck

Work Proportional To All Posts

The query cost is O(n log n) for n posts held by the followed accounts, and n grows without bound while k stays at ten. Sorting is not even needed, because each account already stores its own posts in time order. The collect-and-sort code throws that order away and rebuilds it.

A second cost hides in the rules. Suppose the code applies the limit first and the filter second. It takes the ten newest posts and then removes the posts of deleted accounts. The user may then see four posts although more than ten eligible posts exist. The filter must run before the limit, so the limit counts only posts the user is allowed to see.

Both costs point to the same fix. The query should read as few posts as the answer needs, and it should never read a post from an account that fails the rules.

Key idea

Read One Post Per Account

Every account stores its posts in time order, so the newest post of an account is the only post of that account that can come next in the answer. Keep one entry per eligible account, which is the frontier. The frontier entry for an account is that account's newest post that the query has not yet returned.

The Merge With A Heap

Store the frontier entries in a heap, a priority queue that returns the entry with the newest time in O(log f) for f entries. Remove the top entry and append its post to the answer. Then push the next older post of the same account, if one exists, as the new frontier entry of that account. After k removals the answer is complete. This is a k-way merge, and the query reads at most k posts plus one starting post per account.

Where The Rules Run

The rules decide which accounts get a frontier entry at all. A visibility check runs before the first push: it admits the user's own account once, admits each followed account that is still live and skips every other account. Because skipped accounts never enter the heap, the limit of k counts only visible posts. A deleted account needs no cleanup in the follow sets of its followers, since the check skips it each time.

The invariant is that the heap holds exactly one entry for each eligible account that still has unreturned posts, so the top entry is always the newest unreturned visible post.

State

State Behind One Timeline Query

The service keeps authoritative state and derived views apart, and each entry below has one owner.

  • Posts is the authoritative list of posts per author, each stored with its time, and a post call appends to it.
  • Follow set is the authoritative set of accounts each user follows, and follow and unfollow calls change it.
  • Deleted set is the authoritative record of accounts that no longer exist, and the delete call adds an id to it.
  • Heap is a local view built for one query, and it disappears when the query returns.
  • Clock is a counter that gives every post a unique time, so two posts never tie.
Walkthrough

Following Heap Pops And Service Calls

A Merge Of Three Streams

The first trace merges three accounts whose posts are stored newest first. The pointer pop marks the removal that happens at each step. The frontier lists the heap entries as time@account, and the output grows by one post per step.

At the second pop, account 1 contributes its next post only after its first post leaves the heap. The heap never holds more than three entries, one for each account.

A Service With A Deleted Account

The second trace follows calls on a service. Account 1 follows account 2, tries to follow itself, and then account 2 is deleted. The pointer call sits on the newest call, while the column follows shows the stored follow set of account 1.

The self-follow call returns false and changes nothing, so the post of account 1 appears once in the feed. After the deletion the follow set still holds account 2, which is a stale entry. The second feed call skips it through the visibility check, and the unfollow call finally removes the entry.

Code

The Merge With A Visibility Check

The class stores each author's posts in an ArrayList and merges only eligible authors.

final class FeedService {
    private final Map<Integer, List<int[]>> posts = new HashMap<>();   // author -> {time, id}, oldest first
    private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> follows = new HashMap<>();
    private final Set<Integer> deleted = new HashSet<>();
    private int clock = 0;

    void post(int user, int id) {
        posts.computeIfAbsent(user, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(new int[] {clock++, id});
    }

    List<Integer> feed(int user, int k) {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        if (deleted.contains(user)) return out;
        List<Integer> authors = new ArrayList<>();
        authors.add(user);                                            // own account once
        for (int b : follows.getOrDefault(user, Set.of()))
            if (b != user && !deleted.contains(b)) authors.add(b);    // visibility check before the heap
        PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((p, q) -> q[0] - p[0]);   // {time, id, author, index}
        for (int a : authors) {
            List<int[]> list = posts.getOrDefault(a, List.of());
            if (!list.isEmpty()) {
                int[] last = list.get(list.size() - 1);
                heap.add(new int[] {last[0], last[1], a, list.size() - 1});
            }
        }
        while (out.size() < k && !heap.isEmpty()) {                   // at most k pops
            int[] top = heap.poll();
            out.add(top[1]);
            if (top[3] > 0) {                                         // next older post of the same author
                int[] nxt = posts.get(top[2]).get(top[3] - 1);
                heap.add(new int[] {nxt[0], nxt[1], top[2], top[3] - 1});
            }
        }
        return out;
    }
}

A query costs O(f + k log f) for f eligible accounts. The space is O(f) for the heap and the author list.

When to use it

Queries That Return The Newest Few

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is a query that returns the newest or highest-ranked k items from several sources, where each source already holds its items in order. Examples are a timeline, a merged log from several files and the next events from several calendars. Name the sources, name the order inside each source, and name every rule that removes a source or an item. The invariant is that filtering runs before the limit, and that the heap holds one entry for each source that still has items.

Policies To State

Write down the tie policy, such as the smaller source index first when two items have equal time. Write down what a user following themselves means, what a follow of a deleted account returns, and whether ids can be reused. A policy stated in the contract becomes an assertion in the tests. A policy left unstated becomes an argument later.

False Friends

Sorting the union and cutting it at k looks like the same answer, and it is a false friend whenever the sources are large. Applying the limit first and the filter second is a false friend of the correct order, because the answer shrinks below k. Removing a deleted account from every follower set on delete looks tidy, yet it costs time proportional to the number of followers, while a check at query time costs nothing extra. A single source needs no heap, because its own list is already the answer.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Merge Recent User Streams

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Given m streams, where streams[s] is an array of integer timestamps in non-increasing order, and an integer k, return the k newest items across all streams. An item is the pair (s, p) of a stream index and a position in that stream. Item (s, p) is newer than (t, q) when its timestamp is larger, and when timestamps are equal, the item with the smaller stream index is newer. When both are in the same stream with equal timestamps, the smaller position is newer. Return the pairs from newest to oldest, or all items if fewer than k exist.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Streams number from 0 to 1000, and each stream has from 0 to 1000 timestamps.
  • Timestamps are integers from 0 to 10^9.
  • k is an integer from 0 to 10^5.
  • Return is a list of int[]{s, p} pairs.

Example 1. Input streams = [[9,6,2],[8,7],[5]], k = 4, output (0,0), (1,0), (1,1), (0,1).

Example 2. Input streams = [[5,5],[5],[]], k = 3, output (0,0), (0,1), (1,0).

Prerequisites. Priority queues and sorted arrays.

What This Exercise Changes. The heap holds one entry per stream, and the code pushes the next position of a stream only after that stream's current entry leaves.

Hint

Which single entry of each stream can be the next answer?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Each stream is already sorted, so the newest unreturned item of a stream is always the item at its current position. The heap holds one entry per stream that still has items, ordered by larger timestamp first and by smaller stream index when timestamps are equal. Each pop returns the newest remaining item overall. The code then pushes the next position of the popped stream, if there is one. Within one stream the next position always has a timestamp no larger than the popped one, and the heap holds only one entry per stream, so equal timestamps in one stream leave in position order.

The invariant is that the heap holds exactly the current position of every stream that has unreturned items. The answer is therefore complete after k pops.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m + k log m) for m streams, because building the heap costs one push per stream and each of the at most k pops costs O(log m).
  • Space is O(m) for the heap, plus O(k) for the returned list.
import java.util.*;

public final class MergeStreamsSolution {
    /**
     * Returns the k newest items of sorted streams as {stream, position} pairs.
     * Time: O(m + k log m).
     * Space: O(m + k).
     * Invariant: the heap holds the current position of each non-exhausted stream.
     */
    static List<int[]> newest(int[][] streams, int k) {
        List<int[]> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Larger timestamp first; the smaller stream index wins a tie.
        PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((p, q) -> {
            int tp = streams[p[0]][p[1]], tq = streams[q[0]][q[1]];
            return tp != tq ? Integer.compare(tq, tp) : Integer.compare(p[0], q[0]);
        });
        // One starting entry per non-empty stream.
        for (int s = 0; s < streams.length; s++) if (streams[s].length > 0) heap.add(new int[] {s, 0});
        // At most k pops, each costing O(log m).
        while (out.size() < k && !heap.isEmpty()) {
            int[] top = heap.poll();
            out.add(top);
            // The next position of the same stream becomes its new entry.
            if (top[1] + 1 < streams[top[0]].length) heap.add(new int[] {top[0], top[1] + 1});
        }
        return out;
    }

    static String show(List<int[]> r) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int[] p : r) sb.append('(').append(p[0]).append(',').append(p[1]).append(')');
        return sb.toString();
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        if (!show(newest(new int[][] {{9, 6, 2}, {8, 7}, {5}}, 4)).equals("(0,0)(1,0)(1,1)(0,1)")) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        if (!show(newest(new int[][] {{5, 5}, {5}, {}}, 3)).equals("(0,0)(0,1)(1,0)")) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random streams checked against sorting every item with the same tie rule.
        Random rnd = new Random(4101);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            int m = rnd.nextInt(6);
            int[][] st = new int[m][];
            for (int s = 0; s < m; s++) {
                st[s] = new int[rnd.nextInt(6)];
                for (int i = 0; i < st[s].length; i++) st[s][i] = rnd.nextInt(8);
                Arrays.sort(st[s]);
                for (int i = 0; i < st[s].length / 2; i++) { int t = st[s][i]; st[s][i] = st[s][st[s].length - 1 - i]; st[s][st[s].length - 1 - i] = t; }
            }
            int k = rnd.nextInt(20);
            List<int[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int s = 0; s < m; s++) for (int i = 0; i < st[s].length; i++) all.add(new int[] {s, i});
            all.sort((p, q) -> {
                int tp = st[p[0]][p[1]], tq = st[q[0]][q[1]];
                if (tp != tq) return Integer.compare(tq, tp);
                return p[0] != q[0] ? Integer.compare(p[0], q[0]) : Integer.compare(p[1], q[1]);
            });
            String want = show(all.subList(0, Math.min(k, all.size())));
            if (!show(newest(st, k)).equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("random " + round);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Design Twitter

LeetCode 355

Problem Specification. This contract is the chapter's own and differs from LeetCode in the feed size and the storage rule. Design a class Twitter. The call postTweet(userId, tweetId) records a tweet with the next time value. A user keeps only their five newest tweets, and older ones are dropped. The call follow(a, b) adds b to the followees of a, and unfollow(a, b) removes it. A call with a == b changes nothing. The call getNewsFeed(userId, k) returns up to k tweet ids, newest first, from the user's own tweets and the tweets of the user's followees.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Users are integers from 1 to 10^5, and tweet ids are distinct integers from 1 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and k is an integer from 1 to 100.
  • Followees of one user number at most 10^3.
  • Return of getNewsFeed is a list of ids, empty when no tweet is visible.

Example 1. Input calls postTweet(1,101), postTweet(2,201), postTweet(2,202), follow(1,2), getNewsFeed(1,2), output the list 202, 201.

Example 2. Input calls postTweet(1,1) to postTweet(1,7) in order, then getNewsFeed(1,10), follow(2,1), unfollow(2,1), getNewsFeed(2,3), output the list 7, 6, 5, 4, 3 and then the empty list.

Prerequisites. The merge exercise above and hash sets.

What This Exercise Changes. The newest five tweets bound each author's list, and the feed length is a parameter, so the merge stops after k pops and not after a fixed ten.

Hint

Which index of an author's list holds the newest tweet, and what is the next entry after it leaves the heap?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The authoritative state is a map from author to the list of that author's last five tweets, each stored with its post time, and a map from user to a set of followees. A post call appends to the author's list and drops the oldest entry when the list exceeds five. A follow call with a == b returns at once, so a user never appears in their own followee set and never contributes two sources.

A feed call builds the source list from the user and the followees, then runs the heap merge. Each heap entry names an author and an index into that author's list, starting at the newest index. A pop returns one tweet id and pushes the next older index of the same author.

The invariant is that the heap holds the newest unreturned tweet of each source that has one. The merge stops after k pops.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(f + k log f) per feed call for f followees, and O(1) for post, follow and unfollow, because each list has at most five entries.
  • Space is O(f) for the heap per query, and O(5u + e) overall for u authors and e follow edges.
import java.util.*;

public final class TwitterSolution {
    static final class Twitter {
        private static final int KEEP = 5;
        private final Map<Integer, ArrayList<int[]>> posts = new HashMap<>();   // author -> {time, id}, oldest first
        private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> follows = new HashMap<>();
        private int clock = 0;

        /** Records a tweet and keeps the newest five; O(1) time and space; invariant: list size at most KEEP. */
        void postTweet(int user, int id) {
            ArrayList<int[]> list = posts.computeIfAbsent(user, x -> new ArrayList<>());
            list.add(new int[] {clock++, id});
            // The list never exceeds five entries, so the removal costs O(1).
            if (list.size() > KEEP) list.remove(0);
        }

        // A self follow changes nothing; O(1) time and space.
        void follow(int a, int b) { if (a != b) follows.computeIfAbsent(a, x -> new HashSet<>()).add(b); }

        // Removing an absent edge is allowed and changes nothing; O(1) time and space.
        void unfollow(int a, int b) { if (follows.containsKey(a)) follows.get(a).remove(b); }

        /**
         * Returns up to k tweet ids, newest first, from the user and the followees.
         * Time: O(f + k log f).
         * Space: O(f).
         * Invariant: the heap holds the newest unreturned tweet of each source.
         */
        List<Integer> getNewsFeed(int user, int k) {
            List<Integer> authors = new ArrayList<>();
            authors.add(user);
            authors.addAll(follows.getOrDefault(user, Set.of()));
            // Entries are {time, id, author, index}; the newest time leaves first.
            PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((p, q) -> Integer.compare(q[0], p[0]));
            // One starting entry per source that has at least one tweet.
            for (int a : authors) {
                ArrayList<int[]> list = posts.get(a);
                if (list != null && !list.isEmpty()) {
                    int[] last = list.get(list.size() - 1);
                    heap.add(new int[] {last[0], last[1], a, list.size() - 1});
                }
            }
            List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
            // At most k pops.
            while (out.size() < k && !heap.isEmpty()) {
                int[] top = heap.poll();
                out.add(top[1]);
                // The next older tweet of the same author becomes its entry.
                if (top[3] > 0) {
                    int[] nxt = posts.get(top[2]).get(top[3] - 1);
                    heap.add(new int[] {nxt[0], nxt[1], top[2], top[3] - 1});
                }
            }
            return out;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Twitter a = new Twitter();
        a.postTweet(1, 101); a.postTweet(2, 201); a.postTweet(2, 202); a.follow(1, 2);
        if (!a.getNewsFeed(1, 2).equals(List.of(202, 201))) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        // Example 2: only five tweets remain, and an unfollow removes the source.
        Twitter b = new Twitter();
        for (int i = 1; i <= 7; i++) b.postTweet(1, i);
        if (!b.getNewsFeed(1, 10).equals(List.of(7, 6, 5, 4, 3))) throw new AssertionError("e2 cap");
        b.follow(2, 1); b.unfollow(2, 1);
        if (!b.getNewsFeed(2, 3).isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("e2 unfollow");
        // A self follow must not repeat the user's own tweets.
        Twitter c = new Twitter();
        c.postTweet(1, 9); c.follow(1, 1);
        if (!c.getNewsFeed(1, 5).equals(List.of(9))) throw new AssertionError("self follow");
        // Random calls checked against a full scan of all stored tweets.
        Random rnd = new Random(355);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Twitter s = new Twitter();
            List<int[]> log = new ArrayList<>();                        // {time, id, author}
            Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> fol = new HashMap<>();
            int nextId = 1;
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                int t = rnd.nextInt(5), u = rnd.nextInt(4), v = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (t <= 1) { s.postTweet(u, nextId); log.add(new int[] {log.size(), nextId, u}); nextId++; }
                else if (t == 2) { s.follow(u, v); if (u != v) fol.computeIfAbsent(u, x -> new HashSet<>()).add(v); }
                else if (t == 3) { s.unfollow(u, v); if (fol.containsKey(u)) fol.get(u).remove(v); }
                else {
                    int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
                    Set<Integer> src = new HashSet<>(fol.getOrDefault(u, Set.of()));
                    src.add(u);
                    List<int[]> keep = new ArrayList<>();
                    for (int a2 : src) {
                        List<int[]> mine = new ArrayList<>();
                        for (int[] p : log) if (p[2] == a2) mine.add(p);
                        keep.addAll(mine.subList(Math.max(0, mine.size() - 5), mine.size()));
                    }
                    keep.sort((p, q) -> Integer.compare(q[0], p[0]));
                    List<Integer> want = new ArrayList<>();
                    for (int i = 0; i < Math.min(k, keep.size()); i++) want.add(keep.get(i)[1]);
                    if (!s.getNewsFeed(u, k).equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("feed " + round);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Follow Self And Deleted Source

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class Network of users with integer ids. Every id is a live user until deleteUser(u) marks it deleted, and a deleted id never becomes live again. The call post(u, id) stores a tweet and returns true when u is live. The call follow(a, b) returns true and adds the edge when a and b are different, both are live and the edge is new. The call unfollow(a, b) returns true and removes the edge when a is live and the edge exists, even if b is deleted. The call deleteUser(u) returns true when u was live. The call feed(u, k) returns up to k tweet ids, newest first, from u and from the live followees of u, and returns an empty list when u is deleted. The call liveFollowees(u) returns the number of live followees of u.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Users are integers from 1 to 10^5, and tweet ids are distinct integers.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and k is an integer from 1 to 100.
  • Tweets are never removed from storage, and a deleted author's tweets stay hidden.
  • Return types are boolean for post, follow, unfollow and deleteUser, a list for feed and an int for liveFollowees.

Example 1. Input calls post(1,10), post(2,20), follow(1,2), follow(1,1), follow(1,2), feed(1,5), deleteUser(2), feed(1,5), liveFollowees(1), follow(1,2), unfollow(1,2), output true, true, true, false, false, the list 20, 10, then true, the list 10, 0, false and true.

Example 2. Input calls post(3,30), post(3,31), deleteUser(3), post(3,32), feed(3,5), deleteUser(3), output true, true, true, false, the empty list and false.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Follow edges to a deleted user stay stored and the feed skips them, so the delete call costs O(1) and the unfollow call must still accept a stale edge.

Hint

Where does the code check that an author is live, and does that place come before or after the limit of k?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The authoritative state has three parts: a list of tweets per author, a follow set per user and a set of deleted ids. Every rule is a check on those parts. A post call checks that the author is not deleted. A follow call rejects equal ids, deleted endpoints and an existing edge. An unfollow call checks only that the actor is live and that the edge exists, so a stale edge to a deleted user can still be removed. A delete call adds the id to the deleted set and touches nothing else.

The feed call applies the visibility check before any heap entry exists. It returns an empty list for a deleted user, admits the user once, and admits each followee that is not deleted.
