The followee count call counts the same admitted followees. Because deleted sources never enter the heap, the limit k counts only visible tweets.

The invariant is that every source in the heap is live and appears once. Follow sets may hold stale entries, and only the check at query time hides them.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for post, follow, unfollow and delete, O(f) for the followee count and O(f + k log f) for a feed call with f stored followees.
  • Space is O(t + e + d) for t tweets, e edges and d deleted ids, plus O(f) per query.
import java.util.*;

public final class FollowSelfDeletedSolution {
    static final class Network {
        private final Map<Integer, List<int[]>> posts = new HashMap<>();   // author -> {time, id}, oldest first
        private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> follows = new HashMap<>();
        private final Set<Integer> deleted = new HashSet<>();
        private int clock = 0;

        /** Stores a tweet for a live author; O(1) time and space; invariant: deleted authors gain no tweets. */
        boolean post(int u, int id) {
            if (deleted.contains(u)) return false;
            posts.computeIfAbsent(u, x -> new ArrayList<>()).add(new int[] {clock++, id});
            return true;
        }

        // Every precondition is checked before the single write; O(1) time and space.
        boolean follow(int a, int b) {
            if (a == b || deleted.contains(a) || deleted.contains(b)) return false;
            return follows.computeIfAbsent(a, x -> new HashSet<>()).add(b);   // false when the edge exists
        }

        // A stale edge to a deleted user is removable; O(1) time and space.
        boolean unfollow(int a, int b) {
            if (deleted.contains(a)) return false;
            Set<Integer> s = follows.get(a);
            return s != null && s.remove(b);
        }

        // Lazy delete: follow sets are not touched; O(1) time and space.
        boolean deleteUser(int u) { return deleted.add(u); }

        // Counts only live followees, so stale edges do not count; O(f) time, O(1) space.
        int liveFollowees(int u) {
            if (deleted.contains(u)) return 0;
            int n = 0;
            for (int b : follows.getOrDefault(u, Set.of())) if (!deleted.contains(b)) n++;
            return n;
        }

        /**
         * Returns up to k visible tweet ids, newest first.
         * Time: O(f + k log f).
         * Space: O(f).
         * Invariant: only live, distinct sources enter the heap.
         */
        List<Integer> feed(int u, int k) {
            List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
            if (deleted.contains(u)) return out;
            List<Integer> authors = new ArrayList<>();
            authors.add(u);
            // The visibility check runs here, before any heap entry exists.
            for (int b : follows.getOrDefault(u, Set.of())) if (!deleted.contains(b)) authors.add(b);
            PriorityQueue<int[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((p, q) -> Integer.compare(q[0], p[0]));
            for (int a : authors) {
                List<int[]> list = posts.get(a);
                if (list != null && !list.isEmpty()) {
                    int[] last = list.get(list.size() - 1);
                    heap.add(new int[] {last[0], last[1], a, list.size() - 1});
                }
            }
            // At most k pops.
            while (out.size() < k && !heap.isEmpty()) {
                int[] top = heap.poll();
                out.add(top[1]);
                if (top[3] > 0) {
                    int[] nxt = posts.get(top[2]).get(top[3] - 1);
                    heap.add(new int[] {nxt[0], nxt[1], top[2], top[3] - 1});
                }
            }
            return out;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Network n = new Network();
        boolean[] r1 = {n.post(1, 10), n.post(2, 20), n.follow(1, 2), n.follow(1, 1), n.follow(1, 2)};
        if (!r1[0] || !r1[1] || !r1[2] || r1[3] || r1[4]) throw new AssertionError("e1 calls");
        if (!n.feed(1, 5).equals(List.of(20, 10))) throw new AssertionError("e1 feed");
        if (!n.deleteUser(2) || !n.feed(1, 5).equals(List.of(10)) || n.liveFollowees(1) != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 delete");
        if (n.follow(1, 2) || !n.unfollow(1, 2)) throw new AssertionError("e1 stale");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Network m = new Network();
        boolean ok = m.post(3, 30) && m.post(3, 31) && m.deleteUser(3) && !m.post(3, 32);
        if (!ok || !m.feed(3, 5).isEmpty() || m.deleteUser(3)) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // The limit counts visible tweets only: a deleted source with newer tweets must not use up a slot.
        Network p = new Network();
        p.post(1, 1); p.post(2, 2); p.post(2, 3); p.follow(1, 2); p.deleteUser(2);
        if (!p.feed(1, 1).equals(List.of(1))) throw new AssertionError("limit before filter");
        // Random calls checked against a full scan with the same rules.
        Random rnd = new Random(4103);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            Network s = new Network();
            List<int[]> log = new ArrayList<>();                        // {time, id, author}
            Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> fol = new HashMap<>();
            Set<Integer> dead = new HashSet<>();
            int nextId = 1;
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                int t = rnd.nextInt(7), u = rnd.nextInt(4), v = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (t == 0 || t == 1) {
                    boolean want = !dead.contains(u);
                    if (want) { log.add(new int[] {log.size(), nextId, u}); }
                    if (s.post(u, nextId) != want) throw new AssertionError("post");
                    nextId++;
                } else if (t == 2) {
                    boolean want = u != v && !dead.contains(u) && !dead.contains(v) && !fol.getOrDefault(u, Set.of()).contains(v);
                    if (want) fol.computeIfAbsent(u, x -> new HashSet<>()).add(v);
                    if (s.follow(u, v) != want) throw new AssertionError("follow");
                } else if (t == 3) {
                    boolean want = !dead.contains(u) && fol.getOrDefault(u, Set.of()).contains(v);
                    if (want) fol.get(u).remove(v);
                    if (s.unfollow(u, v) != want) throw new AssertionError("unfollow");
                } else if (t == 4) {
                    if (s.deleteUser(u) != dead.add(u)) throw new AssertionError("delete");
                } else {
                    int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
                    List<Integer> want = new ArrayList<>();
                    int live = 0;
                    if (!dead.contains(u)) {
                        Set<Integer> src = new HashSet<>();
                        for (int b : fol.getOrDefault(u, Set.of())) if (!dead.contains(b)) { src.add(b); live++; }
                        src.add(u);
                        List<int[]> vis = new ArrayList<>();
                        for (int[] q : log) if (src.contains(q[2])) vis.add(q);
                        vis.sort((x, y) -> Integer.compare(y[0], x[0]));
                        for (int i = 0; i < Math.min(k, vis.size()); i++) want.add(vis.get(i)[1]);
                    }
                    if (!s.feed(u, k).equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("feed " + round);
                    if (s.liveFollowees(u) != live) throw new AssertionError("count");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Design Video Sharing Platform

LeetCode 2254

Problem Specification. This contract is written from the title and is the chapter's own, so it can differ from LeetCode. Design a class Videos. The call upload(video) stores a digit string and returns the smallest id not held by a stored video, counting from 0. The call remove(id) deletes a stored video and returns true, and the id becomes free. The call watch(id, start, end) returns the characters at positions start through min(end, length - 1) and adds one view, but it returns "-1" and changes nothing when the id is not stored or start >= length. The calls like(id) and dislike(id) add one to the matching counter of a stored video and do nothing otherwise. The call getLikesAndDislikes(id) returns [likes, dislikes], or [-1] for an unknown id. The call getViews(id) returns the view count, or -1 for an unknown id. A video that reuses a freed id starts with zero views, likes and dislikes.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Video strings have from 1 to 10^5 digits, and their total length is at most 10^5.
  • Ids are integers from 0 to 10^5, and positions satisfy 0 <= start <= end <= 10^5.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Return types are int for upload and getViews, String for watch, boolean for remove and int[] for getLikesAndDislikes.

Example 1. Input calls upload("123"), upload("456"), remove(0), upload("789"), watch(0,1,5), getViews(0), output 0, 1, true, 0, "89" and 1.

Example 2. Input calls upload("12"), watch(0,2,3), like(0), getLikesAndDislikes(0), remove(5), remove(0), getViews(0), output 0, "-1", then [1,0], false, true and -1.

Prerequisites. Priority queues and the three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. This is a transfer of the lesson: the id pool replaces the follow state and a removed video carries no stale index, so reuse must reset every counter. The contract is the chapter's own and need not match LeetCode.

Hint

Which structure returns the smallest freed id, and when does the counter for a new id advance?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The authoritative state is a map from id to a record of the video text and its three counters. The derived index is the id pool, made of a counter next for never-used ids and a min-heap of freed ids. An upload call takes the smallest freed id when the heap is not empty and otherwise takes next and advances it. This returns the smallest id that no stored video holds, because every id below next is either stored or in the freed heap.

A remove call deletes the record and pushes the id onto the freed heap, and it rejects an id that is not stored. A reused id gets a fresh record, so the counters of the earlier video never leak into the new one. A watch call validates the id and start before it writes, then adds one view and returns the substring. Like and dislike calls change one counter of a stored record.

The invariant is that each id is either a key of the record map or in the freed heap or at least next, and never in two of these places.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) for upload and remove because of the heap, and O(L) for a watch call that returns L characters. Likes, dislikes and views take O(1).
  • Space is O(T + n), where T is the total length of the stored texts and n is the number of ids ever issued.
import java.util.*;

public final class VideoPlatformSolution {
    static final class Videos {
        private static final class Video {
            final String text; int views, likes, dislikes;
            Video(String text) { this.text = text; }
        }
        private final Map<Integer, Video> store = new HashMap<>();            // id -> record, source of truth
        private final PriorityQueue<Integer> freed = new PriorityQueue<>();   // derived: reusable ids
        private int next = 0;

        /** Stores a video under the smallest unused id; O(log n) time, O(1) extra space; invariant: ids are never shared. */
        int upload(String text) {
            int id = freed.isEmpty() ? next++ : freed.poll();
            store.put(id, new Video(text));
            return id;
        }

        // The id returns to the pool only when it was stored; O(log n) time, O(1) space.
        boolean remove(int id) {
            if (store.remove(id) == null) return false;
            freed.add(id);
            return true;
        }

        /** Returns the requested slice and counts a view; O(L) time for L returned characters, O(L) space. */
        String watch(int id, int start, int end) {
            Video v = store.get(id);
            // Both preconditions are checked before any counter changes.
            if (v == null || start >= v.text.length()) return "-1";
            v.views++;
            return v.text.substring(start, Math.min(end, v.text.length() - 1) + 1);
        }

        void like(int id) { Video v = store.get(id); if (v != null) v.likes++; }          // O(1)
        void dislike(int id) { Video v = store.get(id); if (v != null) v.dislikes++; }    // O(1)

        int[] getLikesAndDislikes(int id) {
            Video v = store.get(id);
            return v == null ? new int[] {-1} : new int[] {v.likes, v.dislikes};         // O(1)
        }

        int getViews(int id) { Video v = store.get(id); return v == null ? -1 : v.views; }   // O(1)
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Videos a = new Videos();
        if (a.upload("123") != 0 || a.upload("456") != 1 || !a.remove(0) || a.upload("789") != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 ids");
        if (!a.watch(0, 1, 5).equals("89") || a.getViews(0) != 1) throw new AssertionError("e1 watch");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Videos b = new Videos();
        if (b.upload("12") != 0 || !b.watch(0, 2, 3).equals("-1")) throw new AssertionError("e2 watch");
        b.like(0);
        if (!Arrays.equals(b.getLikesAndDislikes(0), new int[] {1, 0}) || b.remove(5) || !b.remove(0) || b.getViews(0) != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // A failed watch must not add a view, and a reused id starts clean.
        Videos c = new Videos();
        c.upload("99"); c.watch(0, 5, 6); c.like(0); c.dislike(0);
        if (c.getViews(0) != 0) throw new AssertionError("failed watch counted");
        c.watch(0, 0, 0); c.remove(0); c.upload("7");
        if (c.getViews(0) != 0 || !Arrays.equals(c.getLikesAndDislikes(0), new int[] {0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("stale counters");
        // Random calls checked against a list of slots where the oracle scans for the first empty slot.
        Random rnd = new Random(2254);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            Videos s = new Videos();
            List<String> text = new ArrayList<>();
            List<int[]> cnt = new ArrayList<>();                         // {views, likes, dislikes}
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                int t = rnd.nextInt(7), id = rnd.nextInt(6);
                if (t == 0 || t == 1) {
                    StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
                    for (int i = 0, n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5); i < n; i++) sb.append(rnd.nextInt(10));
                    int slot = text.indexOf(null);
                    if (slot < 0) { text.add(sb.toString()); cnt.add(new int[3]); slot = text.size() - 1; }
                    else { text.set(slot, sb.toString()); cnt.set(slot, new int[3]); }
                    if (s.upload(sb.toString()) != slot) throw new AssertionError("upload id");
                } else {
                    boolean live = id < text.size() && text.get(id) != null;
                    if (t == 2) {
                        if (s.remove(id) != live) throw new AssertionError("remove");
                        if (live) text.set(id, null);
                    } else if (t == 3) {
                        int st = rnd.nextInt(6), en = st + rnd.nextInt(6);
                        String want = "-1";
                        if (live && st < text.get(id).length()) { want = text.get(id).substring(st, Math.min(en, text.get(id).length() - 1) + 1); cnt.get(id)[0]++; }
                        if (!s.watch(id, st, en).equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("watch");
                    } else if (t == 4) { s.like(id); if (live) cnt.get(id)[1]++; }
                    else if (t == 5) { s.dislike(id); if (live) cnt.get(id)[2]++; }
                    else {
                        int[] wantLd = live ? new int[] {cnt.get(id)[1], cnt.get(id)[2]} : new int[] {-1};
                        if (!Arrays.equals(s.getLikesAndDislikes(id), wantLd)) throw new AssertionError("likes");
                        if (s.getViews(id) != (live ? cnt.get(id)[0] : -1)) throw new AssertionError("views");
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Apply Transactions Without Partial Changes

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Transfer That Loses Money

A bank service keeps one balance per account. A client asks to move 40 from account 2 to account 9. The code subtracts 40 from account 2 and then looks up account 9. Account 9 does not exist, so the call returns false.

The caller sees a refusal and assumes nothing happened. In fact account 2 is now 40 poorer, and no other account is richer. The money has left the system without any record.

Ride systems, order counters and card authorizations show the same failure. Each public call touches several pieces of state, and a refusal that arrives after the first write leaves a mix of old and new values. This lesson asks how to write such a call so that a refused call changes nothing, and so that every counter still agrees with the records it summarizes.

The contract is small and in memory. One object holds all state in hash maps and arrays, one call runs at a time, and nothing is saved to disk.

Simple approach

Writing While Still Checking

The direct design follows the order of the story. It takes money from the source, then looks for the destination, and it reports a refusal wherever it finds one.

final class NaiveBank {
    private final long[] balance;

    NaiveBank(long[] start) { balance = start.clone(); }

    boolean transfer(int from, int to, long money) {
        if (from < 1 || from > balance.length) return false;
        balance[from - 1] -= money;                 // writes before every check has run
        if (to < 1 || to > balance.length) return false;   // refusal after the write
        if (balance[from - 1] < 0) return false;    // refusal after the write
        balance[to - 1] += money;
        return true;
    }

    long balance(int account) { return balance[account - 1]; }
}

The success path is correct. Each refusal after the first write leaves the source account changed.

Predict first: The bank has accounts 1 to 3 with balances 10, 100 and 20. The call transfer(2, 9, 40) arrives. What does account 2 hold afterwards, and what should it hold?

The source check passes, so the code subtracts 40 and account 2 holds 60. The destination check then fails and the call returns false. Account 2 should still hold 100, because a refused call must change nothing.

Bottleneck

Every Refusal Point After A Write

The cost here is correctness. The code does O(1) work per call, and the damage comes from the order of the steps. The call writes the source balance before it knows that the destination exists and that the source can pay.

Each refusal that follows a write creates one wrong state. A call with three checks and two writes has up to three such states. A real service has more: a trip service updates an active map and a route total, and an order counter updates a count and a price. Each extra write multiplies the mixed states that a failure can leave.

Repairing a mixed state afterwards means remembering every earlier write and undoing it in reverse order. That repair code runs rarely, so it hides its own bugs. The cheaper path is to read first, decide, and write only when every check has passed. A duplicate id, an unknown account and an overdraft are all checks of the same kind, so one early block can handle them together.

Key idea

Check Everything, Then Write Everything

Split every public call into two blocks. The first block reads state and tests all preconditions of the call. A precondition is a fact that must hold before the call may change anything, such as "the account exists" or "the id is not already active". The block writes nothing except local variables. When a test fails, the call returns its failure value and the object is exactly as it was.

The Commit Step

The second block is the commit. It applies every change of the call in one run, and it has no failure path. Every test that could fail already ran in the first block. A reader of the object therefore never sees a state that is half old and half new.

One Record And Its Summaries

Every service of this kind holds two layers. The ledger is the authoritative record of what happened: balances, active trips, open authorizations. Counters such as a route total, a held amount or an order count are derived from the ledger, and they exist only to answer queries in O(1). The commit writes the ledger entry and every derived counter in the same block, so a counter never describes an older ledger.

The invariant is that each accepted call moves the object from one complete state to the next, and each refused call leaves the state unchanged. The target cost is O(1) per call for hash-map state, plus O(log n) when a call also touches an ordered index.

State

State Read And Written By One Call

Each call involves four kinds of data, and each has a fixed moment of change.

  • Ledger entry is the authoritative record, such as a balance or an active trip, and it changes only in the commit.
  • Summary is a derived counter or total, and it changes in the same commit as its ledger entry.
  • Verdict is the boolean or failure code that the first block produces before any write.
  • Local values are the amounts and sums computed during the checks, and they die when the call returns.
Walkthrough

Following Checks And Commits

Transfers With Refusals

The first trace runs a bank with three accounts that start at 10, 100 and 20. The pointer call marks the latest call, and the balances appear in account order.

The second call names account 4, which does not exist, so the first block refuses it before any write. The fifth call asks for 30 from an account that holds 25, and it is refused for the same reason.

Trips With A Stale Check-Out

The second trace follows an underground service. A rider id may hold at most one open trip, and each finished trip adds its duration to a route total. The column stats lists route:total/count.

The second call reuses a rider id that is already inside a trip, so it changes nothing. The fifth call tries to check out a rider whose trip is not open, and it leaves both the active map and the stats untouched.

Code

The Bank With Checks Before Writes

The class runs every test first and writes the two balances only after the last test passes.

final class SafeBank {
    private final long[] balance;

    SafeBank(long[] start) { balance = start.clone(); }

    private boolean exists(int account) { return account >= 1 && account <= balance.length; }

    boolean transfer(int from, int to, long money) {
        if (!exists(from) || !exists(to)) return false;   // block one: reads only
        if (balance[from - 1] < money) return false;      // block one: reads only
        balance[from - 1] -= money;                       // block two: cannot fail
        balance[to - 1] += money;
        return true;
    }

    long balance(int account) { return balance[account - 1]; }
}

A call costs O(1) time and the object uses O(n) space for n accounts. A transfer from an account to itself passes both tests and leaves the balance unchanged.

When to use it

Calls That Change Several Values

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is a public call that changes more than one value, or that pairs an opening call with a closing call. List every ledger entry and every summary that the call writes. If a refusal is possible, move all tests before the first write. The invariant to state is that each accepted call produces one complete next state and each refused call produces none.

Rejecting Reused Ids

Many services reject a call because an id is already inside a lifecycle, such as a rider already checked in or an authorization still open. The test reads the active map, and it passes again once the lifecycle ends and the entry leaves the map. An id that finished is free to start again.

False Friends

A try-catch block around the writes looks like protection. It is a false friend when the catch block cannot restore the old values after some writes already ran. A lock protects against other callers, and it does not make a call all-or-nothing against its own refusal. Skip the two blocks only when a call writes exactly one value, because one write cannot be left half done.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Simple Bank System

LeetCode 2043

Problem Specification. Design a class Bank built from a long array balance, where balance[i - 1] is the starting balance of account i. The call transfer(from, to, money) moves money from one account to another and returns true, or returns false and changes nothing. The call deposit(account, money) adds money and returns true, or returns false for an unknown account. The call withdraw(account, money) subtracts money and returns true, or returns false for an unknown account or a balance below money. A transfer fails for an unknown account or when the source balance is below money, and a transfer from an account to itself is legal when the balance covers it. The call balance(account) is added by this chapter and returns the current balance of a known account.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Accounts number from 1 to 10^5, and account numbers are integers from 1 to the account count.
  • Balances and money are integers from 0 to 10^12, and totals fit in a long.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and a call may name an account outside the valid range.
  • Return is boolean for the three money calls and long for balance.

Example 1. Input balances [10,100,20,50,50], then transfer(5,1,20), transfer(3,4,25), withdraw(3,20), balance(1), balance(3), output true, false, true, 30 and 0.

Example 2. Input the same balances, then transfer(2,6,5), deposit(0,5), transfer(2,2,100), balance(2), output false, false, true and 100.

Prerequisites. Arrays and one-based indexing.

What This Exercise Changes. Both accounts and the source balance are tested first, so the two balance writes happen only after every refusal has been ruled out.

Hint

Which two accounts must you look at before any balance changes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A long array holds the balances, and account i lives at index i - 1. A helper tests whether an account number lies in the range 1 to the array length. The transfer call tests both account numbers and then tests that the source balance covers the amount. Only after those tests does it subtract from the source and add to the destination. Deposit and withdraw follow the same shape with one account.

The invariant is that every balance write sits after the last test of its call. A refused call therefore changes no balance, and the sum of all balances changes only through deposit and withdraw.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because each call does a fixed number of array reads and writes.
  • Space is O(n) for n accounts, because the array holds one balance per account.
import java.util.*;

public final class BankTransferSolution {
    static final class Bank {
        private final long[] balance;

        Bank(long[] start) { balance = start.clone(); }

        private boolean exists(int account) { return account >= 1 && account <= balance.length; }

        /**
         * Moves money between two accounts, or changes nothing.
         * Time: O(1).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: both writes run only after every test has passed.
         */
        boolean transfer(int from, int to, long money) {
            // Unknown accounts are refused before any read of a balance.
            if (!exists(from) || !exists(to)) return false;
            // An overdraft is refused before any write.
            if (balance[from - 1] < money) return false;
            // The commit cannot fail; a self transfer nets to zero.
            balance[from - 1] -= money;
            balance[to - 1] += money;
            return true;
        }

        /** Adds money to a known account; O(1) time and space; invariant: unknown accounts stay unwritten. */
        boolean deposit(int account, long money) {
            // The only refusal is an unknown account.
            if (!exists(account)) return false;
            balance[account - 1] += money;
            return true;
        }

        /** Removes money from a known account that covers it; O(1) time and space; invariant: balances never go below zero. */
        boolean withdraw(int account, long money) {
            // Both refusals are tested before the single write.
            if (!exists(account) || balance[account - 1] < money) return false;
            balance[account - 1] -= money;
            return true;
        }

        long balance(int account) { return balance[account - 1]; }
    }

    /** Oracle: applies the call to a copy, and keeps the copy only when no balance went negative. */
    static final class Oracle {
        long[] b;
        Oracle(long[] s) { b = s.clone(); }
        boolean apply(int kind, int x, int y, long m) {
            long[] c = b.clone();
            int n = c.length;
            if (kind == 0) {
                if (x < 1 || x > n || y < 1 || y > n) return false;
                c[x - 1] -= m;
                if (c[x - 1] < 0) return false;
                c[y - 1] += m;
            } else if (kind == 1) {
                if (x < 1 || x > n) return false;
                c[x - 1] += m;
            } else {
                if (x < 1 || x > n) return false;
                c[x - 1] -= m;
            }
            for (long v : c) if (v < 0) return false;
            b = c;
            return true;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        Bank e = new Bank(new long[] {10, 100, 20, 50, 50});
        if (!e.transfer(5, 1, 20)) throw new AssertionError("e1 a");
        if (e.transfer(3, 4, 25)) throw new AssertionError("e1 b");
        if (!e.withdraw(3, 20)) throw new AssertionError("e1 c");
        if (e.balance(1) != 30 || e.balance(3) != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 balances");
        Bank f = new Bank(new long[] {10, 100, 20, 50, 50});
        if (f.transfer(2, 6, 5) || f.deposit(0, 5)) throw new AssertionError("e2 refusals");
        if (!f.transfer(2, 2, 100) || f.balance(2) != 100) throw new AssertionError("e2 self transfer");
        // A refused transfer must leave every balance as it was.
        Bank g = new Bank(new long[] {10, 100, 20});
        if (g.transfer(2, 9, 40) || g.balance(2) != 100) throw new AssertionError("refusal changed state");
        // Random calls against the copy-and-discard oracle, with total conservation for transfers.
        Random rnd = new Random(2043);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            long[] start = new long[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) start[i] = rnd.nextInt(30);
            Bank s = new Bank(start);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(start);
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                int x = rnd.nextInt(n + 3) - 1, y = rnd.nextInt(n + 3) - 1;
                long m = rnd.nextInt(40);
                long before = 0;
                for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) before += s.balance(i);
                boolean got = kind == 0 ? s.transfer(x, y, m) : kind == 1 ? s.deposit(x, m) : s.withdraw(x, m);
                boolean want = o.apply(kind, x, y, m);
                if (got != want) throw new AssertionError("verdict " + kind);
                long after = 0;
                for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
                    if (s.balance(i) != o.b[i - 1]) throw new AssertionError("balance drift");
                    after += s.balance(i);
                }
                if (kind == 0 && before != after) throw new AssertionError("transfer changed the total");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Design Underground System

LeetCode 1396

Problem Specification. This contract is written for this chapter and differs from the usual statement. Design a class Metro. The call checkIn(id, station, t) opens a trip for rider id and returns true, or returns false if that rider already has an open trip. The call checkOut(id, station, t) closes the open trip of the rider and returns true. It returns false and changes nothing when the rider has no open trip or when t is below the check-in time. A closed trip adds t - checkInTime to the statistics of the route from the check-in station to the check-out station. The call getAverage(start, end) returns the total duration divided by the trip count, rounded down, or -1 if the route has no trip. The call maxTrip(start, end) returns the longest duration on the route, or -1 if the route has no trip.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Rider ids are integers from 1 to 10^6, and stations are lowercase strings of length 1 to 10.
  • Times are integers from 0 to 10^9, and calls need not arrive in time order.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5.
  • Return is boolean for the two trip calls and long for the two route queries.

Example 1. Input calls checkIn(1,a,3), checkIn(1,b,5), checkIn(2,a,4), checkOut(1,b,10), checkOut(2,b,12), getAverage(a,b), maxTrip(a,b), output true, false, true, true, true, 7 and 8.

Example 2. Input calls checkIn(4,c,10), checkOut(4,a,9), checkOut(4,a,15), checkOut(4,a,16), getAverage(c,a), getAverage(a,c), output true, false, true, false, 5 and -1.

Prerequisites. The bank exercise above and hash maps.

What This Exercise Changes. A check-out is refused before any write when the trip is missing or the time is earlier, and a refused check-out keeps the rider's trip open. The route also keeps a maximum, and the average rounds down.

Hint

What must be true about the rider before the check-out may touch the route totals?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from rider id to an immutable open-trip record holds the authoritative active state. A second hash map from route to an immutable statistics record holds the derived totals: duration sum, trip count and maximum. A route key is a record of the two station names, so two routes never collide.

Check-in tests that the id is absent from the active map and then stores the trip. Check-out reads the open trip first and refuses when it is missing or when the time is earlier than the check-in time. Only then does it remove the trip and replace the route statistics with a merged record. A refused check-out leaves the trip open, so the rider can check out again with a valid time.

The invariant is that the statistics of each route equal the sum over all closed trips of that route, and each rider id appears in the active map at most once. Both facts hold because one block removes the open trip and updates the statistics together.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per call, because each call does a fixed number of hash-map operations.
  • Space is O(a + r), where a is the number of open trips and r is the number of routes with at least one closed trip.
import java.util.*;

public final class RouteStatisticsSolution {
    static final class Metro {
        private record Open(String station, long time) {}
        private record Route(String from, String to) {}
        private record Stat(long total, long count, long max) {}
        private final Map<Integer, Open> active = new HashMap<>();   // authoritative open trips
        private final Map<Route, Stat> stats = new HashMap<>();      // derived route totals

        /**
         * Opens a trip unless the rider already has one.
         * Time: O(1) expected.
         * Space: O(1) per open trip.
         * Invariant: a rider id appears in the active map at most once.
         */
        boolean checkIn(int id, String station, long t) {
            // An id that is already open is refused before any write.
            if (active.containsKey(id)) return false;
            active.put(id, new Open(station, t));
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Closes the open trip of a rider and merges its duration into the route.
         * Time: O(1) expected.
         * Space: O(1) per route.
         * Invariant: the trip leaves the active map in the same block that updates the route.
         */
        boolean checkOut(int id, String station, long t) {
            Open trip = active.get(id);
            // A missing trip and an earlier time are both refused before any write.
            if (trip == null || t < trip.time()) return false;
            long d = t - trip.time();
            // The commit removes the trip and replaces the immutable statistics record.
            active.remove(id);
            stats.merge(new Route(trip.station(), station), new Stat(d, 1, d),
                (a, b) -> new Stat(a.total() + b.total(), a.count() + b.count(), Math.max(a.max(), b.max())));
            return true;
        }

        /** Returns the rounded-down average duration of a route, or -1; O(1) expected time and space. */
        long getAverage(String start, String end) {
            Stat s = stats.get(new Route(start, end));
            // An unknown route has no trips, so it reports -1.
            return s == null ? -1 : s.total() / s.count();
        }

        /** Returns the longest duration of a route, or -1; O(1) expected time and space. */
        long maxTrip(String start, String end) {
            Stat s = stats.get(new Route(start, end));
            return s == null ? -1 : s.max();
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: keeps every closed trip in a list and scans the list for each query. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final List<int[]> open = new ArrayList<>();            // {id, stationIndex, time}
        final List<long[]> done = new ArrayList<>();           // {from, to, duration}
        boolean in(int id, int st, long t) {
            for (int[] o : open) if (o[0] == id) return false;
            open.add(new int[] {id, st, (int) t});
            return true;
        }
        boolean out(int id, int st, long t) {
            for (int i = 0; i < open.size(); i++) {
                int[] o = open.get(i);
                if (o[0] != id) continue;
                if (t < o[2]) return false;
                done.add(new long[] {o[1], st, t - o[2]});
                open.remove(i);
                return true;
            }
            return false;
        }
        long avg(int a, int b) {
            long sum = 0, cnt = 0;
            for (long[] d : done) if (d[0] == a && d[1] == b) { sum += d[2]; cnt++; }
            return cnt == 0 ? -1 : sum / cnt;
        }
        long max(int a, int b) {
            long best = -1;
            for (long[] d : done) if (d[0] == a && d[1] == b) best = Math.max(best, d[2]);
            return best;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Metro m = new Metro();
        boolean[] r1 = {m.checkIn(1, "a", 3), m.checkIn(1, "b", 5), m.checkIn(2, "a", 4), m.checkOut(1, "b", 10), m.checkOut(2, "b", 12)};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r1, new boolean[] {true, false, true, true, true})) throw new AssertionError("e1 verdicts");
        if (m.getAverage("a", "b") != 7 || m.maxTrip("a", "b") != 8) throw new AssertionError("e1 stats");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Metro n = new Metro();
        boolean[] r2 = {n.checkIn(4, "c", 10), n.checkOut(4, "a", 9), n.checkOut(4, "a", 15), n.checkOut(4, "a", 16)};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r2, new boolean[] {true, false, true, false})) throw new AssertionError("e2 verdicts");
        if (n.getAverage("c", "a") != 5 || n.getAverage("a", "c") != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2 stats");
        // A refused check-out keeps the trip open, so a later valid check-out succeeds.
        Metro k = new Metro();
        k.checkIn(1, "x", 10);
        if (k.checkOut(1, "y", 5) || !k.checkOut(1, "y", 12) || k.getAverage("x", "y") != 2) throw new AssertionError("open trip lost");
        // Random calls against the list-scanning oracle, including early check-outs and repeated ids.
        Random rnd = new Random(1396);
        String[] names = {"a", "b", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Metro s = new Metro();
            Oracle o = new Oracle();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                int id = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4), st = rnd.nextInt(3);
                long t = rnd.nextInt(30);
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (kind == 0) { if (s.checkIn(id, names[st], t) != o.in(id, st, t)) throw new AssertionError("checkIn"); }
                else if (kind == 1) { if (s.checkOut(id, names[st], t) != o.out(id, st, t)) throw new AssertionError("checkOut"); }
                else {
                    int a = rnd.nextInt(3), b = rnd.nextInt(3);
                    if (s.getAverage(names[a], names[b]) != o.avg(a, b)) throw new AssertionError("average");
                    if (s.maxTrip(names[a], names[b]) != o.max(a, b)) throw new AssertionError("max");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Duplicate Active Transaction

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class Holds built from a long array balance, where balance[i - 1] is the balance of account i. The call authorize(txId, account, amount) reserves amount on an account and returns true. It returns false and changes nothing if txId is already active, the account is unknown, or amount exceeds the available amount of the account. The available amount is the balance minus the sum of the account's active reservations. The call settle(txId) removes the reservation and lowers the balance by its amount. The call release(txId) removes the reservation and leaves the balance unchanged. Both calls return true, or return false for an id that is not active. The call available(account) returns the available amount of a known account.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Accounts number from 1 to 10^4, and balances are integers from 0 to 10^12.
  • Transaction ids are integers from 1 to 10^9, and amounts are integers from 1 to 10^12.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and an id may start again after it was settled or released.
  • Return is boolean for the three transaction calls and long for available.

Example 1. Input balances [100,50], then authorize(7,1,60), authorize(7,2,10), authorize(8,1,50), available(1), settle(7), authorize(7,2,10), available(2), output true, false, false, 40, true, true and 40.

Example 2. Input the same balances, then authorize(1,2,50), release(1), release(1), available(2), authorize(1,3,5), output true, true, false, 50 and false.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The same id is refused while it is active, even for a different account, and accepted again after it ends. Both ends of the lifecycle update the reservation map and the held total together.

Hint

Which map answers whether an id is active, and when does an entry leave it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The balance array and a hash map from transaction id to an immutable reservation hold the authoritative state. A second long array stores the held total per account as a derived summary, so available is a subtraction. Authorize tests the id against the active map first, then the account range, then the available amount. Only then does it store the reservation and raise the held total.

Settle and release read the reservation first and refuse an id that is not active. Settle lowers the balance and the held total by the amount, and release lowers only the held total. Both remove the map entry in the same block. Because the entry leaves the map, the same id can start again later. While the entry stays in the map, even a request for a different account is refused.

The invariant is that the held total of each account equals the sum of its active reservations, and available never goes below zero.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per call, because each call does a fixed number of hash-map and array operations.
  • Space is O(n + a) for n accounts and a active reservations.
import java.util.*;

public final class DuplicateActiveSolution {
    static final class Holds {
        private record Hold(int account, long amount) {}
        private final long[] balance;                          // authoritative balances
        private final long[] held;                             // derived total of active holds per account
        private final Map<Integer, Hold> active = new HashMap<>();   // authoritative active reservations

        Holds(long[] start) {
            balance = start.clone();
            held = new long[start.length];
        }

        /**
         * Reserves an amount unless the id is active, the account is unknown or funds are short.
         * Time: O(1) expected.
         * Space: O(1) per reservation.
         * Invariant: held[a] equals the sum of the active holds of account a.
         */
        boolean authorize(int txId, int account, long amount) {
            // A reused active id is refused first, whatever its account.
            if (active.containsKey(txId)) return false;
            // An unknown account is refused before any array read.
            if (account < 1 || account > balance.length) return false;
            // An amount above the available funds is refused before any write.
            if (balance[account - 1] - held[account - 1] < amount) return false;
            // The commit writes the entry and the summary together.
            active.put(txId, new Hold(account, amount));
            held[account - 1] += amount;
            return true;
        }

        /** Finalizes a reservation and lowers the balance; O(1) expected time and space; invariant: entry, hold and balance move together. */
        boolean settle(int txId) {
            Hold h = active.get(txId);
            // An id that is not active is refused.
            if (h == null) return false;
            active.remove(txId);
            held[h.account() - 1] -= h.amount();
            balance[h.account() - 1] -= h.amount();
            return true;
        }

        /** Cancels a reservation and keeps the balance; O(1) expected time and space; invariant: entry and hold leave together. */
        boolean release(int txId) {
            Hold h = active.get(txId);
            if (h == null) return false;
            active.remove(txId);
            held[h.account() - 1] -= h.amount();
            return true;
        }

        long available(int account) { return balance[account - 1] - held[account - 1]; }
    }

    /** Oracle: stores reservations in a list and recomputes the available amount by scanning it. */
    static final class Oracle {
        final long[] bal;
        final List<long[]> list = new ArrayList<>();   // {txId, account, amount}
        Oracle(long[] s) { bal = s.clone(); }
        long avail(int a) {
            long v = bal[a - 1];
            for (long[] h : list) if (h[1] == a) v -= h[2];
            return v;
        }
        boolean authorize(int tx, int a, long amt) {
            for (long[] h : list) if (h[0] == tx) return false;
            if (a < 1 || a > bal.length) return false;
            if (avail(a) < amt) return false;
            list.add(new long[] {tx, a, amt});
            return true;
        }
        boolean end(int tx, boolean pay) {
            for (int i = 0; i < list.size(); i++) {
                if (list.get(i)[0] != tx) continue;
                long[] h = list.remove(i);
                if (pay) bal[(int) h[1] - 1] -= h[2];
                return true;
            }
            return false;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Holds h = new Holds(new long[] {100, 50});
        boolean[] r1 = {h.authorize(7, 1, 60), h.authorize(7, 2, 10), h.authorize(8, 1, 50)};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r1, new boolean[] {true, false, false}) || h.available(1) != 40) throw new AssertionError("e1 first part");
        if (!h.settle(7) || !h.authorize(7, 2, 10) || h.available(2) != 40) throw new AssertionError("e1 reuse after settle");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Holds g = new Holds(new long[] {100, 50});
        if (!g.authorize(1, 2, 50) || !g.release(1) || g.release(1)) throw new AssertionError("e2 release");
        if (g.available(2) != 50 || g.authorize(1, 3, 5)) throw new AssertionError("e2 tail");
        // A refused duplicate leaves the first reservation in place.
        Holds d = new Holds(new long[] {100});
        d.authorize(5, 1, 30);
        if (d.authorize(5, 1, 10) || d.available(1) != 70) throw new AssertionError("duplicate changed state");
        // Random calls against the list-scanning oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(7043);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
            long[] start = new long[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) start[i] = rnd.nextInt(60);
            Holds s = new Holds(start);
            Oracle o = new Oracle(start);
            for (int op = 0; op < 70; op++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(3), tx = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
                boolean got, want;
                if (kind == 0) {
                    int a = rnd.nextInt(n + 2);
                    long amt = 1 + rnd.nextInt(40);
                    got = s.authorize(tx, a, amt);
                    want = o.authorize(tx, a, amt);
                } else {
                    got = kind == 1 ? s.settle(tx) : s.release(tx);
                    want = o.end(tx, kind == 1);
                }
                if (got != want) throw new AssertionError("verdict " + kind);
                for (int a = 1; a <= n; a++) if (s.available(a) != o.avail(a)) throw new AssertionError("available drift");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Apply Discount Every n Orders

LeetCode 1357

Problem Specification. The rules below are this chapter's own wording and may differ from LeetCode in detail. Design a class Cashier built from n, a percentage discount, an array products of distinct ids and an array prices of integer prices. The call getBill(product, amount) receives parallel arrays of order lines and returns the bill as a long. The bill is the sum of price * amount over the lines. If any product id is not in the catalog, the call returns -1 and the order does not count. Every n-th counted order is paid at total * (100 - discount) / 100, rounded down. Orders that return -1 are not counted.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Catalog holds 1 to 200 products with ids from 1 to 200 and prices from 1 to 1000.
  • Parameters satisfy 1 <= n <= 10^4 and 0 <= discount <= 100.
  • Orders have 1 to 200 lines with amounts from 1 to 1000, and an id may repeat inside an order.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.

Example 1. Input Cashier(3,50,[1,2,3],[100,200,300]), then getBill([1,2],[1,1]), getBill([3],[1]), getBill([1,1],[1,2]), output 300, 300 and 150.

Example 2. Input Cashier(2,10,[1],[100]), then getBill([1],[1]), getBill([5],[1]), getBill([1],[3]), output 100, -1 and 270.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The counter and the discount depend on the whole order being valid, so every line is priced into a local total before the counter moves.

Hint

When does the order counter change, and what must be known by then?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from product id to price is the catalog, and one integer counts the orders served so far. The bill call walks the lines and adds price * amount into a local long. An id missing from the catalog ends the call with -1 before the counter moves. After the loop the counter increases by one, and when it is a multiple of n the local total is replaced by the discounted total in integer arithmetic.

The invariant is that the counter equals the number of calls that returned a bill, so a refused order never shifts the position of the discount. The counter is the only state a call writes, and the write sits after the last test.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L) per call for an order of L lines, because each line costs one hash lookup.
  • Space is O(P) for P catalog products, plus O(1) for the counter.
import java.math.BigInteger;
import java.util.*;

public final class CashierDiscountSolution {
    static final class Cashier {
        private final int n, discount;
        private final Map<Integer, Integer> price = new HashMap<>();   // authoritative catalog
        private int served;                                            // derived count of billed orders

        Cashier(int n, int discount, int[] products, int[] prices) {
            this.n = n;
            this.discount = discount;
            for (int i = 0; i < products.length; i++) price.put(products[i], prices[i]);
        }

        /**
         * Prices one order and applies the discount on every n-th billed order.
         * Time: O(L) for L lines.
         * Space: O(1) beyond the catalog.
         * Invariant: served counts only the orders that returned a bill.
         */
        long getBill(int[] product, int[] amount) {
            long total = 0;
            for (int i = 0; i < product.length; i++) {
                Integer p = price.get(product[i]);
                // An unknown product ends the call before the counter moves.
                if (p == null) return -1;
                total += (long) p * amount[i];
            }
            // The counter moves only for a fully priced order.
            served++;
            // Every n-th billed order is paid at the discounted total, rounded down.
            if (served % n == 0) total = total * (100 - discount) / 100;
            return total;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Cashier a = new Cashier(3, 50, new int[] {1, 2, 3}, new int[] {100, 200, 300});
        long[] r1 = {a.getBill(new int[] {1, 2}, new int[] {1, 1}), a.getBill(new int[] {3}, new int[] {1}), a.getBill(new int[] {1, 1}, new int[] {1, 2})};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r1, new long[] {300, 300, 150})) throw new AssertionError("e1 " + Arrays.toString(r1));
        // Example 2 from the problem text: the refused order is not counted.
        Cashier b = new Cashier(2, 10, new int[] {1}, new int[] {100});
        long[] r2 = {b.getBill(new int[] {1}, new int[] {1}), b.getBill(new int[] {5}, new int[] {1}), b.getBill(new int[] {1}, new int[] {3})};
        if (!Arrays.equals(r2, new long[] {100, -1, 270})) throw new AssertionError("e2 " + Arrays.toString(r2));
        // Random orders against a linear-scan oracle that uses BigInteger and its own counter list.
        Random rnd = new Random(1357);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int p = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5), n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4), disc = rnd.nextInt(101);
            int[] ids = new int[p], prices = new int[p];
            for (int i = 0; i < p; i++) { ids[i] = i * 2 + 1; prices[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(1000); }
            Cashier s = new Cashier(n, disc, ids, prices);
            List<Long> billed = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 40; op++) {
                int lines = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
                int[] prod = new int[lines], amt = new int[lines];
                BigInteger sum = BigInteger.ZERO;
                boolean bad = false;
                for (int i = 0; i < lines; i++) {
                    prod[i] = rnd.nextInt(2 * p + 1);
                    amt[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(1000);
                    int found = -1;
                    for (int j = 0; j < p; j++) if (ids[j] == prod[i]) found = prices[j];
                    if (found < 0) bad = true; else sum = sum.add(BigInteger.valueOf((long) found * amt[i]));
                }
                long want;
                if (bad) want = -1;
                else {
                    billed.add(0L);
                    want = (billed.size() % n == 0) ? sum.multiply(BigInteger.valueOf(100 - disc)).divide(BigInteger.valueOf(100)).longValueExact() : sum.longValueExact();
                }
                if (s.getBill(prod, amt) != want) throw new AssertionError("bill");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Answer Prefix Queries With A Trie

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Search Box That Scans Every Word

A shop keeps 100,000 product names in memory. A search box sends a request after every keystroke, and each request asks for the total sales of all products whose name starts with the typed text. The code loops over every name and calls startsWith. Typing the word "apple" sends five requests, so the code scans the whole list five times.

The list also changes while people type. A product is renamed, a sales figure is overwritten with a corrected value, and a discontinued product is erased. The totals must follow each change at once, or the box shows a number that no longer matches the data.

This lesson asks how to answer a prefix question in time that depends on the length of the prefix and not on the number of stored words. It also asks how to keep that answer correct when words are overwritten, erased and ranked.

The contract is bounded. All words are lowercase English letters, all state lives in memory, and one thread makes every call in order.

Simple approach

Scanning The Whole Map Per Query

The direct design keeps a map from word to value. A query loops over every entry and adds the value when the word starts with the prefix. An overwrite is a plain put, so the map always holds the newest value.

final class NaivePrefixSum {
    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> value = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    void insert(String key, int val) { value.put(key, val); }      // overwrite is a plain put

    long sum(String prefix) {
        long total = 0;
        for (java.util.Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : value.entrySet())   // visits every stored word
            if (e.getKey().startsWith(prefix)) total += e.getValue();
        return total;
    }
}

The answers are correct after every overwrite. The cost of one query does not depend on how many words match.

Predict first: The map holds 100,000 words, and a client asks for the sum of the prefix "ap" 1,000 times without any insert in between. How many `startsWith` calls run, and how many words does each query actually need?

The code makes 100,000 times 1,000, which is 100 million calls to startsWith. Each query needs only the words that begin with "ap", and that can be a handful. The loop pays for every word, so the number of matches never reduces the work.

Bottleneck

Repeated Work On Shared Prefixes

One query costs O(n * p) character comparisons for n stored words and a prefix of length p. The loop cannot skip a word before it reads that word. Ten queries on ten different prefixes cost ten full scans.

The scans also repeat work between words. The words "apple", "apply" and "applet" share the letters "appl". Each query compares those four letters three times, once per word, and every later query compares them again.

Rank queries make the cost worse. A request for the best three words by a score has to collect every matching word and sort them by score, so each keystroke pays a full scan plus a sort. The data has structure that the loop ignores: every prefix of a stored word is a shared starting point for many words.

Key idea

Store One Counter On Each Prefix

A trie is a tree in which every edge carries one character. The path from the root to a node spells a prefix, so every node stands for exactly one prefix. Words that share letters share the first nodes of their paths. A query for a prefix walks p edges and stops at one node, and that costs O(p) no matter how many words are stored.

Counters On The Path

The query needs a number that is ready when the walk ends. Each node stores that number for its prefix. The number is a sum of values for Map Sum, or a pass count, which is how many stored words go through the node. Insert walks the key once and adds to the number on every node of the path, so insert also costs O(p).

Changing A Value With A Delta

An overwrite must not add the new value on top of the old one. The call needs the delta, which is the new value minus the old value. Only the authoritative map from word to value knows the old value, so that map stays next to the trie. The trie holds derived numbers, and the map holds the truth. An erase uses the delta of minus the old value, and a node whose pass count falls to zero can be removed.

Branching And Ranking

A magic-dictionary search follows the word and, at most once, leaves the word to try every other child. A ranked search stores at each node only the words that pass through it. It reads the current counts from the authoritative map when it sorts, so a rank is never stale.

The invariant is that every node holds the exact aggregate of the live words under its prefix, and every public call applies all its node changes or none.

State

State Behind A Prefix Service

Four pieces of state take part in each call, and each has one owner.

  • Word map is the authoritative state, from each stored word to its value or count, and it changes first in every call.
  • Node is derived state for one prefix, with up to 26 children and its sum or pass count.
  • Delta is a local number, computed once from the word map and added to every node on the path.
  • Cursor is the node reached after reading some characters, or a missing node when the prefix matches no word.

The target costs are O(p) per insert, erase and query for a prefix of length p. Memory is O(total characters in the live words). Validation of an erase runs before the first node changes.

Walkthrough

Following Overwrites And Erases

Overwriting A Word

The first trace follows the sums stored on the nodes of the path to "apple". The pointer call sits on the latest call. Each step lists the nonzero prefix sums, and delta shows the number added to every node of the path.

Call 3 overwrites "apple" from 3 to 5, so the delta is 2 and the prefix "ap" rises from 5 to 7. The word "app" keeps its value of 2 on its own node. Call 4 repeats the value 2 for "app", so the delta is 0 and no node changes.

Erasing A Word

The second trace follows pass counts, written as prefix=pass/end. The first number counts words that go through the prefix, and the second counts words that end exactly there. An erase of a missing word is checked before any node changes.

Call 4 asks to erase "ap". That prefix has a pass count of 2 but an end count of 0, so no stored word equals it. The call returns false and leaves every node as it was. Calls 6 and 7 bring the pass count of "a" to zero, and the nodes below it disappear.

Code

A Prefix Sum Structure In Java

The class keeps the word map and the trie in step. It computes the delta from the map once and then walks the key.

final class PrefixSums {
    private static final class Node {
        final Node[] next = new Node[26];
        long sum;                                   // sum of values of live words under this prefix
    }
    private final Node root = new Node();
    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> value = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    void insert(String key, int val) {
        int old = value.getOrDefault(key, 0);       // the map knows the value being replaced
        value.put(key, val);
        long delta = (long) val - old;              // add only the difference to the path
        Node cur = root;
        for (int i = 0; i < key.length(); i++) {    // one step per character
            int c = key.charAt(i) - 'a';
            if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
            cur = cur.next[c];
            cur.sum += delta;
        }
    }

    long sum(String prefix) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (int i = 0; i < prefix.length() && cur != null; i++)
            cur = cur.next[prefix.charAt(i) - 'a'];  // a missing child ends the walk
        return cur == null ? 0 : cur.sum;
    }
}

An insert or a query costs O(p) time for a key or prefix of length p. The space is O(total characters in the live words) for the nodes plus O(n) for the word map.

When to use it

When A Prefix Index Fits

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is an API that inserts words and then asks about prefixes, wildcard patterns, counts or best matches. Write down what each node must store and which authoritative map can rebuild it. The invariant is that every node describes exactly the live words below it.

Choosing What A Node Stores

A sum or a pass count needs a delta on every path node. A one-letter-substitution search needs only the words and a flag for the end of a word. A ranked search needs the set of words under each node and reads the order from the word map. A suffix query needs a second trie built from reversed words.

False Friends

A sorted set with ceiling and floor looks like a prefix tool. It is a false friend when the question asks for a sum or a count, because the range scan still visits every match. A node that caches a rank is also a false friend, since a later count change leaves that cache stale. A plain hash map is the better choice when queries name whole words and never prefixes.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Map Sum Pairs

LeetCode 677

Problem Specification. Design a class MapSum. The call insert(key, val) stores val for key, replaces any earlier value of that key, and returns the earlier value, or -1 when the key is new. The call sum(prefix) returns the total of the values of all stored keys that start with prefix, or 0 when none does. The rules are this chapter's own and differ from LeetCode, because insert returns a value.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys and prefixes are non-empty strings of 1 to 50 lowercase letters.
  • Values are integers from 1 to 1000.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and totals fit in a long.
  • Return of insert is an int, and of sum is a long.

Example 1. Input calls insert(apple,3), insert(app,2), sum(ap), output -1, -1 and 5.

Example 2. Input calls insert(apple,3), insert(apple,5), insert(app,2), sum(app), output -1, 3, -1 and 7.

Prerequisites. Hash maps and the trie walk.

What This Exercise Changes. The trie stores sums, so an overwrite adds the difference between the new and old value and not the new value.

Hint

Which number does the second insert of the same key add to each node?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from key to value is the authoritative state. The trie is a derived index in which each node holds the sum of the values of all live keys under its prefix. An insert reads the earlier value from the map, stores the new value and computes the difference between them. It then walks the key once and adds that difference to every node on the path, creating missing nodes on the way.

A sum call walks the prefix and returns the number on the last node, or 0 when a child is missing. The invariant is that every node equals the sum of the map values of the keys under its prefix. An overwrite keeps that equality because the node already contains the old value, and only the difference is added.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(p) for an insert or a sum with a key or prefix of length p, because each call visits one node per character.
  • Space is O(L) for the trie, where L is the total length of the distinct keys, plus O(n) for the map.
import java.util.*;

public final class MapSumSolution {
    static final class MapSum {
        private static final class Node {
            final Node[] next = new Node[26];
            long sum;
        }
        private final Node root = new Node();
        private final Map<String, Integer> value = new HashMap<>();   // authoritative key -> value

        /**
         * Stores a value and returns the earlier one, or -1 for a new key.
         * Time: O(p).
         * Space: O(p) for new nodes.
         * Invariant: each node sum equals the map values under its prefix.
         */
        int insert(String key, int val) {
            Integer old = value.put(key, val);
            // Only the difference reaches the nodes, because they already hold the old value.
            long delta = (long) val - (old == null ? 0 : old);
            Node cur = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < key.length(); i++) {
                int c = key.charAt(i) - 'a';
                // Create the child on first use so the path exists for the whole key.
                if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
                cur = cur.next[c];
                cur.sum += delta;
            }
            return old == null ? -1 : old;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the sum of values of keys under the prefix.
         * Time: O(p).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a missing child means no live key has this prefix.
         */
        long sum(String prefix) {
            Node cur = root;
            // Follow the prefix and stop early when no key continues that way.
            for (int i = 0; i < prefix.length(); i++) {
                cur = cur.next[prefix.charAt(i) - 'a'];
                if (cur == null) return 0;
            }
            return cur.sum;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        MapSum m = new MapSum();
        if (m.insert("apple", 3) != -1 || m.insert("app", 2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 insert");
        if (m.sum("ap") != 5) throw new AssertionError("e1 sum");
        MapSum n = new MapSum();
        if (n.insert("apple", 3) != -1 || n.insert("apple", 5) != 3 || n.insert("app", 2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("e2 insert");
        if (n.sum("app") != 7) throw new AssertionError("e2 sum");
        // Random calls checked against a full scan of a reference map.
        Random rnd = new Random(677);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            MapSum s = new MapSum();
            Map<String, Integer> ref = new HashMap<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                String w = word(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    int v = 1 + rnd.nextInt(1000);
                    Integer old = ref.put(w, v);
                    if (s.insert(w, v) != (old == null ? -1 : old)) throw new AssertionError("returned old value");
                } else {
                    long want = 0;
                    for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : ref.entrySet()) if (e.getKey().startsWith(w)) want += e.getValue();
                    if (s.sum(w) != want) throw new AssertionError("sum " + w);
                }
            }
        }
    }

    static String word(Random rnd, int len) {
        StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) b.append((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
        return b.toString();
    }
}verified
Variation

Implement Magic Dictionary

LeetCode 676

Problem Specification. Design a class MagicDictionary. The call buildDict(words) discards any earlier dictionary and stores the given words. The call search(word) returns true when changing exactly one character of word to a different letter produces a stored word, and false otherwise. Changing no character does not count.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Words and queries are strings of 1 to 20 lowercase letters.
  • Dictionary holds 0 to 100 words, and duplicates are allowed.
  • Calls number at most 5000.
  • Return of search is a boolean.

Example 1. Input buildDict([hello,leetcode]), then search(hello), search(hhllo), search(hell), output false, true, false.

Example 2. Input buildDict([abc,abd]), then search(abc), search(xyz), then buildDict([zzz]), search(abc), output true, false, false.

Prerequisites. The map sum exercise above and recursion on a trie.

What This Exercise Changes. The walk follows the query word and may branch to a different child once, so it carries a flag that records whether the one change is used.

Hint

What does the walk carry besides the node, so that a second change is refused?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The build call creates a fresh root, so a later build discards the earlier dictionary. Each stored word marks its last node as the end of a word. A search is a depth-first walk that carries the node, the index in the query and a flag that tells whether the one change is already used.

At each index the walk always follows the child that matches the query letter. While the flag is unset, it also tries each other child and sets the flag on that branch. At the end of the query the walk accepts only a node that ends a word and only when the flag is set, so an exact match without a change is rejected. The invariant is that a walk with the flag set has followed the query on every index except one.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(26 * L * L) per search for a query of length L, because each of the L positions may branch to 25 children and each branch then follows at most L letters.
  • Space is O(T) for the trie over T total dictionary letters, plus O(L) for the recursion.
import java.util.*;

public final class MagicDictionarySolution {
    static final class MagicDictionary {
        private static final class Node {
            final Node[] next = new Node[26];
            boolean end;
        }
        private Node root = new Node();

        /** Replaces the dictionary; Time O(total letters); Space O(total letters); invariant: only the new words are stored. */
        void buildDict(String[] words) {
            root = new Node();
            for (String w : words) {
                Node cur = root;
                for (int i = 0; i < w.length(); i++) {
                    int c = w.charAt(i) - 'a';
                    if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
                    cur = cur.next[c];
                }
                cur.end = true;
            }
        }

        boolean search(String word) { return dfs(root, word, 0, false); }

        /**
         * Walks the query and may leave it once.
         * Time: O(26 * L * L).
         * Space: O(L) recursion.
         * Invariant: when used is true, exactly one earlier index differed from the query.
         */
        private boolean dfs(Node node, String w, int i, boolean used) {
            // At the end the word must exist and the one change must have happened.
            if (i == w.length()) return used && node.end;
            int want = w.charAt(i) - 'a';
            for (int c = 0; c < 26; c++) {
                Node nx = node.next[c];
                if (nx == null) continue;
                // The matching child keeps the flag as it is.
                if (c == want) { if (dfs(nx, w, i + 1, used)) return true; }
                // A different child spends the one allowed change.
                else if (!used && dfs(nx, w, i + 1, true)) return true;
            }
            return false;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        MagicDictionary d = new MagicDictionary();
        d.buildDict(new String[] {"hello", "leetcode"});
        if (d.search("hello") || !d.search("hhllo") || d.search("hell")) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        d.buildDict(new String[] {"abc", "abd"});
        if (!d.search("abc") || d.search("xyz")) throw new AssertionError("e2 first");
        d.buildDict(new String[] {"zzz"});
        if (d.search("abc")) throw new AssertionError("e2 rebuild");
        // Random dictionaries checked against a pairwise count of differing positions.
        Random rnd = new Random(676);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            String[] words = new String[rnd.nextInt(7)];
            for (int i = 0; i < words.length; i++) words[i] = word(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
            MagicDictionary m = new MagicDictionary();
            m.buildDict(words);
            for (int q = 0; q < 30; q++) {
                String w = word(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
                boolean want = false;
                for (String x : words) {
                    if (x.length() != w.length()) continue;
                    int diff = 0;
                    for (int i = 0; i < w.length(); i++) if (x.charAt(i) != w.charAt(i)) diff++;
                    if (diff == 1) want = true;
                }
                if (m.search(w) != want) throw new AssertionError("search " + w + " in " + Arrays.toString(words));
            }
        }
    }

    static String word(Random rnd, int len) {
        StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) b.append((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
        return b.toString();
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Implement Trie II

LeetCode 1804

Problem Specification. The rules are written from the title and need not match LeetCode. Design a class TrieCounts. The call insert(word) adds one copy of word. The call countEqual(word) returns how many copies of word are stored. The call countPrefix(prefix) returns how many stored copies start with prefix. The call erase(word) removes one copy and returns true, or returns false and changes nothing when no copy is stored.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Words and prefixes are strings of 1 to 50 lowercase letters.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Copies of one word are unlimited, and counts fit in an int.
  • Erase of a word that is only a prefix of stored words returns false.

Example 1. Input calls insert(apple), insert(apple), insert(app), countEqual(apple), countPrefix(app), erase(apple), countPrefix(app), output 2, 3, true and 2 for the last four.

Example 2. Input calls insert(cat), erase(car), erase(ca), countPrefix(ca), erase(cat), erase(cat), countPrefix(c), output false, false, 1, true, false and 0.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. An erase can fail, so the call first confirms that a copy exists and only then lowers the pass count along the path.

Hint

Which check must pass before the first node count goes down?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Every node stores a pass count, which is the number of stored copies that go through it, and an end count, which is the number of copies that end there. Insert raises the pass count on each node of the path and raises the end count on the last node. A count call walks the string and reads one of the two numbers.

Erase is a multi-field change, so it validates first. It walks the word and returns false when a node is missing or when the end count on the last node is 0. A word that is only a prefix of other words therefore cannot be erased. After that check, a second walk lowers every pass count on the path and the end count on the last node. When a pass count reaches 0 the parent drops the whole child, because no copy lives below it. The invariant is that a pass count equals the end counts in its subtree, and no node with a pass count of 0 stays linked.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(p) for each call with a string of length p, because validation and update are two walks of the same path.
  • Space is O(L) for the nodes of the live words, since erase frees nodes with a pass count of 0.
import java.util.*;

public final class TrieCountsSolution {
    static final class TrieCounts {
