        private static final class Node {
            final Node[] next = new Node[26];
            int pass, end;
        }
        private final Node root = new Node();

        /** Adds one copy; Time O(p); Space O(p); invariant: pass counts rise on the whole path together. */
        void insert(String word) {
            Node cur = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < word.length(); i++) {
                int c = word.charAt(i) - 'a';
                if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
                cur = cur.next[c];
                cur.pass++;
            }
            cur.end++;
        }

        // Walks to the node of a string, or returns null when no stored word continues that way; O(p) time, O(1) space.
        private Node find(String s) {
            Node cur = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < s.length() && cur != null; i++) cur = cur.next[s.charAt(i) - 'a'];
            return cur;
        }

        int countEqual(String word) { Node n = find(word); return n == null ? 0 : n.end; }

        int countPrefix(String prefix) { Node n = find(prefix); return n == null ? 0 : n.pass; }

        /**
         * Removes one copy, or changes nothing.
         * Time: O(p).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: validation finishes before the first count changes.
         */
        boolean erase(String word) {
            // Validate on the read-only walk, because a prefix without an end count is not a word.
            if (countEqual(word) == 0) return false;
            Node parent = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < word.length(); i++) {
                int c = word.charAt(i) - 'a';
                Node child = parent.next[c];
                // The pass count drops once for each node on the path.
                if (--child.pass == 0) { parent.next[c] = null; return true; }
                parent = child;
            }
            // The end count drops on the last node when it stays linked.
            parent.end--;
            return true;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        TrieCounts t = new TrieCounts();
        t.insert("apple"); t.insert("apple"); t.insert("app");
        if (t.countEqual("apple") != 2 || t.countPrefix("app") != 3) throw new AssertionError("e1 counts");
        if (!t.erase("apple") || t.countPrefix("app") != 2) throw new AssertionError("e1 erase");
        TrieCounts u = new TrieCounts();
        u.insert("cat");
        if (u.erase("car") || u.erase("ca") || u.countPrefix("ca") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 failed erases");
        if (!u.erase("cat") || u.erase("cat") || u.countPrefix("c") != 0) throw new AssertionError("e2 last");
        // Random calls checked against a multiset and a scan for prefixes.
        Random rnd = new Random(1804);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            TrieCounts s = new TrieCounts();
            Map<String, Integer> ref = new HashMap<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 100; op++) {
                String w = word(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
                switch (rnd.nextInt(4)) {
                    case 0 -> { s.insert(w); ref.merge(w, 1, Integer::sum); }
                    case 1 -> {
                        boolean want = ref.getOrDefault(w, 0) > 0;
                        if (s.erase(w) != want) throw new AssertionError("erase result " + w);
                        if (want) ref.merge(w, -1, Integer::sum);
                    }
                    case 2 -> { if (s.countEqual(w) != ref.getOrDefault(w, 0)) throw new AssertionError("equal " + w); }
                    default -> {
                        int want = 0;
                        for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : ref.entrySet()) if (e.getKey().startsWith(w)) want += e.getValue();
                        if (s.countPrefix(w) != want) throw new AssertionError("prefix " + w);
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    static String word(Random rnd, int len) {
        StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) b.append((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
        return b.toString();
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Design Search Autocomplete System

LeetCode 642

Problem Specification. LeetCode marks this problem as premium, so the contract below is the one this lesson checks and may differ in detail. Design a class Autocomplete built from arrays sentences and times that give each starting sentence and its count. The call input(c) receives one character. A letter or a space extends the typed text and returns the best three stored sentences that start with the typed text, ordered by larger count first and then by smaller String.compareTo. The character # ends the typed text, adds one to the count of that text, and returns an empty list. An empty typed text at # changes nothing.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Characters are lowercase letters, the space, and #.
  • Sentences have 1 to 100 characters, and the start list has 0 to 100 sentences.
  • Calls number at most 5000.
  • Return is a list of at most three sentences.

Example 1. Input ["i love you","island","iroman","i love leetcode"] with times [5,3,2,2], then input(i), input( ), input(a), input(#), output the lists [i love you, island, i love leetcode], [i love you, i love leetcode], [] and [].

Example 2. Input the same start, then input(i), input( ), input(a), input(#), input(i), input( ), output the same four lists, then [i love you, i love leetcode, i a] for the last call.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Each node lists only the sentences under its prefix, and the sort reads current counts from the word map, so the order is never stale.

Hint

Where does the order of the matches come from when the counts change after a build?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from sentence to count is the authoritative state. Each trie node keeps only the set of sentences that pass through it, which is derived state. A sentence enters the sets once, when it first appears, and later count changes touch only the map. The typed text lives in a string builder, and a cursor holds the current node, or null when no stored sentence starts with the typed text.

A letter or space extends the typed text and moves the cursor one edge. While the cursor is null it stays null, so every further character returns an empty list until the next #. Otherwise the call copies the node's set, sorts it by the count read from the map and then by compareTo, and returns the first three. A # adds one to the count of the typed text, links a new sentence into the trie when needed, clears the typed text and resets the cursor. The sort comparator reads counts from the map and never from objects inside the sets, so no key of an ordered structure changes in place.

The review note is that the sort costs O(m log m) for m matching sentences. A cache of the best three sentences on each node would give O(p) per character, but it needs an update on every count change along the path. The transfer prompt asks only for the correct order, and the simpler sort is enough here.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m log m) per character for m sentences under the typed text, and O(p) per # for a sentence of length p.
  • Space is O(L) for the nodes and sets, where L is the total length of the distinct sentences, plus the length of the typed text.
import java.util.*;

public final class AutocompleteSolution {
    static final class Autocomplete {
        private static final class Node {
            final Map<Character, Node> next = new HashMap<>();
            final Set<String> through = new HashSet<>();     // derived: sentences under this prefix
        }
        private final Node root = new Node();
        private final Map<String, Integer> count = new HashMap<>();   // authoritative sentence -> count
        private final StringBuilder typed = new StringBuilder();
        private Node cur = root;                              // null once the typed text matches nothing

        Autocomplete(String[] sentences, int[] times) {
            for (int i = 0; i < sentences.length; i++) add(sentences[i], times[i]);
        }

        // Adds to the count and links a sentence into the trie on its first appearance; O(p) time, O(p) space.
        private void add(String s, int times) {
            boolean fresh = !count.containsKey(s);
            count.merge(s, times, Integer::sum);
            if (!fresh) return;
            Node n = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
                n = n.next.computeIfAbsent(s.charAt(i), k -> new Node());
                n.through.add(s);
            }
        }

        /**
         * Handles one typed character.
         * Time: O(m log m) for a letter, O(p) for the end mark.
         * Space: O(m).
         * Invariant: the cursor is the node of the typed text, or null when no sentence matches.
         */
        List<String> input(char c) {
            if (c == '#') {
                // An empty typed text changes nothing, otherwise the text becomes one more use.
                if (typed.length() > 0) add(typed.toString(), 1);
                typed.setLength(0);
                cur = root;
                return List.of();
            }
            typed.append(c);
            // A null cursor stays null, so a dead prefix cannot match again.
            if (cur != null) cur = cur.next.get(c);
            if (cur == null) return List.of();
            List<String> cand = new ArrayList<>(cur.through);
            // Counts come from the map at sort time, so the order is never stale.
            cand.sort((a, b) -> {
                int byCount = Integer.compare(count.get(b), count.get(a));
                return byCount != 0 ? byCount : a.compareTo(b);
            });
            return new ArrayList<>(cand.subList(0, Math.min(3, cand.size())));
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        String[] start = {"i love you", "island", "iroman", "i love leetcode"};
        int[] times = {5, 3, 2, 2};
        Autocomplete a = new Autocomplete(start, times);
        if (!a.input('i').equals(List.of("i love you", "island", "i love leetcode"))) throw new AssertionError("e1 i");
        if (!a.input(' ').equals(List.of("i love you", "i love leetcode"))) throw new AssertionError("e1 space");
        if (!a.input('a').isEmpty() || !a.input('#').isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("e1 end");
        a.input('i');
        if (!a.input(' ').equals(List.of("i love you", "i love leetcode", "i a"))) throw new AssertionError("e2 last");
        // Random calls checked against a full scan with the same sort rule.
        Random rnd = new Random(642);
        char[] alpha = {'a', 'b', ' '};
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int k = rnd.nextInt(5);
            String[] ss = new String[k];
            int[] tt = new int[k];
            Map<String, Integer> ref = new HashMap<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) {
                ss[i] = text(rnd, alpha, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
                tt[i] = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
                ref.merge(ss[i], tt[i], Integer::sum);
            }
            Autocomplete s = new Autocomplete(ss, tt);
            StringBuilder typed = new StringBuilder();
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                char c = rnd.nextInt(5) == 0 ? '#' : alpha[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                List<String> got = s.input(c);
                if (c == '#') {
                    if (typed.length() > 0) ref.merge(typed.toString(), 1, Integer::sum);
                    typed.setLength(0);
                    if (!got.isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("end mark returns empty");
                    continue;
                }
                typed.append(c);
                List<String> want = new ArrayList<>();
                for (String x : ref.keySet()) if (x.startsWith(typed.toString())) want.add(x);
                want.sort((x, y) -> ref.get(x).equals(ref.get(y)) ? x.compareTo(y) : ref.get(y) - ref.get(x));
                if (want.size() > 3) want = new ArrayList<>(want.subList(0, 3));
                if (!got.equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("typed [" + typed + "] got " + got + " want " + want);
            }
        }
    }

    static String text(Random rnd, char[] alpha, int len) {
        StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) b.append(alpha[rnd.nextInt(3)]);
        return b.toString();
    }
}verified

Track Status And Pick The Next Task

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Cancelled Job That Still Runs

A job runner accepts print jobs with a priority. A user cancels job 7 because the file was wrong. A minute later the printer starts job 7 anyway, because it was the highest priority job in the line and nobody told the line that it was cancelled. The paper is wasted, and the user sees the cancel screen say "done".

The opposite failure is just as common. A job finishes, but the line still lists it, so the next pick returns the same job twice. Both failures come from one cause. The program keeps two views of the same jobs, one that says what happened to each job and one that says which job is next, and the two views disagree.

This lesson asks how to keep the answer to "which job is next" correct while jobs are added, cancelled, finished and changed, and how to keep each call fast.

Simple approach

Scanning Every Job On Every Pick

The direct design stores every job in one list with a flag for its state. A pick walks the whole list, skips jobs that are not waiting, and keeps the best one by priority, with the smaller id winning a tie.

final class NaiveRunner {
    private static final class Job {
        final int id; final int priority; String state = "PENDING";
        Job(int id, int priority) { this.id = id; this.priority = priority; }
    }
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Job> jobs = new java.util.ArrayList<>();

    boolean add(int id, int priority) {
        for (Job j : jobs) if (j.id == id) return false;       // duplicate check scans every job
        jobs.add(new Job(id, priority));
        return true;
    }

    int next() {
        Job best = null;
        for (Job j : jobs) {                                    // visits finished and cancelled jobs too
            if (!j.state.equals("PENDING")) continue;
            if (best == null || j.priority > best.priority) best = j;
        }
        return best == null ? -1 : best.id;
    }
}

The answers are right. The flag is the only record of what happened to a job, so a cancelled job can never be picked.

Predict first: The runner holds n jobs. A loop calls next() and then marks the job it returned as done, until no job is waiting. About how many job visits do the next() calls make in total?

Each next() visits every stored job, because finished jobs stay in the list. There are n calls and each visits n jobs, so the total is about n squared visits. For n equal to 100000 that is ten billion visits.

Bottleneck

Visiting Jobs That Cannot Win

The cost of one pick is O(n) for n stored jobs. The add call is also O(n) because of the duplicate check. A sequence of n calls therefore costs O(n^2), which is too slow for 10^5 calls.

Most visits are wasted. A job that is done or cancelled can never be the answer again, yet every later pick still reads it. The list grows with every job ever added, so the work per pick grows even when only a few jobs are waiting.

A structure ordered by priority would find the best job without a scan. That structure brings back the failure from the opening: it holds jobs, and a cancel call has to reach into it. Removing an arbitrary entry from a heap costs O(n) to find it. A design that ignores the cancel keeps returning dead jobs.

Key idea

Keep Status Apart From The Queue

Store the truth about each job in one place and let every other structure be a hint that the truth can overrule. The status table is a hash map from job id to its record, which holds the priority and the current state. Every call that changes what happened to a job writes this table first. A question such as "is job 7 done" is answered by the table and by nothing else.

The Ready Set

The ready set is a tree set or a heap that orders the waiting jobs by priority, then by smaller id. It exists only to answer the next-job question in O(log n). A job enters the ready set when it is added, and it leaves when it is picked, cancelled or finished. When the structure supports removal by key, as a tree set does, the call removes the job in O(log n) and the ready set never holds a dead job.

Entries That Go Stale

A heap cannot remove an arbitrary job quickly. So a cancel or a priority change only updates the status table and leaves the old heap entry in place. A stale entry is a heap entry whose job is no longer waiting, or whose priority or version no longer matches the table. A pick pops entries from the top and discards each stale one after comparing it with the table. The first entry that matches the table is the answer. Each entry is pushed once and popped once, so the discards cost O(log n) amortized per call.

The invariant is that the status table alone decides which jobs are live, and every live job has at least one matching entry in the ready set, so a pick that skips stale entries returns the best live job.

State

What The Service Stores

The service keeps five pieces of state, and each has one owner.

  • Status table is the authoritative map from job id to priority, version and state, and every call reads and writes it first.
  • Ready set is the derived order of waiting jobs, and it changes only after the status table accepts the call.
  • Entry is an immutable record of priority, id and version, so no comparator field changes while it sits in the ready set.
  • Version is a counter in the job record that increases on each priority change and marks older entries as stale.
  • Pending count is a number kept in step with the status table, because the size of a heap also counts stale entries.
Walkthrough

Following Calls Through Both Structures

Tree Set With Exact Removal

The first trace uses a tree set, so each cancel and each completion removes the job from the ready set at once. The pointer call sits on the latest call. The ready set is shown from the best job to the worst, with each job written as id and priority.

Jobs 2 and 3 share priority 9, so the smaller id 2 comes first. After job 2 is cancelled, the pick returns job 3. The last cancel names job 3 after it is done, which is not a legal move, so the call returns false and the ready set stays unchanged.

Heap With Stale Entries

The second trace uses a heap, so the status table and the heap can disagree. Each heap entry is written as id, priority and the version it was pushed with. The pending count comes from the status table.

The priority change pushes a second entry for job 2 and leaves the old one. The cancel of job 1 leaves its entry too. The first take pops the entries 2@9 v0 and 1@5 v0, finds both stale and discards them. The next entry, 3@4 v0, matches the table, so the call returns job 3. The second take returns job 2 from the entry 2@1 v1. The pending count stays correct throughout, even while the heap holds more entries than there are live jobs.

Code

The Runner With Table And Heap

The class keeps the status table as the source of truth, pushes a new heap entry on each priority change, and skips stale entries in the pick.

final class Runner {
    private record Entry(int priority, int id, int version) {}
    private static final class Job { int priority; int version; boolean pending = true; }

    private final java.util.Map<Integer, Job> table = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.PriorityQueue<Entry> ready = new java.util.PriorityQueue<>(
        (a, b) -> a.priority() != b.priority() ? Integer.compare(b.priority(), a.priority())
                                                : Integer.compare(a.id(), b.id()));

    boolean add(int id, int priority) {
        if (table.containsKey(id)) return false;               // one hash lookup, no scan
        Job j = new Job(); j.priority = priority;
        table.put(id, j);
        ready.add(new Entry(priority, id, 0));                  // O(log n) push
        return true;
    }

    int next() {
        while (!ready.isEmpty()) {
            Entry e = ready.peek();
            Job j = table.get(e.id());
            if (j.pending && j.version == e.version()) return e.id();   // matches the table: live
            ready.poll();                                       // stale: discard, each entry once
        }
        return -1;
    }
}

Each call costs O(log n) amortized, because an entry is pushed once and discarded at most once. The space is O(n + p) for n jobs and p priority changes.

When to use it

Spotting Entities With A Lifecycle

Recognizing The Pattern

The cue is a set of entities that move through states while a query asks for the best live one under a rule. Name the authoritative record first, then list every derived view that depends on it. The invariant is that the record decides what is live and each view only narrows the search.

Choosing The Removal Style

Use exact removal when the index supports it in O(log n), as a tree set or a linked hash structure does. Use stale entries when the index is a heap or a plain queue and an entry in the middle cannot be reached. In both styles the record is written before the index, and a rejected call writes nothing.

False Friends

A boolean flag inside the queued object looks like a complete answer. It is a false friend when the object is also a key of a sorted structure, because changing a comparator field in place breaks the structure. Replace the entry instead. A heap size is another false friend, since it counts stale entries, so keep a separate counter for the number of live jobs. A plain queue with no status table cannot tell a cancelled job from a waiting one.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

In-Memory Task Lifecycle

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class Tasks. The call add(id, priority) creates a task in state PENDING and returns true, or returns false if the id was ever used. The call cancel(id) moves a PENDING task to CANCELLED and returns true, or returns false for any other case. The call complete(id) moves a PENDING task to DONE and returns true, or returns false for any other case. The call next() returns the id of the PENDING task with the highest priority, breaking ties by the smaller id, or returns -1 if none exists. It changes nothing. The call state(id) returns the state name, or NONE for an unknown id.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Ids are integers from 1 to 10^5, and priorities are integers from 0 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5.
  • States are the strings PENDING, DONE, CANCELLED and NONE.
  • Return types are boolean for add, cancel and complete, int for next, and String for state.

Example 1. Input calls add(1,5), add(2,9), add(3,9), next(), cancel(2), next(), output true, true, true, 2, true and 3.

Example 2. Input calls add(4,1), complete(4), cancel(4), next(), add(4,7), state(4), output true, true, false, -1, false and DONE.

Prerequisites. Hash maps and tree sets.

What This Exercise Changes. A move out of PENDING removes the task from the ordered set in the same call that writes the table.

Hint

Which structure answers "what happened to task 4", and which structure answers "who is next"?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from task id to an immutable record of priority and state is the authoritative table. A tree set of immutable keys holds the pending tasks. A key stores the priority and the id, and its comparator puts the larger priority first and the smaller id first among equals.

Every mutating call reads the table first. A cancel or a complete on a task that is not pending returns false before any write. Otherwise the call removes the key from the tree set, and then stores a new record with the new state. The key is removed with the same priority it was inserted with, because the table holds that priority and never changes it.

The invariant is that the tree set holds exactly the ids whose table state is PENDING, so the first key is always the answer to next().

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per call, because each call does one hash lookup and at most one tree operation. The call next() reads the first key.
  • Space is O(n), because each task owns one record and at most one key.
import java.util.*;

public final class TaskLifecycleSolution {
    static final class Tasks {
        private enum State { PENDING, DONE, CANCELLED }
        private record Rec(int priority, State state) {}
        private record Key(int priority, int id) {}
        private final Map<Integer, Rec> table = new HashMap<>();                 // id -> record, source of truth
        private final TreeSet<Key> ready = new TreeSet<>((a, b) ->
            a.priority() != b.priority() ? Integer.compare(b.priority(), a.priority()) : Integer.compare(a.id(), b.id()));

        /** Adds a pending task; O(log n) time, O(1) extra space; invariant: ready holds exactly the pending ids. */
        boolean add(int id, int priority) {
            // An id that was ever used is rejected, whatever its state.
            if (table.containsKey(id)) return false;
            table.put(id, new Rec(priority, State.PENDING));
            ready.add(new Key(priority, id));
            return true;
        }

        /** Moves a pending task to a new state; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: the key leaves ready with the move. */
        private boolean leave(int id, State to) {
            Rec r = table.get(id);
            // Validate before any write: only a pending task can move.
            if (r == null || r.state() != State.PENDING) return false;
            ready.remove(new Key(r.priority(), id));
            table.put(id, new Rec(r.priority(), to));
            return true;
        }

        boolean cancel(int id) { return leave(id, State.CANCELLED); }

        boolean complete(int id) { return leave(id, State.DONE); }

        /** Returns the best pending id or -1; O(log n) time (first()), O(1) space; invariant: reads only. */
        int next() { return ready.isEmpty() ? -1 : ready.first().id(); }

        String state(int id) { Rec r = table.get(id); return r == null ? "NONE" : r.state().name(); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Tasks a = new Tasks();
        if (!a.add(1, 5) || !a.add(2, 9) || !a.add(3, 9) || a.next() != 2 || !a.cancel(2) || a.next() != 3) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Tasks b = new Tasks();
        if (!b.add(4, 1) || !b.complete(4) || b.cancel(4) || b.next() != -1 || b.add(4, 7) || !b.state(4).equals("DONE")) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random calls checked against a full scan of a plain map.
        Random rnd = new Random(7001);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Tasks s = new Tasks();
            Map<Integer, int[]> ref = new HashMap<>();                           // id -> {priority, state 0 pending 1 done 2 cancelled}
            for (int op = 0; op < 120; op++) {
                int id = rnd.nextInt(8), t = rnd.nextInt(5);
                if (t == 0) {
                    int p = rnd.nextInt(4);
                    boolean want = !ref.containsKey(id);
                    if (want) ref.put(id, new int[] {p, 0});
                    if (s.add(id, p) != want) throw new AssertionError("add");
                } else if (t == 1 || t == 2) {
                    boolean want = ref.containsKey(id) && ref.get(id)[1] == 0;
                    if (want) ref.get(id)[1] = t;
                    boolean got = t == 1 ? s.complete(id) : s.cancel(id);
                    if (got != want) throw new AssertionError("move");
                } else {
                    int best = -1;
                    for (int i : ref.keySet()) {
                        int[] v = ref.get(i);
                        if (v[1] != 0) continue;
                        if (best == -1 || v[0] > ref.get(best)[0] || (v[0] == ref.get(best)[0] && i < best)) best = i;
                    }
                    if (s.next() != best) throw new AssertionError("next");
                    String want = !ref.containsKey(id) ? "NONE" : new String[] {"PENDING", "DONE", "CANCELLED"}[ref.get(id)[1]];
                    if (!s.state(id).equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("state");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Design a Todo List

LeetCode 2590

Problem Specification. This contract is written from the title and may differ from LeetCode. Design a class Todo. The call addTask(user, due, tags) creates a pending task for the user, where tags is an array of strings, and returns the task id. Ids start at 1 and increase by 1 across all users. Repeated tags in one call count once. The call complete(user, taskId) marks the task done and returns true when the task exists, belongs to that user and is pending, and otherwise returns false. The call pending(user) returns the user's pending task ids ordered by due, then by id. The call pendingWithTag(user, tag) returns the same order, limited to tasks that carry the tag. The call doneWithTag(user, tag) returns how many of the user's done tasks carry the tag.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Users are integers from 1 to 10^4, and due is an integer from 1 to 10^9.
  • Tags are lowercase strings, with 0 to 5 per task.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and a call may name an unknown task or user.
  • Return of the two list calls is a list of ids, and doneWithTag returns an int.

Example 1. Input calls addTask(1,10,[a,b]), addTask(1,5,[b]), addTask(2,7,[a]), pending(1), pendingWithTag(1,a), complete(2,2), complete(1,1), pending(1), output 1, 2, 3, the list 2, 1, the list 1, false, true and the list 2.

Example 2. Input calls addTask(3,4,[x,x]), complete(3,1), doneWithTag(3,x), complete(3,1), pendingWithTag(3,x), output 1, true, 1, false and the empty list.

Prerequisites. The task exercise above and tree sets.

What This Exercise Changes. The user's index and every tag index lose the task in one step, while a done counter per user and tag gains it. The ordered key never changes after creation.

Hint

Which indexes must lose a task when it is completed, and which count must gain it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Three arrays indexed by task id hold the authoritative facts: the owner, the due date and a done flag, and a set of tags per task. Three derived views depend on them. A map from user to a tree set holds that user's pending tasks. A map from the pair of user and tag to a tree set holds the pending tasks that carry the tag. A map from the same pair to an integer counts the done tasks.

A tree set stores due * 2^20 + id as one long. Because the due date and the id never change after creation, the key never changes while it sits in a set. Sorting by this key orders tasks by due date and then by id.

A complete call checks that the task exists, belongs to the user and is not done. Only then does it set the flag, remove the key from the user set and from each tag set, and raise each tag counter. Duplicate tags are removed when the task is added, so each tag index and counter is touched once per task.

The invariant is that each pending task has its key in the user set and in every tag set it carries, and no done task has a key anywhere.

Complexity.

  • Time is O((t + 1) log n) per add or complete call for t tags per task, and O(k) per list call for k returned ids.
  • Space is O(n * (t + 1)), because each task owns one key per index it appears in.
import java.util.*;

public final class TodoListSolution {
    static final class Todo {
        private final List<Integer> owner = new ArrayList<>();                       // index id -> user (id 0 unused)
        private final List<Integer> dueOf = new ArrayList<>();
        private final List<Boolean> done = new ArrayList<>();
        private final List<Set<String>> tagsOf = new ArrayList<>();
        private final Map<Integer, TreeSet<Long>> byUser = new HashMap<>();          // derived: pending keys per user
        private final Map<String, TreeSet<Long>> byTag = new HashMap<>();            // derived: pending keys per user and tag
        private final Map<String, Integer> doneCount = new HashMap<>();              // derived: done tasks per user and tag

        Todo() { owner.add(0); dueOf.add(0); done.add(false); tagsOf.add(Set.of()); }

        private static long key(int due, int id) { return ((long) due << 20) | id; }

        private static String slot(int user, String tag) { return user + "#" + tag; }

        /** Creates a pending task and indexes it; O((t+1) log n) time, O(t) space; invariant: every index sees the new key. */
        int addTask(int user, int due, String[] tags) {
            int id = owner.size();
            Set<String> set = new TreeSet<>(Arrays.asList(tags));                    // repeated tags count once
            owner.add(user); dueOf.add(due); done.add(false); tagsOf.add(set);
            long k = key(due, id);
            byUser.computeIfAbsent(user, x -> new TreeSet<>()).add(k);
            for (String t : set) byTag.computeIfAbsent(slot(user, t), x -> new TreeSet<>()).add(k);
            return id;
        }

        /** Completes a task; O((t+1) log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: validation precedes every write. */
        boolean complete(int user, int taskId) {
            // Validate existence, ownership and state before any write.
            if (taskId < 1 || taskId >= owner.size() || owner.get(taskId) != user || done.get(taskId)) return false;
            done.set(taskId, true);
            long k = key(dueOf.get(taskId), taskId);
            byUser.get(user).remove(k);
            for (String t : tagsOf.get(taskId)) {
                byTag.get(slot(user, t)).remove(k);
                doneCount.merge(slot(user, t), 1, Integer::sum);
            }
            return true;
        }

        private static List<Integer> ids(TreeSet<Long> keys) {
            List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
            if (keys != null) for (long k : keys) out.add((int) (k & ((1 << 20) - 1)));
            return out;
        }

        List<Integer> pending(int user) { return ids(byUser.get(user)); }

        List<Integer> pendingWithTag(int user, String tag) { return ids(byTag.get(slot(user, tag))); }

        int doneWithTag(int user, String tag) { return doneCount.getOrDefault(slot(user, tag), 0); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Todo a = new Todo();
        if (a.addTask(1, 10, new String[] {"a", "b"}) != 1 || a.addTask(1, 5, new String[] {"b"}) != 2 || a.addTask(2, 7, new String[] {"a"}) != 3) throw new AssertionError("e1 ids");
        if (!a.pending(1).equals(List.of(2, 1)) || !a.pendingWithTag(1, "a").equals(List.of(1))) throw new AssertionError("e1 lists");
        if (a.complete(2, 2) || !a.complete(1, 1) || !a.pending(1).equals(List.of(2))) throw new AssertionError("e1 complete");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Todo b = new Todo();
        if (b.addTask(3, 4, new String[] {"x", "x"}) != 1 || !b.complete(3, 1) || b.doneWithTag(3, "x") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 a");
        if (b.complete(3, 1) || !b.pendingWithTag(3, "x").isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("e2 b");
        // Random calls checked against a scan of every task.
        Random rnd = new Random(2590);
        String[] pool = {"a", "b", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            Todo s = new Todo();
            List<int[]> ref = new ArrayList<>();                                     // {user, due, done}
            List<Set<String>> refTags = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                int t = rnd.nextInt(5), user = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (t == 0 || ref.isEmpty()) {
                    int due = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5), n = rnd.nextInt(4);
                    String[] tags = new String[n];
                    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) tags[i] = pool[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                    int id = s.addTask(user, due, tags);
                    ref.add(new int[] {user, due, 0});
                    refTags.add(new TreeSet<>(Arrays.asList(tags)));
                    if (id != ref.size()) throw new AssertionError("id");
                } else if (t == 1) {
                    int id = 1 + rnd.nextInt(ref.size() + 1);
                    boolean want = id <= ref.size() && ref.get(id - 1)[0] == user && ref.get(id - 1)[2] == 0;
                    if (want) ref.get(id - 1)[2] = 1;
                    if (s.complete(user, id) != want) throw new AssertionError("complete");
                } else {
                    String tag = pool[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                    List<Integer> all = new ArrayList<>(), withTag = new ArrayList<>();
                    int doneN = 0;
                    for (int i = 0; i < ref.size(); i++) {
                        int[] v = ref.get(i);
                        if (v[0] != user) continue;
                        if (v[2] == 0) { all.add(i); if (refTags.get(i).contains(tag)) withTag.add(i); }
                        else if (refTags.get(i).contains(tag)) doneN++;
                    }
                    Comparator<Integer> byDue = (x, y) -> ref.get(x)[1] != ref.get(y)[1] ? Integer.compare(ref.get(x)[1], ref.get(y)[1]) : Integer.compare(x, y);
                    all.sort(byDue); withTag.sort(byDue);
                    List<Integer> wantAll = new ArrayList<>(), wantTag = new ArrayList<>();
                    for (int i : all) wantAll.add(i + 1);
                    for (int i : withTag) wantTag.add(i + 1);
                    if (!s.pending(user).equals(wantAll)) throw new AssertionError("pending");
                    if (!s.pendingWithTag(user, tag).equals(wantTag)) throw new AssertionError("tagged");
                    if (s.doneWithTag(user, tag) != doneN) throw new AssertionError("done count");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Repeated Cancel Or Complete

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class Jobs on a heap. The call add(id, priority) creates a PENDING job and returns true, or returns false if the id was ever used. The call setPriority(id, priority) changes the priority of a PENDING job and returns true, or returns false otherwise. The call cancel(id) returns true when the job is PENDING or already CANCELLED, and returns false when it is DONE or unknown. The call complete(id) returns true when the job is PENDING or already DONE, and returns false when it is CANCELLED or unknown. The call take() marks the highest priority PENDING job DONE, breaking ties by the smaller id, and returns its id, or returns -1 if none exists. The call pending() returns the number of PENDING jobs.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Ids are integers from 1 to 10^5, and priorities are integers from 0 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 3 * 10^5, and any call may repeat.
  • Repeats of an accepted terminal call change nothing and still return true.
  • Return types are boolean for the four state calls and int for take and pending.

Example 1. Input calls add(1,5), add(2,9), setPriority(2,1), take(), take(), take(), complete(2), cancel(2), output true, true, true, 1, 2, -1, true and false.

Example 2. Input calls add(3,4), cancel(3), cancel(3), complete(3), setPriority(3,8), take(), pending(), output true, true, true, false, false, -1 and 0.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above and priority queues.

What This Exercise Changes. A repeated terminal call is accepted without any write, and pending() reads a counter because the heap size includes stale entries.

Hint

What does a heap entry need to carry so that take() can tell it is out of date?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map holds one mutable job record with a priority, a version and a state. The heap holds immutable entries of priority, id and version. A set of live entries is not kept, so the heap may hold several entries for one job, and only one of them matches the record.

A setPriority call changes the record, raises the version and pushes a new entry. The old entry stays in the heap and becomes stale. A cancel or a complete call changes only the record. A take call pops from the top until it meets an entry whose job is pending and whose version equals the record version. It marks that job done and returns the id. A separate counter changes with each pending-to-other move and each add, so pending() never reads the heap size.

A repeated terminal call is handled before any write. A cancel on a cancelled job and a complete on a done job return true and change nothing, so the counter cannot drop twice. A conflicting terminal call returns false.

The invariant is that the record alone decides which jobs are live, every pending job has exactly one matching entry in the heap, and the counter equals the number of pending records.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) amortized per call, because each entry is pushed once and popped at most once.
  • Space is O(n + p) for n jobs and p priority changes, because stale entries stay until a take reaches them.
import java.util.*;

public final class RepeatCancelSolution {
    static final class Jobs {
        private enum State { PENDING, DONE, CANCELLED }
        private record Entry(int priority, int id, int version) {}
        private static final class Job { int priority; int version; State state = State.PENDING; }
        private final Map<Integer, Job> table = new HashMap<>();                     // source of truth
        private final PriorityQueue<Entry> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) ->
            a.priority() != b.priority() ? Integer.compare(b.priority(), a.priority()) : Integer.compare(a.id(), b.id()));
        private int pendingCount;                                                    // derived, never read from heap.size()

        /** Adds a pending job; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: one matching entry per pending job. */
        boolean add(int id, int priority) {
            if (table.containsKey(id)) return false;
            Job j = new Job(); j.priority = priority;
            table.put(id, j);
            heap.add(new Entry(priority, id, 0));
            pendingCount++;
            return true;
        }

        /** Changes the priority of a pending job; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: the old entry becomes stale. */
        boolean setPriority(int id, int priority) {
            Job j = table.get(id);
            if (j == null || j.state != State.PENDING) return false;
            j.priority = priority;
            j.version++;                                                             // raising the version marks older entries stale
            heap.add(new Entry(priority, id, j.version));
            return true;
        }

        /** Cancels a job; O(1) time and space; invariant: a repeat changes nothing and the counter drops once. */
        boolean cancel(int id) {
            Job j = table.get(id);
            if (j == null || j.state == State.DONE) return false;
            if (j.state == State.CANCELLED) return true;                             // idempotent repeat
            j.state = State.CANCELLED;
            pendingCount--;
            return true;
        }

        /** Completes a job; O(1) time and space; invariant: a repeat changes nothing and the counter drops once. */
        boolean complete(int id) {
            Job j = table.get(id);
            if (j == null || j.state == State.CANCELLED) return false;
            if (j.state == State.DONE) return true;                                  // idempotent repeat
            j.state = State.DONE;
            pendingCount--;
            return true;
        }

        /** Takes the best pending job; O(log n) amortized time, O(1) space; invariant: stale entries are discarded once. */
        int take() {
            while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
                Entry e = heap.poll();
                Job j = table.get(e.id());
                // An entry is live only when the record is pending and carries the same version.
                if (j.state == State.PENDING && j.version == e.version()) {
                    j.state = State.DONE;
                    pendingCount--;
                    return e.id();
                }
            }
            return -1;
        }

        int pending() { return pendingCount; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Jobs a = new Jobs();
        if (!a.add(1, 5) || !a.add(2, 9) || !a.setPriority(2, 1) || a.take() != 1 || a.take() != 2 || a.take() != -1 || !a.complete(2) || a.cancel(2)) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Jobs b = new Jobs();
        if (!b.add(3, 4) || !b.cancel(3) || !b.cancel(3) || b.complete(3) || b.setPriority(3, 8) || b.take() != -1 || b.pending() != 0) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random calls checked against a scan of a plain map with no heap.
        Random rnd = new Random(7003);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Jobs s = new Jobs();
            Map<Integer, int[]> ref = new HashMap<>();                               // id -> {priority, state 0 pending 1 done 2 cancelled}
            for (int op = 0; op < 150; op++) {
                int id = rnd.nextInt(6), t = rnd.nextInt(6), p = rnd.nextInt(4);
                int[] v = ref.get(id);
                if (t == 0) {
                    boolean want = v == null;
                    if (want) ref.put(id, new int[] {p, 0});
                    if (s.add(id, p) != want) throw new AssertionError("add");
                } else if (t == 1) {
                    boolean want = v != null && v[1] == 0;
                    if (want) v[0] = p;
                    if (s.setPriority(id, p) != want) throw new AssertionError("setPriority");
                } else if (t == 2) {
                    boolean want = v != null && v[1] != 1;
                    if (want) v[1] = 2;
                    if (s.cancel(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("cancel");
                } else if (t == 3) {
                    boolean want = v != null && v[1] != 2;
                    if (want) v[1] = 1;
                    if (s.complete(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("complete");
                } else {
                    int best = -1;
                    for (int i : ref.keySet()) {
                        int[] w = ref.get(i);
                        if (w[1] != 0) continue;
                        if (best == -1 || w[0] > ref.get(best)[0] || (w[0] == ref.get(best)[0] && i < best)) best = i;
                    }
                    if (best != -1) ref.get(best)[1] = 1;
                    if (s.take() != best) throw new AssertionError("take");
                }
                int count = 0;
                for (int[] w : ref.values()) if (w[1] == 0) count++;
                if (s.pending() != count) throw new AssertionError("pending count");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Design Ride Sharing System

LeetCode 3829

Problem Specification. This contract is written for the chapter, so the LeetCode statement may differ in detail. Design a class Rides with separate id spaces for riders and drivers. The call addRider(riderId) puts a rider in state WAITING, and addDriver(driverId) puts a driver in state AVAILABLE. Each returns false if that id was ever used. The call cancelRider(riderId) moves a WAITING rider to CANCELLED. The call removeDriver(driverId) moves an AVAILABLE driver to OFFLINE. The call finishTrip(driverId) moves a BUSY driver back to AVAILABLE. Each of these three returns true when the move is legal and false otherwise. The call match() pairs the rider who has waited longest with the AVAILABLE driver of smallest id, marks the rider MATCHED and the driver BUSY, and returns [driverId, riderId]. If either side has nobody, it returns [-1, -1] and changes nothing. The calls waiting() and available() return the two live counts.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Ids are integers from 1 to 10^5 in each id space.
  • Calls number at most 3 * 10^5.
  • Order of riders is the order of their successful addRider calls.
  • Return types are boolean for the state calls, int[] of length 2 for match, and int for the counts.

Example 1. Input calls addRider(10), addRider(11), addDriver(5), addDriver(3), match(), cancelRider(11), match(), waiting(), available(), output true, true, true, true, [3,10], true, [-1,-1], 0 and 1.

Example 2. Input calls addDriver(2), addRider(7), cancelRider(7), addRider(8), match(), removeDriver(2), finishTrip(2), removeDriver(2), addDriver(2), output true, true, true, true, [2,8], false, true, true and false.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Both sides are checked for a live candidate before either status changes, so a match with no driver leaves the waiting rider in place. This contract uses a longest-waiting rider and a smallest-id driver, and it may differ from the LeetCode statement.

Hint

Which side can remove an entry by key, and which side needs stale entries to be skipped?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Two hash maps hold the authoritative state of riders and drivers. Each maps an id to its state. A rider is WAITING, MATCHED or CANCELLED. A driver is AVAILABLE, BUSY or OFFLINE. Two live counters follow those states.

The available drivers live in a tree set of ids, so a driver leaves it by key in O(log n), and the first element is the smallest id. The waiting riders live in a FIFO queue in arrival order. A queue cannot remove a rider from the middle, so cancelRider only writes the state. The queued id becomes a stale entry, and match discards stale entries from the front.

A match call first discards stale riders from the front of the queue, and then checks that the queue and the driver set are both non-empty. If either is empty it returns [-1, -1] and has written nothing apart from the discards, which change no state. Otherwise it removes both fronts, writes both new states and moves both counters. Because both checks come before the first status write, a match with no driver cannot consume a rider.

The invariant is that the state maps alone decide who is live, the driver set holds exactly the AVAILABLE drivers, and every WAITING rider has exactly one queue entry in arrival order.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) amortized per call, because the driver set costs O(log n) per change and each rider entry is queued once and discarded at most once.
  • Space is O(r + d) for r riders and d drivers, because every id owns one state and at most one entry.
import java.util.*;

public final class RideMatchingSolution {
    static final class Rides {
        private enum R { WAITING, MATCHED, CANCELLED }
        private enum D { AVAILABLE, BUSY, OFFLINE }
        private final Map<Integer, R> rider = new HashMap<>();                       // source of truth for riders
        private final Map<Integer, D> driver = new HashMap<>();                      // source of truth for drivers
        private final ArrayDeque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();                // derived: rider ids in arrival order, may hold stale ids
        private final TreeSet<Integer> free = new TreeSet<>();                       // derived: exactly the AVAILABLE drivers
        private int waiting;                                                         // derived live counter

        /** Registers a waiting rider; O(1) time and space; invariant: one queue entry per waiting rider. */
        boolean addRider(int id) {
            if (rider.containsKey(id)) return false;
            rider.put(id, R.WAITING);
            queue.addLast(id);
            waiting++;
            return true;
        }

        /** Registers an available driver; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: free holds exactly the available ids. */
        boolean addDriver(int id) {
            if (driver.containsKey(id)) return false;
            driver.put(id, D.AVAILABLE);
            free.add(id);
            return true;
        }

        /** Cancels a waiting rider; O(1) time and space; invariant: the queue entry becomes stale and the counter drops once. */
        boolean cancelRider(int id) {
            if (rider.get(id) != R.WAITING) return false;
            rider.put(id, R.CANCELLED);
            waiting--;
            return true;
        }

        /** Takes an available driver offline; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: the id leaves free in the same call. */
        boolean removeDriver(int id) {
            if (driver.get(id) != D.AVAILABLE) return false;
            driver.put(id, D.OFFLINE);
            free.remove(id);
            return true;
        }

        /** Returns a busy driver to the available set; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: free gains the id. */
        boolean finishTrip(int id) {
            if (driver.get(id) != D.BUSY) return false;
            driver.put(id, D.AVAILABLE);
            free.add(id);
            return true;
        }

        /** Pairs the longest waiting rider with the smallest free driver; O(log n) amortized time, O(1) space; invariant: both checks precede both writes. */
        int[] match() {
            // Discard stale riders at the front; this changes no state.
            while (!queue.isEmpty() && rider.get(queue.peekFirst()) != R.WAITING) queue.pollFirst();
            if (queue.isEmpty() || free.isEmpty()) return new int[] {-1, -1};
            int r = queue.pollFirst();
            int d = free.pollFirst();
            rider.put(r, R.MATCHED);
            driver.put(d, D.BUSY);
            waiting--;
            return new int[] {d, r};
        }

        int waiting() { return waiting; }

        int available() { return free.size(); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Rides a = new Rides();
        a.addRider(10); a.addRider(11); a.addDriver(5); a.addDriver(3);
        if (!Arrays.equals(a.match(), new int[] {3, 10}) || !a.cancelRider(11) || !Arrays.equals(a.match(), new int[] {-1, -1}) || a.waiting() != 0 || a.available() != 1) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Rides b = new Rides();
        if (!b.addDriver(2) || !b.addRider(7) || !b.cancelRider(7) || !b.addRider(8)) throw new AssertionError("e2 setup");
        if (!Arrays.equals(b.match(), new int[] {2, 8}) || b.removeDriver(2) || !b.finishTrip(2) || !b.removeDriver(2) || b.addDriver(2)) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random calls checked against scans of plain state maps.
        Random rnd = new Random(3829);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Rides s = new Rides();
            Map<Integer, Integer> rs = new HashMap<>();                              // rider -> 0 waiting 1 matched 2 cancelled
            Map<Integer, Integer> ds = new HashMap<>();                              // driver -> 0 available 1 busy 2 offline
            List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 150; op++) {
                int id = rnd.nextInt(6), t = rnd.nextInt(6);
                if (t == 0) {
                    boolean want = !rs.containsKey(id);
                    if (want) { rs.put(id, 0); order.add(id); }
                    if (s.addRider(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("addRider");
                } else if (t == 1) {
                    boolean want = !ds.containsKey(id);
                    if (want) ds.put(id, 0);
                    if (s.addDriver(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("addDriver");
                } else if (t == 2) {
                    boolean want = Integer.valueOf(0).equals(rs.get(id));
                    if (want) rs.put(id, 2);
                    if (s.cancelRider(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("cancelRider");
                } else if (t == 3) {
                    boolean want = Integer.valueOf(0).equals(ds.get(id));
                    if (want) ds.put(id, 2);
                    if (s.removeDriver(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("removeDriver");
                } else if (t == 4) {
                    boolean want = Integer.valueOf(1).equals(ds.get(id));
                    if (want) ds.put(id, 0);
                    if (s.finishTrip(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("finishTrip");
                } else {
                    int r = -1, d = -1;
                    for (int x : order) if (rs.get(x) == 0) { r = x; break; }
                    for (int x : new TreeSet<>(ds.keySet())) if (ds.get(x) == 0) { d = x; break; }
                    int[] want = r == -1 || d == -1 ? new int[] {-1, -1} : new int[] {d, r};
                    if (want[0] != -1) { rs.put(r, 1); ds.put(d, 1); }
                    if (!Arrays.equals(s.match(), want)) throw new AssertionError("match");
                }
                int w = 0, f = 0;
                for (int v : rs.values()) if (v == 0) w++;
                for (int v : ds.values()) if (v == 0) f++;
                if (s.waiting() != w || s.available() != f) throw new AssertionError("counts");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Unlocked Combinations

A search box suggests the three most used phrases for each typed prefix. A ride service must find a waiting driver after riders cancel, drivers go offline and trips finish. The first service needs a trie that also carries ranking data. The second needs several indexes that agree after each call. The two closing lessons pair the techniques that make both work.

These lessons combine the prefix, ledger, feed and lifecycle techniques from the seven lessons before them.

Pairings Taught In This Chapter

Combination Representative problem
Trie + Ranked Service LeetCode 677 Map Sum Pairs and LeetCode 642 Design Search Autocomplete System
Simulation + Multiple Indexes LeetCode 348 Design Tic-Tac-Toe and LeetCode 3829 Design Ride Sharing System

The lesson Rank Words By Prefix requires every prefix node to repair its ranked list whenever a count changes. The lesson Keep Several Indexes In Step requires one writer that removes the old record and inserts the new one in every index.

Deferred Pairing

Concurrent service state is not taught here. Safe access from several threads belongs to the later concurrency curriculum, so each class in this chapter assumes one caller at a time.

Rank Words By Prefix

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Suggestion Box That Lags

A search box shows the three most popular searches that start with the text typed so far. The first version stores every search with its count. On each keystroke it keeps the searches that start with the typed text, sorts them and shows three. With a thousand stored searches nobody notices. With a million, every keystroke costs a full pass, and the suggestions arrive after the user typed the next letter.

A second failure is quieter. Two searches with the same count swap places between calls, because the sort has no rule for ties. The box flickers although no count changed.

This lesson combines prefix navigation with ranking. It asks how to answer "the best three words that start with this text" without touching words that do not start with it, and how to keep that answer exact while counts grow.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Earlier Lessons Contribute

A Walk That Costs One Step Per Letter

The lesson Answer Prefix Queries With A Trie supplied the trie walk. A query follows one child per letter, so its cost depends on the prefix length and not on the number of stored words. That lesson also stored an aggregate in every node on a word's path and repaired it from the record that decides. This lesson keeps the walk and the repair, and it changes what the aggregate is.

A Bound And A Tie Rule

The lesson Merge Feeds From Many Sources supplied the rule that a ranked answer keeps only a bounded number of candidates, and that the order of equal candidates is fixed before the bound cuts anything. A feed applies that rule to a merge of several sources. This lesson applies it to the words below one prefix.

The Choice This Lesson Adds

The new work is keeping a bounded ranked answer correct while the numbers behind the ranking change. A sum or a count can be repaired by adding a difference. A bounded ranking cannot always be repaired that way, and the lesson shows which changes keep it exact.

Simple approach

Scanning Every Stored Word

The direct design keeps a map from each word to its count. A query walks the whole map, keeps the words that start with the prefix, sorts them by higher count and then by smaller word, and returns the first three.

final class NaiveRanker {
    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> count = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    void record(String word) { count.merge(word, 1, Integer::sum); }

    java.util.List<String> top(String prefix) {
        java.util.List<String> hits = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        for (String w : count.keySet()) {
            if (w.startsWith(prefix)) hits.add(w);       // every stored word is examined
        }
        hits.sort((a, b) -> count.get(a).equals(count.get(b))
                ? a.compareTo(b) : Integer.compare(count.get(b), count.get(a)));
        return hits.size() > 3 ? hits.subList(0, 3) : hits;
    }
}

The answers are correct and the order of equal counts is fixed. The cost is the only problem.

Predict first: A prefix matches only four of one million stored words. How many words does the query above examine, and how many does the answer need?

It examines all one million words and sorts the four matches. The answer needs only the three best of those four. Almost all of the work is spent on words that the prefix excludes.

Bottleneck

Every Keystroke Pays For Every Word

Let n be the number of stored words and L the longest word length. The query tests each word against the prefix, which costs O(n * L) comparisons. It then sorts the matches, which costs O(m log m) for m matches. A prefix of one letter can match most of the words, so m is close to n.

The record call is cheap, but the query is the call that runs on every keystroke. The work that the answer needs is tiny, because it returns at most three words. The cost comes from rebuilding that answer from scratch each time.

A faster design must keep the answer for every prefix ready, and it must pay a small price when a count changes instead of a large price on every query.

Key idea

Keep The Best Three At Every Letter

Store the words in a trie. Every prefix node stands for one prefix, the text on the path from the root to that node. The root stands for the empty prefix.

What Each Node Keeps

Every prefix node keeps a top list: the words below it, at most three, in ranked order. A query walks one child per letter to the node of its prefix and returns a copy of that node's top list. The walk costs O(L), and the copy costs at most three entries.

The ranking is the rank test: a word comes before another word when its count is higher, and the smaller word comes first when the counts are equal. The test reads the counts from the map that decides. The lists store only words, never counts, so a list cannot hold a stale count.

Repairing On Every Record

A record call raises the count of one word by one. It then visits each prefix node on the word's path, from the root to the last letter. At each node it removes the word from the top list if the word is there, finds the word's place by the rank test and inserts it. It cuts the list back to three.

Only the recorded word moved up. Every other word kept its count, so the other entries of each list keep their relative order. A word that was cut from a list earlier cannot belong in it now, because the words in the list only moved up or stayed. The invariant is that every top list holds exactly the best three words below its prefix under the rank test.

State

One Map And A Trie Of Lists

The service stores one authoritative map and one derived trie.

  • count maps each recorded word to its number of records and is the structure that decides.
  • Node holds 26 child links and one list of at most three words.
  • K is the list bound, fixed at 3.
  • before applies the rank test to two words and reads both counts from count.
  • repair removes a word from one node's list, reinserts it by before and cuts the list to K.

A query for a prefix that has no node returns an empty list. A query for the empty prefix reads the root.

Walkthrough

Following Counts And Lists

Words Moving Inside A List

The first trace records five words and then asks for the prefix te. The pointer call moves along the calls in order. The columns show every count, the list at the node for t and the list at the node for te.

After record(ten) the words tea and ten both have a count of 1, so the smaller word tea leads the list of t. The second record(tea) lifts tea to a count of 2, and the call record(team) adds a word that starts with tea. The query for te copies the list of that node and returns it.

A Word Returning After A Cut

The second trace uses four words that share the prefix b. The list holds three, so the last word is cut when it arrives. The query for be ends at a missing child, and the query for the empty prefix reads the root.

The word bd arrives with a count of 1 and loses the tie to three smaller words, so it is cut. Its second record gives it a count of 2. The call repairs the root, the node for b and the node for bd. In the first two lists, bd now ranks first and pushes bc out.

Code

The Word Ranker In Java

The class keeps the map that decides, and each record repairs the lists on one path.

final class WordRanker {
    private static final int K = 3;

    private static final class Node {
        final Node[] next = new Node[26];
        final java.util.ArrayList<String> top = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    }

    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> count = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final Node root = new Node();

    private boolean before(String a, String b) {
        int ca = count.get(a), cb = count.get(b);
        return ca != cb ? ca > cb : a.compareTo(b) < 0;
    }

    private void repair(Node node, String word) {
        node.top.remove(word);                              // drop the old place of the word
        int at = 0;
        while (at < node.top.size() && before(node.top.get(at), word)) at++;
        if (at < K) {
            node.top.add(at, word);
            if (node.top.size() > K) node.top.remove(K);    // cut the list back to the bound
        }
    }

    void record(String word) {
        count.merge(word, 1, Integer::sum);                 // the map changes first
        Node cur = root;
        repair(cur, word);
        for (int i = 0; i < word.length(); i++) {
            int c = word.charAt(i) - 'a';
            if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
            cur = cur.next[c];
            repair(cur, word);
        }
    }

    java.util.List<String> top(String prefix) {
        Node cur = root;
        for (int i = 0; i < prefix.length() && cur != null; i++) cur = cur.next[prefix.charAt(i) - 'a'];
        return cur == null ? java.util.List.of() : new java.util.ArrayList<>(cur.top);
    }
}

A record call costs O(L * K), because it repairs L + 1 lists of at most K entries. A query costs O(L + K). The space is O(S * K), where S is the number of trie nodes.

When to use it

Bounded Answers Under Change

When A Short List Is Enough

A short list per node stays exact only when every change moves a word up the ranking. Counts that only grow, and indexes that only grow, both qualify. A word that falls out of a list cannot return unless it is recorded again, and a record call visits that list. The invariant is that each list equals the best K words below its prefix after every call.

False Friends And Limits

A call that lowers a count or deletes a word breaks the shortcut. A word that was cut earlier may now deserve a place, and the three stored words give no way to find it. The fix is to keep every word below the prefix in an ordered set per node and read its first three entries. The set holds immutable entries that carry the count, so a change removes the old entry and inserts a new one. A tree set must never contain an entry whose counted fields change in place.

Skip the lists when queries are rare and writes are frequent, because the repair cost then outweighs the saved scans. A sorted array of all words with binary search is also a valid design for a fixed dictionary that never changes counts.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Prefix Maximum Under Overwrite

LeetCode 677

Problem Specification. Design a class PrefixMax. The call put(key, value) stores value for key and replaces any earlier value of that key. The call max(prefix) returns the largest value among all stored keys that start with prefix, or -1 if no stored key starts with it.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys and prefixes are lowercase strings of length 1 to 50.
  • Values are integers from 0 to 10^6, so -1 never collides with a stored value.
  • Calls number at most 5 * 10^4.
  • Overwrite may lower a value.

Example 1. Input calls put(apple,3), put(app,5), max(ap), put(app,2), max(ap), max(b), output 5, then 3, then -1.

Example 2. Input calls put(ab,4), put(ab,9), put(ab,1), max(a), put(ac,6), max(a), max(ab), output 1, then 6, then 1.

Prerequisites. The trie walk and the repair from the record that decides.

What This Exercise Changes. LeetCode sums the values below a prefix. This contract takes the maximum, so a lowered value cannot be repaired by a subtraction and each node keeps all values it covers.

Hint

After an overwrite lowers the value of the key that held the maximum, which value is the maximum now?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from key to value is the record that decides. A trie holds the derived view. Every node on a key's path keeps a tree map from value to the number of stored keys below the node that carry that value. The largest key of that tree map is the maximum for the prefix of the node.

A put call reads the old value of the key from the record. It then walks the key's path. At each node it removes one copy of the old value, if there is one, and adds one copy of the new value. A maximum cannot be repaired by subtraction, because a lowered key may have held the maximum. The multiset answers that case, since the next largest value is already stored.

A max call walks the prefix and returns the last key of the node's tree map, or -1 for a missing node.

The invariant is that every node's multiset equals the multiset of the current values of the keys below it.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L log n) per put and O(L) per max, where L is the key length and n the number of keys. Each path node runs one or two tree map updates.
  • Space is O(n * L), because each key adds one multiset entry to each of its at most L nodes.
import java.util.*;

public final class PrefixMaxSolution {
    static final class PrefixMax {
        private static final class Node {
            final Node[] next = new Node[26];
            final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> values = new TreeMap<>();
        }
        private final Map<String, Integer> valueOf = new HashMap<>();
        private final Node root = new Node();

        /**
         * Stores a value and repairs the multiset of each node on the path.
         * Time: O(L log n).
         * Space: O(L) for new nodes.
         * Invariant: each multiset counts the current values of the keys below its node.
         */
        void put(String key, int value) {
            // The record returns the old value that each path node must forget.
            Integer old = valueOf.put(key, value);
            Node cur = root;
            for (int i = 0; i < key.length(); i++) {
                int c = key.charAt(i) - 'a';
                // A missing child is created on the way down.
                if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
                cur = cur.next[c];
                // One copy of the old value leaves the node; a count of zero deletes the entry.
                if (old != null) cur.values.merge(old, -1, (a, b) -> a + b == 0 ? null : a + b);
                cur.values.merge(value, 1, Integer::sum);
            }
        }

        /** Returns the largest value below the prefix, or -1; O(L) time, O(1) space. */
        int max(String prefix) {
            Node cur = root;
            // The walk stops early when a letter has no child.
            for (int i = 0; i < prefix.length() && cur != null; i++) cur = cur.next[prefix.charAt(i) - 'a'];
            return cur == null || cur.values.isEmpty() ? -1 : cur.values.lastKey();
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        PrefixMax a = new PrefixMax();
        a.put("apple", 3); a.put("app", 5);
        if (a.max("ap") != 5) throw new AssertionError("e1 first");
        a.put("app", 2);
        if (a.max("ap") != 3 || a.max("b") != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1 second");
        PrefixMax b = new PrefixMax();
        b.put("ab", 4); b.put("ab", 9); b.put("ab", 1);
        if (b.max("a") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 first");
        b.put("ac", 6);
        if (b.max("a") != 6 || b.max("ab") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2 second");
        // Random calls checked against a scan of a plain map.
        Random rnd = new Random(4101);
        String[] keys = {"a", "ab", "abc", "b", "ba", "bb", "abb"};
        String[] prefixes = {"a", "ab", "abc", "b", "ba", "bc", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            PrefixMax s = new PrefixMax();
            Map<String, Integer> ref = new HashMap<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 80; op++) {
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    String k = keys[rnd.nextInt(keys.length)];
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(6);
                    ref.put(k, v);
                    s.put(k, v);
                } else {
                    String p = prefixes[rnd.nextInt(prefixes.length)];
                    int want = -1;
                    for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : ref.entrySet()) if (e.getKey().startsWith(p)) want = Math.max(want, e.getValue());
                    if (s.max(p) != want) throw new AssertionError("max " + p);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Count Near Words Within A Budget

LeetCode 676

Problem Specification. Design a class NearDictionary built from an array of words. The call countWithin(query, d) returns the number of distinct dictionary words that have the same length as query and differ from it in at most d positions.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Words and queries are lowercase strings of length 1 to 20.
  • Dictionary holds at most 10^4 words and may repeat a word.
  • Budget d is an integer from 0 to 2.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.

Example 1. Input words hello, hallo, hullo, help, hello and the calls countWithin(hello,0), countWithin(hello,1), countWithin(hxllo,1), output 1, 3 and 3.

Example 2. Input words abc, abd, xyz and the calls countWithin(abz,1), countWithin(abz,2), output 2 and 3.

Prerequisites. The exercise above and the trie walk.

What This Exercise Changes. LeetCode asks for exactly one substituted letter and returns a boolean. This contract counts words and spends a branch budget, so a mismatch costs one unit and a repeated word counts once.

Hint

What should a step do when the letter on the trie edge differs from the query letter?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The trie stores each distinct dictionary word and marks its last node as a word end. A query runs a depth-first search that carries the position in the query and the remaining budget. At each node the search tries every child. A child whose letter equals the query letter costs nothing. Any other child costs one unit and is skipped when the budget is zero. When the position reaches the query length, the search counts one if the node ends a word.

Each dictionary word has exactly one path in the trie, so the search reaches it at most once and a repeated word is counted once. Words of a different length never reach a word end at the right depth, so the length rule needs no extra check.

The invariant is that the search at a node with position p and budget b counts the word ends below it whose letters from position p differ from the query in at most b places.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(25^d * L^(d+1)) per query in the worst case, where d is the budget, at most 2. The search branches into at most 25 wrong letters at each of at most d mismatching positions, and it spends O(L) steps on each branch. The trie size also bounds it.
  • Space is O(L) for the recursion, plus O(S) for the trie with S nodes.
import java.util.*;

public final class NearWordsSolution {
    static final class NearDictionary {
        private static final class Node {
            final Node[] next = new Node[26];
            boolean end;
        }
        private final Node root = new Node();

        NearDictionary(String[] words) {
            for (String w : words) {
                Node cur = root;
                for (int i = 0; i < w.length(); i++) {
                    int c = w.charAt(i) - 'a';
                    if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
                    cur = cur.next[c];
                }
                // A repeated word marks the same node, so it counts once.
                cur.end = true;
            }
        }

        /**
         * Counts the word ends reachable with at most the remaining budget of mismatches.
         * Time: O(25^d * L^(d+1)) in the worst case.
         * Space: O(L) recursion.
         * Invariant: the result counts words below node that match q from pos with at most budget differences.
         */
        private int dfs(Node node, String q, int pos, int budget) {
            // The end of the query counts the node only when a word ends here.
            if (pos == q.length()) return node.end ? 1 : 0;
            int total = 0;
            int want = q.charAt(pos) - 'a';
            for (int c = 0; c < 26; c++) {
                Node child = node.next[c];
                if (child == null) continue;
                // A matching letter is free; any other letter spends one unit of budget.
                int cost = c == want ? 0 : 1;
                if (cost <= budget) total += dfs(child, q, pos + 1, budget - cost);
            }
            return total;
        }

        int countWithin(String query, int d) { return dfs(root, query, 0, d); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        NearDictionary a = new NearDictionary(new String[] {"hello", "hallo", "hullo", "help", "hello"});
