        if (a.countWithin("hello", 0) != 1 || a.countWithin("hello", 1) != 3 || a.countWithin("hxllo", 1) != 3) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        NearDictionary b = new NearDictionary(new String[] {"abc", "abd", "xyz"});
        if (b.countWithin("abz", 1) != 2 || b.countWithin("abz", 2) != 3) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random dictionaries checked against a Hamming distance scan.
        Random rnd = new Random(4102);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            String[] words = new String[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) words[i] = rand(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
            NearDictionary s = new NearDictionary(words);
            Set<String> distinct = new HashSet<>(Arrays.asList(words));
            for (int q = 0; q < 20; q++) {
                String query = rand(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
                int d = rnd.nextInt(3), want = 0;
                for (String w : distinct) {
                    if (w.length() != query.length()) continue;
                    int diff = 0;
                    for (int i = 0; i < w.length(); i++) if (w.charAt(i) != query.charAt(i)) diff++;
                    if (diff <= d) want++;
                }
                if (s.countWithin(query, d) != want) throw new AssertionError("count " + query + " " + d);
            }
        }
    }

    private static String rand(Random rnd, int len) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) sb.append((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
        return sb.toString();
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Prefix And Suffix Filter

LeetCode 745

Problem Specification. Design a class WordFilter built from an array of words. The call f(prefix, suffix) returns the largest index i such that words[i] starts with prefix and ends with suffix, or -1 if no word does.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Words are lowercase strings of length 1 to 7, and the array holds at most 10^4 entries.
  • Prefixes and suffixes are lowercase strings of length 0 to 7, and an empty one matches every word.
  • Duplicates may appear, and the larger index wins.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.

Example 1. Input words apple, ample, maple and the calls f(a,e), f(ap,le), f(m,e), f(z,e), output 1, 0, 2 and -1.

Example 2. Input words bob, bob, bo and the calls f(bo,b), f(bo,o), f(,b), output 1, 2 and 1.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. One walk must satisfy two constraints at the end of a word and at its start. Each word is inserted once per suffix, and each node stores the largest index, which a later word always overwrites.

Hint

Which single string can encode both the suffix and the prefix of a word so that one walk checks both?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Each word is inserted into one trie once for every suffix start, including the empty suffix. The inserted key is the suffix, then the separator {, then the whole word. The separator is the character after z, so the alphabet has 27 letters. The query key is the query suffix, the separator and the query prefix. One walk of that key checks both constraints, because the key begins with a suffix of the word and then continues with the word itself from its first letter.

Every node on an inserted key stores the largest index of any word whose key passes through it. Words arrive in index order, so each new index is at least as large as any stored index, and an overwrite is enough. A repeated word therefore replaces its earlier index. The empty prefix or the empty suffix needs no special case.

The invariant is that a node's value is the largest index among the words with a key that starts with the path to the node.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L^2) per word at construction and O(L) per query, where L is the word length. Each word inserts L + 1 keys of length at most 2L + 1.
  • Space is O(n * L^2) trie nodes in the worst case, since each word contributes L + 1 keys of length O(L).
import java.util.*;

public final class AffixFilterSolution {
    static final class WordFilter {
        private static final class Node {
            final Node[] next = new Node[27];
            int best = -1;
        }
        private final Node root = new Node();

        WordFilter(String[] words) {
            for (int i = 0; i < words.length; i++) {
                String w = words[i];
                // One key per suffix start; the empty suffix is included.
                for (int s = 0; s <= w.length(); s++) {
                    String key = w.substring(s) + "{" + w;
                    Node cur = root;
                    for (int k = 0; k < key.length(); k++) {
                        int c = key.charAt(k) - 'a';
                        if (cur.next[c] == null) cur.next[c] = new Node();
                        cur = cur.next[c];
                        // Later words have larger indexes, so the overwrite keeps the maximum.
                        cur.best = i;
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        /** Returns the largest index of a word with the prefix and the suffix, or -1; O(L) time, O(1) space. */
        int f(String prefix, String suffix) {
            String key = suffix + "{" + prefix;
            Node cur = root;
            // A missing child means no word has both parts.
            for (int k = 0; k < key.length() && cur != null; k++) cur = cur.next[key.charAt(k) - 'a'];
            return cur == null ? -1 : cur.best;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        WordFilter a = new WordFilter(new String[] {"apple", "ample", "maple"});
        if (a.f("a", "e") != 1 || a.f("ap", "le") != 0 || a.f("m", "e") != 2 || a.f("z", "e") != -1) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        WordFilter b = new WordFilter(new String[] {"bob", "bob", "bo"});
        if (b.f("bo", "b") != 1 || b.f("bo", "o") != 2 || b.f("", "b") != 1) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random words checked against a scan from the last index.
        Random rnd = new Random(4103);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            String[] words = new String[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) words[i] = rand(rnd, 1 + rnd.nextInt(4));
            WordFilter s = new WordFilter(words);
            for (int q = 0; q < 20; q++) {
                String p = rand(rnd, rnd.nextInt(4)), x = rand(rnd, rnd.nextInt(4));
                int want = -1;
                for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0 && want == -1; i--) if (words[i].startsWith(p) && words[i].endsWith(x)) want = i;
                if (s.f(p, x) != want) throw new AssertionError("f " + p + " " + x);
            }
        }
    }

    private static String rand(Random rnd, int len) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) sb.append((char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
        return sb.toString();
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Autocomplete With Recency Ties

LeetCode 642

Problem Specification. This chapter writes the contract below itself, and it can differ in detail from LeetCode. Design a class Autocomplete. The call type(c) appends the character c to the current text and returns up to 3 stored sentences that start with that text. The character # instead commits the current text as one use of that sentence, clears the text and returns an empty list. The call retract(sentence) removes one use of a stored sentence and returns true, or returns false if the sentence is not stored. Sentences rank by higher use count, then by more recent last use, then by smaller text.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Sentences hold lowercase letters and spaces, with length 1 to 30, and # follows at least one character.
  • Last use is the order of commits, and retract leaves it unchanged.
  • Calls number at most 10^4.
  • Count of uses reaches zero when a sentence is removed.

Example 1. Input calls go#, gone#, go#, ga# typed one character at a time, then type(g), type(o), type(#), output [go, ga, gone], then [go, gone], then [].

Example 2. Input the commits ab#, ab#, ac#, then retract(ab), type(a), retract(ab), retract(zz), type(c), output true, [ac, ab], true, false and [ac].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The tie rule is recency, uses can be retracted and the answer is not bounded by growth. Each node keeps every sentence below it in an ordered set of immutable entries, and a change swaps the old entry for a new one on the whole path.

Hint

The counts can fall, so which structure per node can still find the next best sentence after a removal?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A hash map from sentence text to an immutable entry of count, last-use stamp and text is the record that decides. A trie is the derived view. Every node on a sentence's path, including the root, holds a tree set of the entries of all sentences below it. The set orders by higher count, then by larger stamp, then by smaller text. The text makes the order total, so removing an entry never removes a different one.

A commit builds a new entry with the count raised by one and the next stamp. It calls one helper that walks the path, removes the old entry from each node, and inserts the new entry. A retract call builds a lowered entry that keeps the old stamp, or none when the count reaches zero, and calls the same helper. A missing sentence returns false before any write. Nodes are never deleted, so the pointer to the current node stays valid.

A type call moves the current-node pointer by one child, and it becomes null when the child is missing. It reads the first three entries of that node's set. A tree set cannot be repaired in place, because the entry fields are the comparator fields. So the code removes the exact old entry before it inserts a new one.

The invariant is that every node's set holds the current entry of every stored sentence below its prefix, and no stale entry.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(L log n) per commit and retract, where L is the sentence length, and O(1) per typed character, since it moves one link and reads three entries.
  • Space is O(n * L), because each sentence has one entry in each of its at most L + 1 node sets.
import java.util.*;

public final class AutocompleteSolution {
    static final class Autocomplete {
        private record Entry(int count, int stamp, String text) {}
        private static final Comparator<Entry> ORDER = (a, b) -> {
            if (a.count() != b.count()) return Integer.compare(b.count(), a.count());
            if (a.stamp() != b.stamp()) return Integer.compare(b.stamp(), a.stamp());
            return a.text().compareTo(b.text());
        };
        private static final class Node {
            final Map<Character, Node> next = new HashMap<>();
            final TreeSet<Entry> ranked = new TreeSet<>(ORDER);
        }
        private final Map<String, Entry> byText = new HashMap<>();
        private final Node root = new Node();
        private final StringBuilder typed = new StringBuilder();
        private Node cur = root;
        private int clock;

        /**
         * Replaces the old entry of a sentence by a new one on the whole path.
         * Time: O(L log n).
         * Space: O(L) for new nodes.
         * Invariant: the old entry leaves a node before the new entry enters it.
         */
        private void swap(String text, Entry old, Entry fresh) {
            Node n = root;
            for (int i = 0; i <= text.length(); i++) {
                // The exact old entry is removed first, so the comparator fields never change inside a set.
                if (old != null) n.ranked.remove(old);
                if (fresh != null) n.ranked.add(fresh);
                if (i < text.length()) {
                    final Character ch = text.charAt(i);
                    n = n.next.computeIfAbsent(ch, k -> new Node());
                }
            }
        }

        /** Appends a character or commits the text; O(1) per character, O(L log n) per commit. */
        List<String> type(char c) {
            if (c == '#') {
                String text = typed.toString();
                Entry old = byText.get(text);
                // A commit raises the count and stamps the entry with a fresh clock value.
                Entry fresh = new Entry(old == null ? 1 : old.count() + 1, ++clock, text);
                swap(text, old, fresh);
                byText.put(text, fresh);
                typed.setLength(0);
                cur = root;
                return List.of();
            }
            typed.append(c);
            cur = cur == null ? null : cur.next.get(c);
            List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
            if (cur != null) for (Entry e : cur.ranked) { if (out.size() == 3) break; out.add(e.text()); }
            return out;
        }

        /** Removes one use of a sentence; O(L log n) time, O(1) extra space. */
        boolean retract(String sentence) {
            Entry old = byText.get(sentence);
            // The precondition is checked before any structure changes.
            if (old == null) return false;
            if (old.count() == 1) {
                swap(sentence, old, null);
                byText.remove(sentence);
            } else {
                Entry fresh = new Entry(old.count() - 1, old.stamp(), sentence);
                swap(sentence, old, fresh);
                byText.put(sentence, fresh);
            }
            return true;
        }
    }

    private static List<String> feed(Autocomplete a, String s) {
        List<String> last = List.of();
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()) last = a.type(c);
        return last;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 and Example 2 from the problem text.
        Autocomplete a = new Autocomplete();
        feed(a, "go#"); feed(a, "gone#"); feed(a, "go#"); feed(a, "ga#");
        if (!a.type('g').equals(List.of("go", "ga", "gone"))) throw new AssertionError("e1 g");
        if (!a.type('o').equals(List.of("go", "gone"))) throw new AssertionError("e1 o");
        if (!a.type('#').isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("e1 #");
        Autocomplete b = new Autocomplete();
        feed(b, "ab#"); feed(b, "ab#"); feed(b, "ac#");
        if (!b.retract("ab") || !b.type('a').equals(List.of("ac", "ab"))) throw new AssertionError("e2 first");
        if (!b.retract("ab") || b.retract("zz") || !b.type('c').equals(List.of("ac"))) throw new AssertionError("e2 second");
        // Random streams checked against a sort of all stored sentences.
        Random rnd = new Random(4104);
        String[] pool = {"a", "b", "ab", "ba", "aa", "abb"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Autocomplete s = new Autocomplete();
            Map<String, int[]> ref = new HashMap<>();
            StringBuilder typed = new StringBuilder();
            int clock = 0;
            for (int op = 0; op < 120; op++) {
                int t = rnd.nextInt(5);
                if (t == 0) {
                    String r = pool[rnd.nextInt(pool.length)];
                    int[] e = ref.get(r);
                    boolean want = e != null;
                    if (want && --e[0] == 0) ref.remove(r);
                    if (s.retract(r) != want) throw new AssertionError("retract");
                } else {
                    char c = t == 1 && typed.length() > 0 ? '#' : (char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(2));
                    List<String> got = s.type(c);
                    List<String> want = new ArrayList<>();
                    if (c == '#') {
                        int[] e = ref.computeIfAbsent(typed.toString(), k -> new int[] {0, 0});
                        e[0]++;
                        e[1] = ++clock;
                        typed.setLength(0);
                    } else {
                        typed.append(c);
                        List<String> hits = new ArrayList<>();
                        for (String k : ref.keySet()) if (k.startsWith(typed.toString())) hits.add(k);
                        hits.sort((x, y) -> {
                            int[] ex = ref.get(x), ey = ref.get(y);
                            if (ex[0] != ey[0]) return Integer.compare(ey[0], ex[0]);
                            if (ex[1] != ey[1]) return Integer.compare(ey[1], ex[1]);
                            return x.compareTo(y);
                        });
                        want = hits.subList(0, Math.min(3, hits.size()));
                    }
                    if (!got.equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("type " + c + " " + got + " " + want);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Keep Several Indexes In Step

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Warehouse Report That Disagrees With Itself

A warehouse service keeps its stock in one table of items. Each item has a code, an aisle and a count. Two screens read that table. One screen shows the total count per aisle, and a second screen lists the item with the fewest units, so a clerk knows what to reorder first. On Monday a clerk moves an item from aisle 1 to aisle 2. The item table shows aisle 2, the least-stocked screen is correct, and the aisle report still counts the item in aisle 1.

Nothing crashed, and no single number looks absurd. Aisle 1 reports too much stock and aisle 2 reports too little, and the totals only drift further apart with every move. The move call updated the table and forgot one of the summaries.

This lesson asks how to build a class whose calls change one stored record and several summaries at once, so that every summary agrees with the record after every call. The same question appears in games that keep line counters, in transit systems that keep route averages, in feeds with visibility rules and in matching services with waiting queues.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Earlier Lessons Contribute

Counters Beside A Board

The lesson Move Pieces On A Board supplied the idea that a count kept per line replaces a scan of the whole board. It also supplied the order of legality checks: reject the move first, then write. This lesson keeps both rules and asks what happens to the counters when a move is taken back.

One Complete Before And After

The lesson Apply Transactions Without Partial Changes supplied the rule that a failed call changes nothing and that an open journey is state like any other record. This lesson uses that rule to separate a plan from a commit, and it adds a derived ranking of routes that the commit must repair.

Visibility Before The Cut

The lesson Merge Feeds From Many Sources supplied the merge of several newest-first lists and the rule that filtering happens before the output limit. This lesson keeps the merge and adds sources whose visibility changes between calls.

Status As The Only Truth

The lesson Track Status And Pick The Next Task supplied the rule that a lifecycle status is authoritative and that a queue shows only live entities. This lesson adds a second index over the same entities, so one status change must repair two structures.

The Choice This Lesson Adds

The new work is the single path that writes. Earlier lessons repaired indexes inside each method. Here every public call builds the old and the new version of a record and hands both to one method, and that method repairs every summary.

Simple approach

Each Method Repairs What It Remembers

The direct design stores items in a map, keeps a map of totals per aisle and keeps a sorted set for the least-stocked screen. Each public method updates the structures that its author had in mind.

final class NaiveShelf {
    record Item(String sku, String aisle, int qty) {}
    private final java.util.Map<String, Item> items = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> aisleTotal = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.TreeSet<Item> lowest = new java.util.TreeSet<>(
            java.util.Comparator.comparingInt(Item::qty).thenComparing(Item::sku));

    void receive(String sku, String aisle, int n) {
        Item old = items.get(sku);
        Item next = new Item(sku, aisle, (old == null ? 0 : old.qty()) + n);
        if (old != null) lowest.remove(old);
        items.put(sku, next);
        lowest.add(next);
        aisleTotal.merge(aisle, n, Integer::sum);
    }

    void transfer(String sku, String aisle) {
        Item old = items.get(sku);
        Item next = new Item(sku, aisle, old.qty());
        lowest.remove(old);
        items.put(sku, next);                    // the aisle totals stay as they were
        lowest.add(next);
    }
}

After receive("a", "A1", 5) and transfer("a", "A2"), the total of A1 is still 5 and the total of A2 is 0.

Predict first: Which structure does transfer forget, and what does the aisle report show for a call to total("A1") and total("A2") after one transfer of five units?

The transfer forgets the map of aisle totals. A1 still reports 5 units, which are no longer there, and A2 reports 0 units, which it now holds. Each later move widens the gap, and nothing in the item table reveals it.

Bottleneck

Every Method Repeats The Same Repairs

The naive design is fast. Each call runs a constant number of hash and tree operations, so the cost is O(log n) per call. The weakness is correctness, and it grows with every new method.

A class with k public methods and m summaries has k times m repair decisions, and the author makes each decision again in each method. Receive remembers the sorted set. Transfer remembers the sorted set and forgets the totals. A ship method written later may remember neither. A single missed decision leaves a summary that disagrees with the record, and no call ever corrects it.

Rebuilding the summaries from the record on every query repairs the disagreement. That costs O(n) per query, and a service with 10^5 items and 10^5 queries then needs 10^10 steps. The class needs a way to keep every summary correct after every write at the price of O(log n).

Key idea

One Writer Updates Every Summary

Split the stored data into two kinds. The authoritative state is the one map from key to record, and a call may never contradict it. A derived index is any other structure that can be rebuilt from that map, such as a total per group, a sorted set or a counter per line.

Give the class one single writer, a private method that takes the old record, or none, and the new record, or none. It reads both versions, removes the old version from every derived index and inserts the new version. Receive, ship and transfer each decide what the new record is and then call the writer. No public method touches a derived index.

Why Old And New Both Go In

A total per group changes by the difference between the new and the old count, and the new group may differ from the old group. A sorted set must find the old entry by its old fields before the new entry exists. The writer sees both versions, so it applies both repairs without guessing what the caller meant.

Why Checks Come First

The writer never rejects a call. Every public method validates its preconditions, builds the new record and only then calls the writer. A rejected call returns before the first write, so no summary is half repaired.

The invariant is that the map and every derived index describe the same records after each call, and only the writer writes them.

State

Three Structures And One Method

The shelf class stores one map, two derived structures and one writer.

  • items maps each sku to its immutable record of sku, aisle and count, and is the authoritative state.
  • aisleTotal maps each aisle to the sum of the counts of its items. An aisle whose sum reaches zero is deleted.
  • lowest is a tree set of all records, ordered by smaller count and then smaller sku.
  • replace is the only method that writes the three structures.

Records are immutable. A change builds a new record, so a record never changes while it sits in the tree set.

Walkthrough

Following Calls Through Every View

A Move And A Shipment

The first trace receives stock in two aisles, moves an item and ships the rest of another. The pointer call marks the call that just ran. The variable lowest lists the records in set order, and totals lists the sum per aisle.

The transfer at call 3 moves the two units of b from A1 to A2. The totals change in the same step as the item table, so the report never disagrees with the record.

Rejected Calls Change Nothing

The second trace shows calls that fail validation. Each rejected call returns false and leaves all three structures as they were.

The shipment of five units at call 2 fails because x holds three. The later shipment of three units empties x, so replace receives a new record of none and removes x from the sorted set, and the total of A1 drops to the three units of y.

Code

The Shelf In Java

The class routes every write through replace, and each public method checks its preconditions before it builds the new record.

final class Shelf {
    private record Item(String sku, String aisle, int qty) {}
    private final java.util.Map<String, Item> items = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.Map<String, Integer> aisleTotal = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.TreeSet<Item> lowest = new java.util.TreeSet<>(
            java.util.Comparator.comparingInt(Item::qty).thenComparing(Item::sku));

    private void replace(Item old, Item next) {
        if (old != null) {
            items.remove(old.sku());
            lowest.remove(old);                               // the old fields find the old entry
            if (aisleTotal.merge(old.aisle(), -old.qty(), Integer::sum) == 0) aisleTotal.remove(old.aisle());
        }
        if (next != null) {
            items.put(next.sku(), next);
            lowest.add(next);
            aisleTotal.merge(next.aisle(), next.qty(), Integer::sum);
        }
    }

    boolean ship(String sku, int n) {
        Item old = items.get(sku);
        if (old == null || n <= 0 || old.qty() < n) return false;   // validate before any write
        replace(old, old.qty() == n ? null : new Item(sku, old.aisle(), old.qty() - n));
        return true;
    }
}

Each call runs a fixed number of hash and tree operations, so the time is O(log n). The space is O(n), because every item appears in the map and in the sorted set, and every aisle appears once.

When to use it

Spotting A Class With Many Views

List The Writes And The Views

Write down every public call that creates, changes or removes a record. Beside each call, list every summary it must repair. If a summary appears in the list of two calls, those calls need a shared writer. The invariant is that the record and every derived index describe the same data after each call, including calls that undo or cancel.

Undo Is Another Writer Call

A call that takes back an earlier change does not need special code when the writer takes an old and a new record. Undo passes the current record as the old one and the logged record as the new one. A log of old records is the only extra state.

False Friends And Limits

Recomputing a summary from the record on demand looks like a false friend when queries are rare, since it needs no writer. It costs O(n) per query, so it fails when queries are frequent. A lazy heap with stale entries is a valid alternative for a single ranking, but it fails for totals and counters, because those need exact decrements. Skip a summary when no call reads it, because it costs memory and one repair per write.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Tic-Tac-Toe With Undo

LeetCode 348

Problem Specification. Design a class UndoBoard for an n by n grid and players 1 and 2. The call move(row, col, player) places a mark and returns 0 if no player has won, returns player if that move completes a full row, column, main diagonal or anti-diagonal of one player, and returns -1 if the call is rejected. A call is rejected if the cell is occupied or if a player has already won. The call undo() removes the latest accepted move, returns true, and returns false if no accepted move exists. The call winner() returns the winning player or 0.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Size satisfies 1 <= n <= 1000, and players are 1 or 2, in any order.
  • Cells satisfy 0 <= row, col < n.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Rejected calls change nothing, and an undo of a winning move clears the winner.

Example 1. Input n = 3 and the calls move(0,0,1), move(1,1,1), move(0,0,2), move(2,2,1), move(0,1,2), undo(), move(0,1,2), output 0, 0, -1, 1, -1, true, 0.

Example 2. Input n = 2 and the calls undo(), move(0,0,2), move(0,1,2), winner(), undo(), winner(), output false, 0, 2, 2, true, 0.

Prerequisites. The shelf class above and its single writer.

What This Exercise Changes. The call adds an undo and a move log, so every counter, the grid and the winner must return to the state before the move.

Hint

Which counters must a move add to, and which numbers must an undo subtract from?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The grid of owners is the authoritative state. The derived indexes are four counter families per player: one count per row, one per column, one for the main diagonal and one for the anti-diagonal. A single method, apply, takes a cell, a player and a step of +1 or -1. It adds the step to every counter that the cell belongs to, and it writes the grid cell. A move validates first: the cell must be empty and no winner may exist. It then logs the cell, calls apply with +1 and checks whether a counter of that player reached n.

An undo pops the log and calls apply with -1 for the logged cell and player. This restores the grid and every counter in one call, with no special code. A winning move is always the latest accepted move, because later moves are rejected. So an undo that pops the winning move clears the winner, and any other undo leaves the winner at 0.

The invariant says each counter equals the number of marks of its player on its line, and the log holds the accepted moves in order.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per call, because a move touches at most four counters and an undo reverses the same four.
  • Space is O(n + m), where m is the number of accepted moves held in the log, plus 2 * (2n + 2) counters.
import java.util.*;

public final class BoardUndoSolution {
    static final class UndoBoard {
        private final int n;
        private final int[][] owner;
        private final int[][] rows, cols;
        private final int[] diag = new int[3], anti = new int[3];
        private final Deque<int[]> log = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private int winner;

        UndoBoard(int n) {
            this.n = n;
            owner = new int[n][n];
            rows = new int[3][n];
            cols = new int[3][n];
        }

        /**
         * The only method that writes the grid and the counters.
         * Time: O(1).
         * Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: each counter equals the marks of its player on its line.
         */
        private void apply(int r, int c, int p, int step) {
            owner[r][c] = step > 0 ? p : 0;
            rows[p][r] += step;
            cols[p][c] += step;
            // The diagonals change only for cells that lie on them.
            if (r == c) diag[p] += step;
            if (r + c == n - 1) anti[p] += step;
        }

        /** Returns 0, the winning player or -1 for a rejected call; O(1) time and space. */
        int move(int r, int c, int p) {
            // Both checks run before the first write.
            if (winner != 0 || owner[r][c] != 0) return -1;
            apply(r, c, p, 1);
            log.push(new int[] {r, c, p});
            boolean won = rows[p][r] == n || cols[p][c] == n
                    || (r == c && diag[p] == n) || (r + c == n - 1 && anti[p] == n);
            if (won) winner = p;
            return won ? p : 0;
        }

        /** Takes back the latest accepted move; O(1) time and space; invariant: the counters return to the earlier state. */
        boolean undo() {
            if (log.isEmpty()) return false;
            int[] m = log.pop();
            apply(m[0], m[1], m[2], -1);
            // A win can only come from the latest move, so any undo clears it.
            winner = 0;
            return true;
        }

        int winner() { return winner; }
    }

    // Brute-force reference: rescans the whole grid after every call.
    static int scanWinner(int[][] g, int n) {
        for (int p = 1; p <= 2; p++) {
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                boolean row = true, col = true;
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) { row &= g[i][j] == p; col &= g[j][i] == p; }
                if (row || col) return p;
            }
            boolean d = true, a = true;
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { d &= g[i][i] == p; a &= g[i][n - 1 - i] == p; }
            if (d || a) return p;
        }
        return 0;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        UndoBoard a = new UndoBoard(3);
        int[] got = {a.move(0, 0, 1), a.move(1, 1, 1), a.move(0, 0, 2), a.move(2, 2, 1), a.move(0, 1, 2)};
        if (!Arrays.equals(got, new int[] {0, 0, -1, 1, -1})) throw new AssertionError("e1 moves");
        if (!a.undo() || a.move(0, 1, 2) != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 undo");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        UndoBoard b = new UndoBoard(2);
        if (b.undo() || b.move(0, 0, 2) != 0 || b.move(0, 1, 2) != 2 || b.winner() != 2) throw new AssertionError("e2 win");
        if (!b.undo() || b.winner() != 0) throw new AssertionError("e2 undo");
        // Random calls checked against a full rescan of the grid.
        Random rnd = new Random(9201);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            UndoBoard s = new UndoBoard(n);
            int[][] g = new int[n][n];
            Deque<int[]> hist = new ArrayDeque<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                if (rnd.nextInt(4) == 0) {
                    boolean want = !hist.isEmpty();
                    if (want) { int[] m = hist.pop(); g[m[0]][m[1]] = 0; }
                    if (s.undo() != want) throw new AssertionError("undo");
                } else {
                    int r = rnd.nextInt(n), c = rnd.nextInt(n), p = 1 + rnd.nextInt(2);
                    int want;
                    if (scanWinner(g, n) != 0 || g[r][c] != 0) want = -1;
                    else { g[r][c] = p; hist.push(new int[] {r, c, p}); want = scanWinner(g, n); }
                    if (s.move(r, c, p) != want) throw new AssertionError("move");
                }
                if (s.winner() != scanWinner(g, n)) throw new AssertionError("winner");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Underground System With Cancel

LeetCode 1396

Problem Specification. Design a class Metro for stations named by lowercase strings. The call checkIn(card, station, t) opens a trip and returns true, and returns false if the card already has an open trip. The call checkOut(card, station, t) closes the open trip, records its duration t - startTime for the route from the check-in station to this station, and returns true. It returns false, and leaves the trip open, if the card has no open trip or if t is less than the check-in time. The call cancel(card) discards an open trip without recording a duration and returns true, or returns false if none is open. The call average(start, end) returns the mean duration of recorded trips on that route, or -1.0 if none. The call slowest() returns the route written start>end with the largest mean duration, with the smaller route string winning ties, or the empty string if no trip is recorded.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number at most 10^5, and times satisfy 0 <= t <= 10^6.
  • Stations are non-empty lowercase strings, and a route may start and end at the same station.
  • Cards are integers.
  • Return of average is a double, and slowest compares means exactly, without rounding.

Example 1. Input the calls checkIn(7,a,3), checkIn(7,b,4), checkOut(7,c,13), checkIn(8,a,0), checkOut(8,b,5), slowest(), average(a,b), output true, false, true, true, true, a>c, 5.0.

Example 2. Input the calls checkIn(1,x,10), checkOut(1,y,9), cancel(1), cancel(1), slowest(), average(x,y), output true, false, true, false, the empty string, -1.0.

Prerequisites. The first exercise above and the rule that checks run before writes.

What This Exercise Changes. A cancel call is a second exit for an open trip, and the slowest-route report adds a sorted index that every recorded trip must repair.

Hint

The mean of a route changes at every checkout, so which entry of a sorted set must leave before it changes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Two maps hold the authoritative state: open trips by card, and route statistics by route string. A route statistic is an immutable record of route, duration sum and trip count. The derived index is a tree set of those records. It orders by larger mean first and then by smaller route string. The comparison of two means uses cross multiplication of sums and counts, so no rounding enters the order.

A check-in validates that the card has no open trip and stores the station and time. A check-out validates that a trip is open and that the time is not earlier than the check-in time. It then calls the single writer record, which removes the old statistic from the map and the set, builds a new statistic with the added duration and inserts it into both. A cancel removes the open trip and touches nothing else, because an open trip is in no sorted index.

The invariant says that every route in the map has exactly one entry in the set with equal sum and count. The largest sum is 10^5 * 10^6 and the largest count is 10^5, so each product stays below 10^16 and fits in a long.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log R) for a check-out, where R is the number of routes, and O(1) for the other calls and for slowest, which reads the first set entry.
  • Space is O(C + R), where C is the number of open trips, because routes keep one record and one set entry.
import java.util.*;

public final class UndergroundSlowSolution {
    static final class Metro {
        private record Open(String station, int time) {}
        private record Stat(String route, long sum, long count) {}
        // Larger mean first; the route string breaks ties.
        private static final Comparator<Stat> ORDER = (x, y) -> {
            int c = Long.compare(y.sum() * x.count(), x.sum() * y.count());
            return c != 0 ? c : x.route().compareTo(y.route());
        };
        private final Map<Integer, Open> open = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<String, Stat> stats = new HashMap<>();
        private final TreeSet<Stat> byMean = new TreeSet<>(ORDER);

        boolean checkIn(int card, String station, int t) {
            // A card holds at most one open trip.
            if (open.containsKey(card)) return false;
            open.put(card, new Open(station, t));
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * The only method that writes the statistics and their sorted set.
         * Time: O(log R).
         * Space: O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: the old entry leaves the set before the new entry enters.
         */
        private void record(String route, long duration) {
            Stat old = stats.get(route);
            if (old != null) byMean.remove(old);
            Stat next = old == null ? new Stat(route, duration, 1) : new Stat(route, old.sum() + duration, old.count() + 1);
            stats.put(route, next);
            byMean.add(next);
        }

        boolean checkOut(int card, String station, int t) {
            Open o = open.get(card);
            // Both checks run before the first write.
            if (o == null || t < o.time()) return false;
            open.remove(card);
            record(o.station() + ">" + station, t - o.time());
            return true;
        }

        boolean cancel(int card) { return open.remove(card) != null; }

        /** Returns the mean duration of a route or -1.0; O(1) time and space. */
        double average(String start, String end) {
            Stat s = stats.get(start + ">" + end);
            return s == null ? -1.0 : (double) s.sum() / s.count();
        }

        /** Returns the route with the largest mean, or the empty string; O(1) time and space. */
        String slowest() { return byMean.isEmpty() ? "" : byMean.first().route(); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        Metro a = new Metro();
        boolean ok = a.checkIn(7, "a", 3) && !a.checkIn(7, "b", 4) && a.checkOut(7, "c", 13)
                && a.checkIn(8, "a", 0) && a.checkOut(8, "b", 5);
        if (!ok || !a.slowest().equals("a>c") || a.average("a", "b") != 5.0) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        Metro b = new Metro();
        if (!b.checkIn(1, "x", 10) || b.checkOut(1, "y", 9) || !b.cancel(1) || b.cancel(1)) throw new AssertionError("e2 calls");
        if (!b.slowest().isEmpty() || b.average("x", "y") != -1.0) throw new AssertionError("e2 reports");
        // Random calls checked against a rescan of all recorded trips.
        Random rnd = new Random(9202);
        String[] st = {"a", "b", "c"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Metro s = new Metro();
            Map<Integer, Object[]> open = new HashMap<>();
            List<Object[]> done = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                int card = rnd.nextInt(4), t = rnd.nextInt(20), k = rnd.nextInt(4);
                String station = st[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                if (k == 0) {
                    boolean want = !open.containsKey(card);
                    if (want) open.put(card, new Object[] {station, t});
                    if (s.checkIn(card, station, t) != want) throw new AssertionError("checkIn");
                } else if (k == 1) {
                    Object[] o = open.get(card);
                    boolean want = o != null && t >= (int) o[1];
                    if (want) { open.remove(card); done.add(new Object[] {o[0] + ">" + station, t - (int) o[1]}); }
                    if (s.checkOut(card, station, t) != want) throw new AssertionError("checkOut");
                } else if (k == 2) {
                    if (s.cancel(card) != (open.remove(card) != null)) throw new AssertionError("cancel");
                } else {
                    Map<String, long[]> agg = new TreeMap<>();
                    for (Object[] d : done) { long[] v = agg.computeIfAbsent((String) d[0], x -> new long[2]); v[0] += (int) d[1]; v[1]++; }
                    String best = "";
                    for (Map.Entry<String, long[]> e : agg.entrySet()) {
                        if (best.isEmpty()) { best = e.getKey(); continue; }
                        long[] bv = agg.get(best), ev = e.getValue();
                        // Strictly larger mean wins; the sorted map visits smaller route strings first.
                        if (ev[0] * bv[1] > bv[0] * ev[1]) best = e.getKey();
                    }
                    if (!s.slowest().equals(best)) throw new AssertionError("slowest");
                    String route = st[rnd.nextInt(3)] + ">" + st[rnd.nextInt(3)];
                    long[] v = agg.get(route);
                    double want = v == null ? -1.0 : (double) v[0] / v[1];
                    if (s.average(route.substring(0, 1), route.substring(2)) != want) throw new AssertionError("average");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Twitter With Mute And Delete

LeetCode 355

Problem Specification. Design a class FeedDesk. The call post(user, tweet) publishes a tweet with a distinct id, and recency is the order of the post calls. The calls follow(a, b) and unfollow(a, b) change who a follows, and the calls mute(viewer, author) and unmute(viewer, author) change which followed authors the viewer hides. The call delete(tweet) hides a tweet for everyone and returns true, or returns false if it is unknown or already deleted. The call feed(user, k) returns a Feed of the tweet ids and a source count. The ids are the k newest tweets that the user can see, newest first. A tweet is visible if its author is the user, or if the user follows the author and has not muted the author, and the tweet is not deleted. The source count is the number of distinct authors among the returned ids.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Users and tweet ids are integers, and tweet ids are distinct.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and 1 <= k <= 20.
  • Self follow and mute calls are ignored.
  • Return of feed has at most k ids and a source count from 0 to k.

Example 1. Input the calls post(1,10), post(2,20), post(2,21), post(3,30), follow(1,2), follow(1,3), feed(1,3), mute(1,3), feed(1,3), delete(21), feed(1,3), output ids 30, 21, 20 with 2 sources, then ids 21, 20, 10 with 2 sources, then true and ids 20, 10 with 2 sources.

Example 2. Input the calls post(5,50), follow(4,5), unfollow(4,5), feed(4,2), follow(4,4), post(4,40), feed(4,2), delete(99), output no ids with 0 sources, then ids 40 with 1 source, then false.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Visibility has two new rules, mute and delete, and the result carries a source count, so a filter that runs after the cut returns too few tweets.

Hint

Where in the merge must a muted author and a deleted tweet disappear, so that the output still holds k tweets when enough exist?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The authoritative state is one tree map per author from post time to tweet id, a map from tweet id to its author and time, a follow set per user and a mute set per user. A delete removes the tweet from its author's tree map through the tweet record, so a deleted tweet exists in no list. The single writer for tweets is remove, and post and delete are the only calls that write the author lists.

A feed call collects the visible authors first: the user, plus every followed author who is not muted. Only these authors enter the merge, so a muted author never competes for a place in the result. The merge puts one descending iterator per visible author into a priority queue ordered by the newest unread post time. It then pops until k ids are collected or the queue empties, and pushes the next entry of the same author after each pop. Every popped tweet is visible by construction, so the cut at k counts only visible tweets. The source count is the size of a set of authors seen in the output.

The invariant says the tweet lists hold exactly the undeleted tweets, and visibility depends only on the follow and mute sets read at the time of the call.

Complexity.

  • Time is O((F + k) log F) per feed call, where F is the number of visible authors. Building the queue costs F log F and each of the k pops costs log F. Other calls cost O(log n).
  • Space is O(T + U), where T is the number of tweets and U the number of follow and mute pairs. A feed call adds O(F + k).
import java.util.*;

public final class FeedSourcesSolution {
    record Feed(List<Integer> ids, int sources) {}

    static final class FeedDesk {
        private int clock;
        private final Map<Integer, TreeMap<Integer, Integer>> byAuthor = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<Integer, int[]> tweetInfo = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> follows = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<Integer, Set<Integer>> muted = new HashMap<>();

        void post(int user, int tweet) {
            int time = clock++;
            byAuthor.computeIfAbsent(user, k -> new TreeMap<>()).put(time, tweet);
            tweetInfo.put(tweet, new int[] {user, time});
        }

        void follow(int a, int b) { if (a != b) follows.computeIfAbsent(a, k -> new HashSet<>()).add(b); }

        void unfollow(int a, int b) { if (follows.containsKey(a)) follows.get(a).remove(b); }

        void mute(int v, int author) { if (v != author) muted.computeIfAbsent(v, k -> new HashSet<>()).add(author); }

        void unmute(int v, int author) { if (muted.containsKey(v)) muted.get(v).remove(author); }

        /** Removes a tweet from its author's list; O(log n) time, O(1) space; invariant: no deleted tweet stays in a list. */
        boolean delete(int tweet) {
            int[] info = tweetInfo.remove(tweet);
            if (info == null) return false;
            byAuthor.get(info[0]).remove(info[1]);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Returns the k newest visible tweets and the number of authors among them.
         * Time: O((F + k) log F).
         * Space: O(F + k).
         * Invariant: only visible authors enter the queue, so the cut at k counts visible tweets.
         */
        Feed feed(int user, int k) {
            List<Integer> authors = new ArrayList<>();
            authors.add(user);
            for (int f : follows.getOrDefault(user, Set.of())) {
                // Muted authors leave before the merge starts.
                if (!muted.getOrDefault(user, Set.of()).contains(f)) authors.add(f);
            }
            record Cursor(int author, Iterator<Map.Entry<Integer, Integer>> it, Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> cur) {}
            PriorityQueue<Cursor> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> Integer.compare(y.cur().getKey(), x.cur().getKey()));
            for (int au : authors) {
                TreeMap<Integer, Integer> m = byAuthor.get(au);
                if (m == null || m.isEmpty()) continue;
                Iterator<Map.Entry<Integer, Integer>> it = m.descendingMap().entrySet().iterator();
                pq.add(new Cursor(au, it, it.next()));
            }
            List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>();
            Set<Integer> seen = new HashSet<>();
            while (ids.size() < k && !pq.isEmpty()) {
                Cursor c = pq.poll();
                ids.add(c.cur().getValue());
                seen.add(c.author());
                if (c.it().hasNext()) pq.add(new Cursor(c.author(), c.it(), c.it().next()));
            }
            return new Feed(ids, seen.size());
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        FeedDesk a = new FeedDesk();
        a.post(1, 10); a.post(2, 20); a.post(2, 21); a.post(3, 30);
        a.follow(1, 2); a.follow(1, 3);
        if (!a.feed(1, 3).equals(new Feed(List.of(30, 21, 20), 2))) throw new AssertionError("e1 first");
        a.mute(1, 3);
        if (!a.feed(1, 3).equals(new Feed(List.of(21, 20, 10), 2))) throw new AssertionError("e1 mute");
        if (!a.delete(21) || !a.feed(1, 3).equals(new Feed(List.of(20, 10), 2))) throw new AssertionError("e1 delete");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        FeedDesk b = new FeedDesk();
        b.post(5, 50); b.follow(4, 5); b.unfollow(4, 5);
        if (!b.feed(4, 2).equals(new Feed(List.of(), 0))) throw new AssertionError("e2 empty");
        b.follow(4, 4); b.post(4, 40);
        if (!b.feed(4, 2).equals(new Feed(List.of(40), 1)) || b.delete(99)) throw new AssertionError("e2 self");
        // Random calls checked against a filter of the full tweet list, then a cut.
        Random rnd = new Random(9203);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            FeedDesk s = new FeedDesk();
            List<int[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
            boolean[][] fol = new boolean[4][4], mut = new boolean[4][4];
            Set<Integer> dead = new HashSet<>();
            int nextId = 100;
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                int u = rnd.nextInt(4), v = rnd.nextInt(4), t = rnd.nextInt(7);
                if (t == 0) { all.add(new int[] {u, nextId}); s.post(u, nextId++); }
                else if (t == 1) { if (u != v) fol[u][v] = true; s.follow(u, v); }
                else if (t == 2) { fol[u][v] = false; s.unfollow(u, v); }
                else if (t == 3) { if (u != v) mut[u][v] = true; s.mute(u, v); }
                else if (t == 4) { mut[u][v] = false; s.unmute(u, v); }
                else if (t == 5) {
                    int id = 100 + rnd.nextInt(nextId - 99);
                    boolean want = id < nextId && dead.add(id);
                    if (s.delete(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("delete");
                } else {
                    int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
                    List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>();
                    Set<Integer> src = new HashSet<>();
                    // Newest first: walk the post list from the end and filter before counting.
                    for (int i = all.size() - 1; i >= 0 && ids.size() < k; i--) {
                        int au = all.get(i)[0], id = all.get(i)[1];
                        boolean vis = au == u || (fol[u][au] && !mut[u][au]);
                        if (vis && !dead.contains(id)) { ids.add(id); src.add(au); }
                    }
                    if (!s.feed(u, k).equals(new Feed(ids, src.size()))) throw new AssertionError("feed");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Ride Sharing With Nearest Driver

LeetCode 3829

Problem Specification. This chapter states the contract below itself, and it can differ in detail from LeetCode. Design a class RideDesk on a line of integer positions. The call addDriver(id, pos) registers a free driver and returns true, or returns false if the id was used before. The call goOffline(id) retires a free driver for good and returns true, or returns false otherwise. The call request(rider, pos, dest) adds a waiting rider and returns true, or returns false if the id was used before. The call cancel(rider) ends a waiting rider and returns true, or returns false otherwise. The call match() takes the longest-waiting rider and the free driver with the smallest distance to the rider's position, with the smaller driver id winning ties, marks both as riding, and returns the array {rider, driver}. It returns an empty array and changes nothing if no rider waits or no driver is free. The call finish(rider) ends a riding trip, frees the driver at the rider's destination and returns true, or returns false if the rider is not riding.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Positions and destinations satisfy 0 <= x <= 10^9.
  • Ids of drivers and riders are separate integer spaces.
  • Calls number at most 2 * 10^5.
  • Return of match is an int[] of length 2 or 0.

Example 1. Input the calls addDriver(1,10), addDriver(2,20), request(100,15,40), request(101,30,5), match(), match(), match(), finish(100), output true, true, true, true, then 100 with driver 1, 101 with driver 2, an empty array, and true.

Example 2. Input the calls request(7,5,9), match(), addDriver(3,100), cancel(7), cancel(7), match(), finish(7), output true, an empty array, true, true, false, an empty array, false.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The match policy is nearest driver with the smaller id on ties, and a finished trip moves the driver to a new position, so the free-driver index replaces an entry instead of only removing one.

Hint

Which index finds the nearest free driver, and which two structures must a finished trip repair?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The authoritative state is two maps: one from driver id to a driver record with position and status, and one from rider id to a rider record with position, destination, status and driver. A status is free, riding, offline, waiting, done or cancelled. The two derived indexes are a linked hash set of waiting rider ids in arrival order, and a tree set of free spots, where a spot is an immutable pair of position and driver id. Spots are never changed while they sit in the set. A driver who moves gets a new spot.

Two writer helpers keep the indexes in step. markFree puts a driver at a position with status free and inserts the spot. takeSpot removes the spot of a free driver. Every call validates its status precondition first and then uses the helpers. A match finds the first waiting rider, then the nearest free spot. The nearest spot is the better of two candidates. The right candidate is the ceiling of the rider's position with the smallest id. The left candidate is the smallest-id spot at the largest position below the rider, found by one lower call and one ceiling call. A distance tie goes to the smaller id.

The invariant says that a driver has a spot exactly when the driver is free, and a rider is in the waiting set exactly when the rider is waiting. Both sides of a match are validated before either is written.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log n) per call, because each call runs a constant number of hash, set and tree operations.
  • Space is O(n), since each driver and rider holds one record and at most one index entry.
import java.util.*;

public final class RideMatchSolution {
    static final class RideDesk {
        private static final int FREE = 0, RIDING = 1, OFFLINE = 2, WAITING = 3, DONE = 4, CANCELLED = 5;
        private static final class Driver { int pos, status; }
        private static final class Rider { int pos, dest, status, driver; }
        private record Spot(int pos, int id) {}
        private static final Comparator<Spot> ORDER = (a, b) ->
                a.pos() != b.pos() ? Integer.compare(a.pos(), b.pos()) : Integer.compare(a.id(), b.id());
        private final Map<Integer, Driver> drivers = new HashMap<>();
        private final Map<Integer, Rider> riders = new HashMap<>();
        private final Set<Integer> waiting = new LinkedHashSet<>();
        private final TreeSet<Spot> free = new TreeSet<>(ORDER);

        // Puts a driver at a position and into the free index; O(log n) time, O(1) space.
        private void markFree(int id, Driver d, int pos) {
            d.pos = pos;
            d.status = FREE;
            free.add(new Spot(pos, id));
        }

        // Removes the exact spot of a free driver; O(log n) time, O(1) space.
        private void takeSpot(int id, Driver d) { free.remove(new Spot(d.pos, id)); }

        boolean addDriver(int id, int pos) {
            if (drivers.containsKey(id)) return false;
            Driver d = new Driver();
            drivers.put(id, d);
            markFree(id, d, pos);
            return true;
        }

        boolean goOffline(int id) {
            Driver d = drivers.get(id);
            if (d == null || d.status != FREE) return false;
            takeSpot(id, d);
            d.status = OFFLINE;
            return true;
        }

        boolean request(int id, int pos, int dest) {
            if (riders.containsKey(id)) return false;
            Rider r = new Rider();
            r.pos = pos; r.dest = dest; r.status = WAITING;
            riders.put(id, r);
            waiting.add(id);
            return true;
        }

        boolean cancel(int id) {
            Rider r = riders.get(id);
            if (r == null || r.status != WAITING) return false;
            waiting.remove(id);
            r.status = CANCELLED;
            return true;
        }

        // Returns the nearest free spot, the smaller id on a tie, or null; O(log n) time, O(1) space.
        private Spot nearest(int pos) {
            Spot right = free.ceiling(new Spot(pos, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
            Spot edge = free.lower(new Spot(pos, Integer.MIN_VALUE));
            // The lower call returns the largest id at the left position, so one more call finds the smallest id there.
            Spot left = edge == null ? null : free.ceiling(new Spot(edge.pos(), Integer.MIN_VALUE));
            if (left == null) return right;
            if (right == null) return left;
            long dl = (long) pos - left.pos(), dr = (long) right.pos() - pos;
            if (dl != dr) return dl < dr ? left : right;
            return left.id() <= right.id() ? left : right;
        }

        /**
         * Pairs the longest-waiting rider with the nearest free driver.
         * Time: O(log n).
         * Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: both sides are found before the first write.
         */
        int[] match() {
            if (waiting.isEmpty() || free.isEmpty()) return new int[0];
            int rid = waiting.iterator().next();
            Rider r = riders.get(rid);
            Spot s = nearest(r.pos);
            Driver d = drivers.get(s.id());
            waiting.remove(rid);
            takeSpot(s.id(), d);
            r.status = RIDING; r.driver = s.id();
            d.status = RIDING;
            return new int[] {rid, s.id()};
        }

        boolean finish(int id) {
            Rider r = riders.get(id);
            if (r == null || r.status != RIDING) return false;
            r.status = DONE;
            markFree(r.driver, drivers.get(r.driver), r.dest);
            return true;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem text.
        RideDesk a = new RideDesk();
        boolean ok = a.addDriver(1, 10) && a.addDriver(2, 20) && a.request(100, 15, 40) && a.request(101, 30, 5);
        if (!ok) throw new AssertionError("e1 setup");
        if (!Arrays.equals(a.match(), new int[] {100, 1}) || !Arrays.equals(a.match(), new int[] {101, 2})) throw new AssertionError("e1 match");
        if (a.match().length != 0 || !a.finish(100)) throw new AssertionError("e1 finish");
        // Example 2 from the problem text.
        RideDesk b = new RideDesk();
        if (!b.request(7, 5, 9) || b.match().length != 0 || !b.addDriver(3, 100)) throw new AssertionError("e2 setup");
        if (!b.cancel(7) || b.cancel(7) || b.match().length != 0 || b.finish(7)) throw new AssertionError("e2 calls");
        // Random calls checked against linear scans of plain arrays.
        Random rnd = new Random(9204);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            RideDesk s = new RideDesk();
            int D = 5, R = 6;
            int[] dpos = new int[D], dst = new int[D];          // dst: -1 unused, 0 free, 1 riding, 2 offline
            Arrays.fill(dst, -1);
            int[] rpos = new int[R], rdest = new int[R], rst = new int[R], rdrv = new int[R];
            Arrays.fill(rst, -1);                                // -1 unused, 0 waiting, 1 riding, 2 done, 3 cancelled
            List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int op = 0; op < 60; op++) {
                int t = rnd.nextInt(6), id = rnd.nextInt(6);
                if (t == 0) {
                    int i = id % D, p = rnd.nextInt(10);
                    boolean want = dst[i] == -1;
                    if (want) { dst[i] = 0; dpos[i] = p; }
                    if (s.addDriver(i, p) != want) throw new AssertionError("addDriver");
                } else if (t == 1) {
                    int i = id % D;
                    boolean want = dst[i] == 0;
                    if (want) dst[i] = 2;
                    if (s.goOffline(i) != want) throw new AssertionError("goOffline");
                } else if (t == 2) {
                    int p = rnd.nextInt(10), d = rnd.nextInt(10);
                    boolean want = rst[id] == -1;
                    if (want) { rst[id] = 0; rpos[id] = p; rdest[id] = d; order.add(id); }
                    if (s.request(id, p, d) != want) throw new AssertionError("request");
                } else if (t == 3) {
                    boolean want = rst[id] == 0;
                    if (want) { rst[id] = 3; order.remove((Integer) id); }
                    if (s.cancel(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("cancel");
                } else if (t == 4) {
                    int[] want = new int[0];
                    int bestD = -1;
                    if (!order.isEmpty()) {
                        int rid = order.get(0);
                        for (int i = 0; i < D; i++) {
                            if (dst[i] != 0) continue;
                            if (bestD == -1 || Math.abs(dpos[i] - rpos[rid]) < Math.abs(dpos[bestD] - rpos[rid])) bestD = i;
                        }
                        if (bestD != -1) {
                            want = new int[] {rid, bestD};
                            order.remove(0); rst[rid] = 1; rdrv[rid] = bestD; dst[bestD] = 1;
                        }
                    }
                    if (!Arrays.equals(s.match(), want)) throw new AssertionError("match");
                } else {
                    boolean want = rst[id] == 1;
                    if (want) { rst[id] = 2; dst[rdrv[id]] = 0; dpos[rdrv[id]] = rdest[id]; }
                    if (s.finish(id) != want) throw new AssertionError("finish");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
